graph-theory.bigb
= Graph theory
{wiki}

<Graph theory> studies <vertices> and the <edges> connecting them: which <graph paths> exist, how efficiently we can travel, and which connections are indispensable. A road map becomes a <graph> when junctions are <vertices> and road segments are <edges>; a communications network uses routers and links instead. The drawing helps us see connections, but its apparent lengths need not be the costs being optimized. Record travel times or transmission costs separately in a <weighted graph>.

The appeal of <graph theory> is that one model serves many practical questions. <Graph paths> describe routes, <connected components of a graph> identify isolated regions, and <minimum spanning trees> connect all locations cheaply. The <shortest path problem> asks a different question: find the cheapest journey between specified locations. Our four-<vertex> example below makes that distinction concrete and shows how the <Dijkstra algorithm> discovers a route without trying every <graph path>.

= Geometric spanner
{parent=Graph theory}
{title2=$d_G(u,v)\leq t\|u-v\|$}
{wiki}

A geometric spanner is a <weighted graph> on points in <Euclidean space>, with edge weights equal to their <Euclidean distances>, in which every pair has a <graph path> of <length> at most $t$ times its direct distance. Its <stretch factor> is the largest ratio $d_G(u,v)/\|u-v\|$, where $d_G$ is shortest-path distance. A square's four perimeter edges, without diagonals, give stretch factor $\sqrt2$: adjacent points have ratio one, and diagonal pairs have route <length> $2$ instead of $\sqrt2$. The aim is to retain short routes with fewer edges than a <complete graph>. https://people.scs.carleton.ca/~michiel/encyc_geometric_spanners.pdf[Gudmundsson, Narasimhan and Smid, Geometric Spanners, §1]{ref}.

= Graph excess
{parent=Graph theory}
{title2=$\operatorname{exc}(G)=|E(G)|-|V(G)|$}

Here excess means edges minus vertices. For a <connected graph> it is one less than the number of independent cycles. Deleting a leaf and its incident edge, or suppressing a degree-two vertex, preserves excess. This convention differs by one from the cyclomatic number for connected graphs.

= Bicyclic graph
{parent=Graph excess}
{title2=$|E|=|V|+1$}

= Bicyclic
{synonym}

A connected bicyclic graph has two independent cycles, equivalently excess one. Its <2-core> has the same excess and every removed vertex lies in a tree attached to the core. The <graph kernel> captures the finite collection of possible branching structures.

= Labelled bicyclic graph count
{parent=Bicyclic graph}
{title2=$C(n,n+1)=\Theta(n^{n+1})\quad(n\geq4)$}

If $b_k$ counts labelled bicyclic cores on $k$ vertices, the <bicyclic kernel classification> gives $b_k\leq A k!k^2$. Three internally nonempty paths between two branching vertices give $b_k\geq k!\binom{k-3}{2}/12$ for $k\geq5$. Attach a forest with those core vertices as roots to obtain $C(n,n+1)=\sum_k\binom nk b_kkn^{n-k-1}$. After division by $n^{n+1}$ the relevant sum is $n^{-2}\sum_k k^3(n)_k/n^k$. Its upper bound follows from $(n)_k/n^k\leq e^{-k(k-1)/(2n)}$; its lower bound uses $\sqrt n\leq k\leq2\sqrt n$, where this product stays bounded below. The sum is bounded above and below by positive constants. The restriction $n\geq4$ is necessary: $C(3,4)=0$.

= Bicyclic kernel classification
{parent=Bicyclic graph}

The <graph kernel> of a <bicyclic graph> satisfies $\sum_v(\deg v-2)=2$, with all degrees at least three. It therefore has either one degree-four vertex carrying two loops, a <bouquet graph>, or two degree-three vertices. In the latter case connectivity and degree counting give either three parallel edges, the three-edge <theta graph>, or one edge between the vertices and one loop at each, a <dumbbell graph>. All bicyclic cores are subdivisions of these three kernels, subject to simplicity restrictions on loop lengths and parallel paths.

= 2-core of a graph
{parent=Graph theory}
{title2=$C_2(G)$}

= Graph 2-core
{synonym}

= 2-core
{synonym}

The 2-core is the unique largest induced <subgraph> with <minimum degree> at least two. It is obtained by repeatedly deleting vertices of degree zero or one. Every subgraph with <minimum degree> two survives each deletion, which proves maximality and order independence. In a connected graph with a nonempty 2-core, the deleted vertices form rooted trees attached individually to the core vertices.

= Graph kernel
{parent=2-core of a graph}
{title2=$K(G)$}

For a connected graph whose <2-core> contains a vertex of degree at least three, suppress each maximal path with degree-two internal vertices to a single edge, allowing its two endpoints to coincide. The resulting <multigraph>, retaining loops and parallel edges, is its kernel. Suppression preserves <graph excess> and leaves <minimum degree> at least three. A purely cyclic core is a separate unicyclic case; it has no branching vertex to anchor this construction.

= Chordal graph
{parent=Graph theory}
{wiki}

A <chordal graph> is a finite <simple graph> with no <induced cycle> of length at least four. Equivalently, every <cycle in a graph> with at least four <vertices> has a chord: an <edge> joining two nonconsecutive <vertices> of that <graph cycle>. The word "induced" matters: the <induced subgraph> on the chosen <vertices> must contain exactly the cycle's <edges>, with no chords. Triangles are allowed. This definition concerns adjacency, independently of how the <graph> is drawn.

For the square <cycle graph> $C_4$, take <vertex set> $\{a,b,c,d\}$ and set of <edges> $\{ab,bc,cd,da\}$, where $ab$ abbreviates $\{a,b\}$. Its <graph neighbourhoods> are $N(a)=N(c)=\{b,d\}$ and $N(b)=N(d)=\{a,c\}$. The square is an <induced cycle>, so $C_4$ is not a <chordal graph>. Add the single <edge> $ac$: now $N(a)=\{b,c,d\}$, $N(b)=\{a,c\}$, $N(c)=\{a,b,d\}$, and $N(d)=\{a,c\}$. The only possible four-<vertex> <graph cycle> has chord $ac$, so this new <graph> is a <chordal graph>. Its <maximal cliques> are exactly $\{a,b,c\}$ and $\{a,c,d\}$.

\Image[chordal-perfect-elimination-square-cliques.png]
{title=An <induced cycle> loses its obstruction when one diagonal is added; the resulting <maximal cliques> form a <junction tree>}

Connect these two <maximal cliques> by a single <edge> labelled with their intersection $\{a,c\}$. The result is a <junction tree>, also called a clique tree: its nodes are <maximal cliques>, and the nodes containing any fixed original <vertex> induce a <connected graph> inside the <tree>. Here $a,c$ occur in both nodes, while $b,d$ each occur in just one. This is the <running intersection property>. More generally, a nonempty <connected graph> is a <chordal graph> exactly when its <maximal cliques> admit such a <tree>; a disconnected <chordal graph> can be represented by one such <tree> per <connected component of a graph>.<An Introduction to Chordal Graphs and Clique Trees by Jean R. S. Blair and others>[§3.1–3.2]{ref}

A <chordal graph> need not be a <planar graph>: the <complete graph> $K_5$ is a <chordal graph>, since every pair of distinct <vertices> is adjacent, but its ten <edges> violate the <planar graph edge bound> $|E|\leq3|V|-6=9$. Conversely, even a plane drawing whose bounded faces are triangles can hide an <induced cycle>: a <wheel graph> with a four-<vertex> rim and a central <vertex> joined to the whole rim still has the rim as an <induced cycle>. Adding chords to obtain a <chordal graph> is called <triangulation of an undirected graph>; this is an adjacency condition, rather than a requirement about planar faces.

Why is this structure useful? The <maximal cliques> collect local interactions, and the <running intersection property> makes their overlaps sufficient for consistent communication. For <random variables> $x_a,x_b,x_c,x_d$ with finite state sets, a <probabilistic graphical model> of the square-plus-diagonal form can have
$$
p(x_a,x_b,x_c,x_d)=Z^{-1}\psi_{abc}(x_a,x_b,x_c)\psi_{acd}(x_a,x_c,x_d),
$$
where the two nonnegative <clique potentials> have a positive <normalizing constant> $Z$. Eliminating $x_b$ produces the two-variable <junction-tree sum-product message>
$$
m(x_a,x_c)=\sum_{x_b}\psi_{abc}(x_a,x_b,x_c),\qquad Z=\sum_{x_a,x_c,x_d}m(x_a,x_c)\psi_{acd}(x_a,x_c,x_d).
$$
The shared <clique> $\{a,c\}$ carries everything needed from the eliminated side. For $q$ possible states per <random variable>, this example uses two sums over $q^3$ configurations after reusing the message, rather than directly summing over $q^4$ configurations. The example's message is an exact finite sum, with no simulated data. Large <maximal cliques> still make exact inference expensive; <chordal graphs> provide a decomposition, not a universal guarantee of cheap inference.<Recitation 8: Junction Trees by Massachusetts Institute of Technology, Department of Electrical Engineering and Computer Science>[§1; §6]{ref}

= Triangulation of an undirected graph
{parent=Chordal graph}

Triangulation adds fill edges to an undirected <graph> to make it a <chordal graph>. Given an elimination ordering, join all remaining neighbors of each removed vertex. The completed graph has that <perfect elimination ordering>. Fill edges can enlarge the <cliques> used for exact <probabilistic graphical model> inference, without changing the original probability factors; they do not assert new biological relationships.

= Perfect elimination ordering
{parent=Chordal graph}

A <perfect elimination ordering> of a finite <simple graph> $G=(V,E)$ is a <permutation> $(v_1,\ldots,v_n)$ of its <vertices> such that each set of later <graph neighbours>
$$
N_i^+=\{v_j:j>i,\ \{v_i,v_j\}\in E\}
$$
is a <clique>. Thus $v_i$ is a <simplicial vertex (graph theory)> in the remaining <induced subgraph> $G[\{v_i,\ldots,v_n\}]$. Empty and one-<vertex> sets satisfy the <clique> condition automatically. The order here is the order of deletion: $v_1$ goes first.

The characterization is powerful: a finite <simple graph> is a <chordal graph> if and only if it has a <perfect elimination ordering>. For one proof outline, the earliest <vertex> of an <induced cycle> of length at least four would have two later <graph neighbours> on that <graph cycle> that are not adjacent, contradicting the <clique> condition. Conversely, every nonempty <chordal graph> has a <simplicial vertex (graph theory)>, and every <induced subgraph> of a <chordal graph> is a <chordal graph>; repeatedly deleting such a <vertex> supplies the ordering.<An Introduction to Chordal Graphs and Clique Trees by Jean R. S. Blair and others>[§2.2; Theorem 2.2]{ref}

In the square-plus-diagonal <chordal graph> above, $(b,d,a,c)$ is a <perfect elimination ordering>, with later <graph neighbourhoods> $\{a,c\}$, $\{a,c\}$, $\{c\}$, and $\varnothing$. Starting instead with $a$ fails: its <graph neighbours> $b,c,d$ do not form a <clique>, because $bd$ is absent. Thus even a <chordal graph> can have a poor elimination ordering.

For a larger example, join three triangles along shared <edges>, using
$$
V=\{a,b,c,d,e\},\qquad E=\{ab,ac,bc,bd,cd,ce,de\}.
$$
The exact <graph neighbourhoods> are $N(a)=\{b,c\}$, $N(b)=\{a,c,d\}$, $N(c)=\{a,b,d,e\}$, $N(d)=\{b,c,e\}$, and $N(e)=\{c,d\}$. The ordering $(a,b,c,d,e)$ has later <graph neighbourhoods> $\{b,c\}$, $\{c,d\}$, $\{d,e\}$, $\{e\}$, and $\varnothing$, all <cliques>; this certifies that the <graph> is a <chordal graph> without enumerating its <graph cycles>. Its <maximal cliques> $\{a,b,c\}$, $\{b,c,d\}$, $\{c,d,e\}$ form a <junction tree> in that order, with intersections $\{b,c\}$ and $\{c,d\}$.

\Image[chordal-perfect-elimination-triangle-chain.png]
{title=A <perfect elimination ordering> removes three overlapping triangles without <fill-in>; choosing the central <vertex> first adds three <edges>}

To find an ordering, <maximum cardinality search> starts with all scores zero, repeatedly selects an unselected <vertex> of largest score, and increases each unselected <graph neighbour>'s score by one. Reverse the selection order to obtain a candidate <perfect elimination ordering>. For the three-triangle <graph>, one valid selection sequence is $(e,d,c,b,a)$, with scores at selection $0,1,2,2,2$; reversing it gives the displayed deletion order. On a <chordal graph> every tie choice works. On an arbitrary <graph>, test the resulting later <graph neighbourhoods> for the <clique> condition to recognize <chordal graphs>. Suitable <adjacency lists>, score buckets and a linear-time verification method give $O(|V|+|E|)$ recognition.<Chordal Graphs and Semidefinite Optimization by Lieven Vandenberghe and others>[§4.1–4.2; Algorithm 4.2]{ref}

The deletion rule also explains <sparse Gaussian elimination>. For a symmetric <positive-definite matrix> whose <matrix graph> is $G$, eliminating pivot $v$ applies the <Schur complement> update
$$
a_{uw}\longleftarrow a_{uw}-\frac{a_{uv}a_{vw}}{a_{vv}}.
$$
Only pairs of remaining <graph neighbours> of $v$ can acquire new off-diagonal entries. A <perfect elimination ordering> already has all those <edges>, so <Cholesky decomposition> creates no symbolic <fill-in>. Positive definiteness ensures nonzero pivots in exact arithmetic. Special numerical cancellation can suppress predicted entries, and floating-point rounding affects their values.<Chordal Graphs and Semidefinite Optimization by Lieven Vandenberghe and others>[§9.1; equation 9.2; Theorem 9.1]{ref} In the three-triangle <graph>, deleting $c$ first instead connects every pair in $\{a,b,d,e\}$ and adds exactly $ad,ae,be$. The <graph> was already a <chordal graph>; the extra <fill-in> comes from the ordering.

One further reward is optimal <graph coloring>. Process a <perfect elimination ordering> backwards and give each <vertex> the smallest positive color absent from its already colored <graph neighbours>. Those <graph neighbours> form a <clique>, so this <greedy algorithm> uses at most the <clique number> $\omega(G)$ colors; any <clique> of that size requires at least that many. In the three-triangle <graph>, coloring $(e,d,c,b,a)$ gives colors $(1,2,3,1,2)$, so both the <chromatic number> and the <clique number> are three. Every <maximal clique> also occurs among $\{v_i\}\cup N_i^+$: take its earliest <vertex> and use maximality. The same ordering therefore supports <graph coloring>, <maximal clique> extraction and sparse computation.

Historically, Donald J. Rose, Robert E. Tarjan and George S. Lueker's 1976 paper connected <graph> elimination to symmetric <sparse matrices> and gave a search-based $O(|V|+|E|)$ <algorithm> for finding a <perfect elimination ordering> when one exists. Its abstract distinguishes a minimal elimination ordering from a minimum one: removing avoidable <fill-in> does not by itself minimize the total number of added <edges>.<Algorithmic Aspects of Vertex Elimination on Graphs by Donald J. Rose and others>[pp. 266–283; abstract]{ref}

= Simplicial vertex
{disambiguate=graph theory}
{parent=Perfect elimination ordering}

A <simplicial vertex (graph theory)> is a <graph vertex> whose <graph neighbourhood> is a <clique>. Every isolated <vertex> and every <leaf of a graph> qualifies. In the three-triangle <chordal graph>, initially $a,e$ qualify, but $b$ only becomes a <simplicial vertex (graph theory)> after deleting $a$. This is a property of adjacency in a <graph>; a <vertex of a simplicial complex> is an element of a different kind of structure.

= Maximum cardinality search
{parent=Perfect elimination ordering}

<Maximum cardinality search> is a <graph> search in which an unselected <vertex>'s score counts its already selected <graph neighbours>. Repeatedly select a <vertex> of maximum score, breaking ties arbitrarily, and update its unselected <graph neighbours>. On a <chordal graph>, the reverse selection order is a <perfect elimination ordering>. On $C_4$, selecting $(a,b,c,d)$ instead yields candidate deletion order $(d,c,b,a)$; $d$'s later <graph neighbours> $a,c$ are not adjacent, so the <clique> check fails.<Chordal Graphs and Semidefinite Optimization by Lieven Vandenberghe and others>[§4.2; Algorithm 4.2]{ref}

= Johnson graph
{parent=Graph theory}
{c}
{title2=$J(n,k)$}
{wiki}

The Johnson graph has the $k$-element subsets of an $n$-element set as vertices, with two vertices adjacent exactly when their intersection has $k-1$ elements. Relabelling the ground set gives graph automorphisms. The <maximal cliques of a Johnson graph> allow reconstruction of those ground-set labels when the two clique sizes are different.

= Distance in a Johnson graph
{parent=Johnson graph}
{title2=$d(A,B)=k-|A\cap B|$}

One edge of a <Johnson graph> replaces one element of a $k$-subset, so it can increase intersection with a fixed target by at most one. Replacing each element outside the target by one missing target element gives a path reaching this lower bound. Equal-distance ordered pairs have equal sizes of intersection, the two differences and the remaining ground set; a <permutation> matching these four regions proves <distance-transitivity>.

= Maximal cliques of a Johnson graph
{parent=Johnson graph}
{title2=$n-k+1\quad\text{and}\quad k+1$}

For $1<k<n-1$, the maximal cliques of $J(n,k)$ are the families containing a fixed $(k-1)$-subset, of size $n-k+1$, and the families contained in a fixed $(k+1)$-subset, of size $k+1$. To see why these exhaust the possibilities, write two adjacent vertices as $D\cup\{a\}$ and $D\cup\{b\}$. A common adjacent vertex either contains $D$ or lies in $D\cup\{a,b\}$. A vertex of the first kind outside that union is not adjacent to a vertex of the second kind omitting an element of $D$, so a clique cannot mix these alternatives. For $J(n,3)$ and $n\ge5$, $n\ne6$, the clique sizes $n-2$ and four distinguish the families containing pairs. Their incidence recovers $J(n,2)$, whose stars of size $n-1$ distinguish the individual ground-set points from its triangular cliques.

= Maximum degree
{parent=Graph theory}
{title2=$\Delta(G)$}

The maximum degree of a finite <graph> is the largest number of edges incident to one vertex. A bound $d$ on degree bounds the number of vertices within distance $k$ of a fixed vertex by $1+d+\cdots+d^k$. Such counting bounds turn a large vertex count into a lower bound on diameter.

= Four-cycle counting by common neighbours
{parent=Graph theory}
{title2=$C_4(G)=\tfrac12\sum_{i<j}\binom{r_{ij}}2$}

For a simple <graph>, let $r_{ij}$ count the common neighbours of vertices $i,j$. Choosing two such neighbours makes a four-cycle, and each cycle is counted twice through its opposite pairs. The displayed identity follows even when extra edges are present, since cycles are counted as subgraphs rather than induced subgraphs. Also $\sum_{i<j}r_{ij}=\sum_v\binom{d(v)}2$. Applying <Cauchy-Schwarz inequality> to degrees and <Jensen inequality> to common-neighbour counts gives extremal edge and cycle bounds.

= Shortest path problem
{parent=Graph theory}
{title2=$\min_{p:s\leadsto t}\sum_{e\in p}w_e$}

The <shortest path problem> asks for a <graph path> from a source <vertex> $s$ to a destination <vertex> $t$ minimizing its total <edge> weight. Here $w_e$ is the cost of traversing <edge> $e$, and costs add along the <graph path>. If no route exists, its shortest-path distance is $\infty$. With nonnegative <edge> weights, deleting <cycles in a graph> from a <walk> never increases its cost, so repetitions cannot improve the optimum.

Consider the <undirected graph> with <vertex set> $V=\{A,B,C,D\}$ and the six <edge> weights
$$
w_{AB}=4,\quad w_{AC}=1,\quad w_{CB}=2,\quad w_{BD}=1,\quad w_{CD}=5,\quad w_{AD}=10.
$$
Each <edge> can be traversed in either direction at the same cost. The direct <graph path> $A,D$ costs $10$; $A,B,D$ costs $4+1=5$; $A,C,D$ costs $1+5=6$. The cheapest <graph path> is $A,C,B,D$, costing $1+2+1=4$. The remaining <graph path> $A,B,C,D$ costs $11$, exhausting the possibilities without repeated <vertices>.

\Image[shortest-path-weights.png]
{title=The <shortest path problem>: three highlighted <edges> cost less than the direct connection}

Thus fewest <edges> and least total cost are different objectives. <Breadth-first search> minimizes the number of <edges>, which suffices when every <edge> has the same positive cost. Unequal nonnegative costs call for the <Dijkstra algorithm>. On a map, weights might be minutes rather than kilometres: a longer-looking route can arrive sooner. This is why a <weighted graph> separates the network's connections from what a particular journey costs.

= Label-setting shortest-path algorithm
{parent=Shortest path problem}

An algorithm that makes each vertex's distance permanent once it is selected. With nonnegative <directed edge> lengths, the <Dijkstra algorithm> selects the unfinalized vertex with least tentative distance; a shorter route through another unfinalized vertex is impossible because its remaining route has at least that next tentative distance. For an all-to-one problem, incoming <directed edges> to the selected vertex are relaxed. Negative lengths can invalidate this finalization argument.

= Bellman inequalities
{c}
{parent=Shortest path problem}
{title2=$d_i\le c_{ij}+d_j$}

The Bellman inequalities turn local <directed edge> checks into a global certificate for the <shortest path problem>. Here the convention is \i[all-to-one]: every journey ends at a fixed <vertex> $r$, and $c_{ij}$ is the cost of the original <directed edge> $i\to j$. Work in a finite <directed graph> with finite real costs. Write $L_i$ for candidate labels, normalized by $L_r=0$, on the <vertices> that can reach $r$. A feasible potential means precisely that these finite labels satisfy
$$
L_i\le c_{ij}+L_j
$$
for every <directed edge> within that set. These labels give <lower bounds> on costs \i[to] $r$. A discovered <directed path> of cost $U_i$ instead gives an <upper bound>. The following constructed <graph> shows both bounds and the exact distances between them; its weights are illustrative costs, not measured travel data.

\Image[edge-relaxation-certificate.png]
{title=<Bellman inequalities> and explicit <directed paths> certify distances to $r$ from opposite sides}

The arrows in the picture point toward the destination, whereas <edge relaxation> propagates an improved label in the opposite direction: an improved $d_j$ can lower $d_i$. In the pictured <graph>, the <directed paths> from $a$ to $r$ cost $7$, $2+3=5$, and $2-2+4=4$. Thus $\delta(a,r)=4$. The candidate $L=(3,1,3,0)$ in <vertex> order $(a,b,c,r)$ is feasible, but underestimates the exact distances $(4,2,4,0)$. Merely satisfying the <Bellman inequalities> does not make a candidate exact.

Why do they give <lower bounds>? Along any <directed path> $i=v_0\to v_1\to\cdots\to v_k=r$, successive substitution gives
$$
L_i\le\sum_{\ell=0}^{k-1}c_{v_\ell v_{\ell+1}}+L_r=\sum_{\ell=0}^{k-1}c_{v_\ell v_{\ell+1}}.
$$
The same calculation works for a <directed walk>, with repeated <vertices> allowed. Taking the <infimum> of route costs gives $L_i\le\delta(i,r)$. A discovered route gives $\delta(i,r)\le U_i$. If the bounds meet, $L_i=U_i$, they certify an optimum without enumerating routes. Equivalently, check the <Bellman inequalities> for the proposed distances and exhibit <directed paths> realizing them. The authored <Bellman-Ford algorithm> implementation checks the corresponding single-source inequalities and equality on recorded route <edges>; reversing every <directed edge> gives this all-to-one convention.https://raw.githubusercontent.com/kevin-wayne/algs4/master/src/main/java/edu/princeton/cs/algs4/BellmanFordSP.java[Sedgewick and Wayne, BellmanFordSP.java, optimality checks]{ref}

For the exact labels in the picture, the slack $c_{ij}+d_j-d_i$ is respectively $0,3,1,0,0$ on $a\to b$, $a\to r$, $b\to r$, $b\to c$, $c\to r$. Zero slack means equality; the zero-slack <directed path> $a\to b\to c\to r$ realizes the cost $4$. This is the useful beauty of the certificate: a few local <inequalities> rule out every cheaper journey, while one explicit journey attains the bound.

Keep unreachable <vertices> separate: $\delta(i,r)=\infty$ when no <directed path> to $r$ exists, and a finite <lower bound> alone cannot certify that fact. Extended labels can use $\infty+c=\infty$ for finite $c$, but never compute a slack as $\infty-\infty$. If $i$ can reach a negative-cost <directed cycle> and then reach $r$, repeatedly traversing the <directed cycle> makes route costs unbounded below: their <infimum> is $-\infty$, with no finite shortest <directed walk>. A finite feasible potential on such a <directed cycle> is impossible, since summing its <Bellman inequalities> would give $0\le\sum c_{ij}<0$. A negative-cost <directed cycle> that cannot reach $r$ does not affect distances to $r$. These are statements about unrestricted <directed walks>; requiring a <directed path> with no repeated <vertices> is a different optimization problem when negative-cost <directed cycles> exist.https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/6-006-introduction-to-algorithms-fall-2011/5edb13fb34cbe7f4cdccc13d131772e7_MIT6_006F11_lec17.pdf[MIT 6.006, Lecture 17, pp. 1 and 5: relevant negative cycles and simple paths]{ref}

For the single-source convention, the same original <directed edge> $u\to v$ instead gives $L(v)\le L(u)+w_{uv}$ with $L(s)=0$, a <lower bound> on distance \i[from] $s$. The endpoint carrying the label on the left therefore changes with the direction of the problem.

= Label-correcting shortest-path algorithm
{parent=Shortest path problem}

An algorithm whose tentative distance labels may be revised repeatedly until all relevant <Bellman inequalities> hold. <Bellman-Ford algorithm> uses this approach: a label is an upper bound supplied by a discovered <directed path>, and relaxing an <directed edge> can replace it with a shorter discovered <directed path>. A finite label is not declared permanent merely because it has been processed.

= Bellman-Ford algorithm
{c}
{parent=Shortest path problem}

For an all-to-one <shortest path problem> with root $r$, initialize $d_r^{(0)}=0$ and all other labels to infinity. Set $d_i^{(k)}=\min(d_i^{(k-1)},\min_{(i,j)\in A}\{c_{ij}+d_j^{(k-1)}\})$, keeping the root at zero. Induction identifies the labels with the shortest <directed paths> using at most $k$ <directed edges>. Without a reachable negative <directed cycle>, $|V|-1$ rounds suffice after removing <directed cycles> from a shortest walk. In-place or queue variants repeatedly relax incoming <directed edges> to an improved label. Labels may decrease multiple times, so this is a <label-correcting shortest-path algorithm>.

= Dijkstra algorithm
{c}
{parent=Shortest path problem}
{wiki=Dijkstra's_algorithm}
{title2=$d(v)=\operatorname{dist}(s,v)$}

= Dijkstra's algorithm
{c}
{synonym}

The <Dijkstra algorithm> solves the single-source <shortest path problem> in a finite <graph> with nonnegative <edge> weights, finding distances to all reachable <vertices>. Start with <tentative distance> $d(A)=0$ and $d(v)=\infty$ elsewhere. Repeatedly choose the unfinalized <vertex> with least <tentative distance>, declare that label permanent, and perform <edge relaxation> on its outgoing <edges>. For an <undirected graph>, inspect both directions. A finite <tentative distance> always records the cost of a discovered <graph path>, but can improve before finalization.

In our example, finalizing $A$ discovers $C$ at $1$, $B$ at $4$, and $D$ at $10$. Choose $C$ next: <edge relaxation> through $C$ lowers $B$ to $1+2=3$ and $D$ to $1+5=6$. Choose $B$: <edge relaxation> through $B$ lowers $D$ to $3+1=4$. Finally choose $D$. The permanent distances are therefore $A:0$, $C:1$, $B:3$, $D:4$; the evolving <tentative distances> were $B:4\to3$ and $D:10\to6\to4$.

\Image[dijkstra-distance-frontier.png]
{title=<Tentative distances> fall until the <Dijkstra algorithm> makes each label permanent}

Store a <shortest-path predecessor> whenever <edge relaxation> improves a label. The final table contains enough information to reconstruct routes, rather than merely their costs:

|| <Vertex>
|| Distance from $A$
|| <Shortest-path predecessor>
|| Finalization order

| $A$
| $0$
| None: source
| $1$

| $C$
| $1$
| $A$
| $2$

| $B$
| $3$
| $C$
| $3$

| $D$
| $4$
| $B$
| $4$

Following <shortest-path predecessors> backwards gives $D,B,C,A$; reversing the order gives $A,C,B,D$. If only this destination matters, stop when $D$ is finalized. Stopping when $D$ is first discovered would incorrectly return $10$. If the smallest remaining <tentative distance> is $\infty$, all remaining <vertices> are unreachable.

Why is the permanent choice safe? Suppose the selected <vertex> $u$ had a cheaper <graph path>. Let $y$ be the first unfinalized <vertex> on that <graph path>. Its preceding <vertex> was finalized, so <edge relaxation> has already offered $y$ a label no larger than that prefix's cost. Nonnegative remaining <edge> weights make the whole cost at least this prefix's cost; selection gives $d(u)\le d(y)$. A cheaper <graph path> would contradict these comparisons. Zero weights are allowed; ties can be resolved arbitrarily. This is the reason the <label-setting shortest-path algorithm> works, rather than a lucky feature of the drawing.

Negative weights break that reasoning. In a <directed graph> with $s\to u$ costing $2$, $s\to v$ costing $5$, and $v\to u$ costing $-4$, the <Dijkstra algorithm> would finalize $u$ at $2$ before discovering the cheaper cost $1$. The <Bellman-Ford algorithm> handles negative <edge> weights when no relevant negative <directed cycle> exists.

With an <adjacency list> and an indexed <binary heap> implementing a <priority queue>, the <time complexity> is $O((|V|+|E|)\log |V|)$ for $|V|\ge2$, using <Big O notation>. Each <vertex> is extracted at most once, each <edge> is inspected once per direction, and each successful <edge relaxation> decreases a heap key in $O(\log |V|)$ time. This assumes constant-cost arithmetic and comparisons; exact computation with very large weights adds their representation cost. The queue uses $O(|V|)$ space beyond the <graph>. Simply scanning all remaining <vertices> gives $O(|V|^2+|E|)$ instead.https://algs4.cs.princeton.edu/44sp/DijkstraSP.java.html[Princeton, DijkstraSP implementation]{ref}https://algs4.cs.princeton.edu/code/javadoc/edu/princeton/cs/algs4/IndexMinPQ.html[Princeton, indexed binary heap operations]{ref}

The 1959 paper presents the <shortest path problem> as its second problem, after the <minimum spanning tree> problem, and grows known shortest routes in increasing distance order.https://www.cs.yale.edu/homes/lans/readings/routing/dijkstra-routing-1959.pdf[Dijkstra, A note on two problems in connexion with graphs, pp. 269–271]{ref} In his later recollection, Dijkstra dated the shortest-route discovery to 1956: a demonstration for the ARMAC computer used a simplified Dutch railway map and printed the route between towns. A practical request became an enduring <algorithm> because the same distance rule applies to any suitable <graph>.https://garfield.library.upenn.edu/classics1983/A1983QA19900001.pdf[Dijkstra's recollection, published in 1983]{ref}

= Tentative distance
{parent=Dijkstra algorithm}
{title2=$d(v)$}

A tentative distance $d(v)$ is the cheapest cost so far recorded by a <shortest path problem> <algorithm>. For the single-source convention, initialize $d(s)=0$ and $d(v)=\infty$ elsewhere. Every finite label is the cost of a discovered route from $s$ to $v$, so
$$
\delta(s,v)\le d(v).
$$
It is an <upper bound> supplied by an actual route, not a guess that might be too low. The <loop invariant> follows directly from <edge relaxation>: extending a discovered route to $u$ by $u\to v$ supplies another discovered route. The usual label initialization and this safety property are stated and proved in the authored MIT notes.https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/6-006-introduction-to-algorithms-fall-2011/6277a1f06100c26a7ff21031af6757b5_MIT6_006F11_lec16.pdf[MIT 6.006, Lecture 16, p. 1: relaxation safety]{ref}

In the trace under <edge relaxation>, $d(t)=10$ first records the direct <directed path> $s\to t$. Later $d(t)=8$ records $s\to a\to t$, and finally $d(t)=3$ records $s\to b\to a\to t$. Each label is a usable route cost, but the first two are not shortest. An infinite <tentative distance> means no route has yet been discovered; only a completed search establishes unreachability. The isolated <vertex> $z$ in that example remains at $\infty$.

In a <directed graph>, the discovered route is formally a <directed walk>, which may repeat <vertices>; in the pictured acyclic <graph> it is always a <directed path>. Negative-cost <directed cycles> can keep supplying cheaper <directed walks> without a finite optimum. In the all-to-one convention, the same meaning applies to discovered routes \i[from] $i$ \i[to] $r$. Do not confuse either version with the <lower bounds> supplied by feasible potentials in the <Bellman inequalities>: one records something achievable, the other rules out anything cheaper.

= Edge relaxation
{parent=Dijkstra algorithm}
{title2=$d(v)\gets\min\{d(v),d(u)+w_{uv}\}$}

Edge relaxation tests whether extending a recorded route by one <directed edge> improves a <tentative distance>. For a single source $s$ and an original <directed edge> $u\to v$ of weight $w_{uv}$, form the candidate $q=d(u)+w_{uv}$. If $d(u)$ is finite and $q<d(v)$, replace $d(v)$ by $q$ and record the <shortest-path predecessor> $\pi(v)=u$. Otherwise leave both unchanged. The <minimum> formula in the header gives the resulting label in either case. The authored implementation uses this strict comparison and records the incoming <directed edge>.https://raw.githubusercontent.com/kevin-wayne/algs4/master/src/main/java/edu/princeton/cs/algs4/DijkstraSP.java[Sedgewick and Wayne, DijkstraSP.java, relax method]{ref}

\Image[edge-relaxation-trace.png]
{title=<Edge relaxation> lowers discovered-route costs, including across a negative-weight <directed edge>}

The table lists labels \i[after] each specified operation, keeping $d(s)=0$ throughout this constructed acyclic <graph>. Relaxing $a\to t$ first offers $6+2=8$. Relaxing $b\to a$ then offers $2-1=1$, lowering $a$ from $6$ to $1$; revisiting $a\to t$ now offers $1+2=3$. Finally $b\to t$ offers $2+7=9$, so the label $3$ stays. Changing a label can make a previously checked outgoing <directed edge> worth checking again. This trace illustrates the operation itself, with a deliberately specified order, rather than a run of the <Dijkstra algorithm>.

A single <edge relaxation> does not require nonnegative weights: its candidate is the cost of a real extended <directed walk>, even when the last <directed edge> has negative weight. Nonnegative weights are needed by the <Dijkstra algorithm> to make its selected minimum <tentative distance> permanent. The <Bellman-Ford algorithm> permits negative weights and revisits labels; its authored implementation also checks for reachable negative-cost <directed cycles>.https://raw.githubusercontent.com/kevin-wayne/algs4/master/src/main/java/edu/princeton/cs/algs4/DijkstraSP.java[Sedgewick and Wayne, DijkstraSP.java, nonnegative-weight precondition]{ref}https://raw.githubusercontent.com/kevin-wayne/algs4/master/src/main/java/edu/princeton/cs/algs4/BellmanFordSP.java[Sedgewick and Wayne, BellmanFordSP.java, signed weights and cycle detection]{ref}

For the all-to-one convention of the <Bellman inequalities>, relax the same original <directed edge> $i\to j$ by $d_i\gets\min\{d_i,c_{ij}+d_j\}$. This prepends $i\to j$ to a discovered route from $j$ to $r$, and improves the tail label $d_i$, not the head label $d_j$. Skip the operation when $d_j=\infty$. In exact arithmetic, a full pass over all relevant <directed edges> with no improvement establishes all their <Bellman inequalities>; combined with the discovered-route <upper bounds>, this certifies finite distances. One unsuccessful operation establishes only that this particular candidate did not improve its label at that moment.

= Shortest-path predecessor
{parent=Dijkstra algorithm}
{title2=$\pi(v)$}

A shortest-path predecessor $\pi(v)$ records the previous <vertex> on a chosen route from source $s$ to $v$. When <edge relaxation> strictly improves $d(v)$ through $u\to v$, set $\pi(v)=u$. For a <multigraph>, store the actual <directed edge>, since two <edges> with the same endpoints can have different weights. Initialize all <vertices>, including the source, without a <shortest-path predecessor>. The authored implementation reconstructs a route by following these incoming <edges> backwards, then reversing their order.https://raw.githubusercontent.com/kevin-wayne/algs4/master/src/main/java/edu/princeton/cs/algs4/DijkstraSP.java[Sedgewick and Wayne, DijkstraSP.java, edgeTo and pathTo]{ref}

At the end of the <edge relaxation> trace, $\pi(t)=a$, $\pi(a)=b$, and $\pi(b)=s$. Following them gives $t,a,b,s$, so the forward <directed path> is $s\to b\to a\to t$, of cost $2-1+2=3$. This makes the distance useful: a number tells us the best cost, whereas the <shortest-path predecessors> tell us which connections to take. In the <Dijkstra algorithm>, reconstruct a destination's shortest route after its label becomes permanent. In a <label-correcting shortest-path algorithm>, wait for convergence and the appropriate negative-cost <directed cycle> check. During correction, a predecessor's label may have changed since its child's last update: immediately after $b\to a$ in the trace, the current chain to $t$ costs $3$ although $d(t)$ still records the earlier discovered route of cost $8$.

Strict improvement is a useful guard against <directed cycles> in the recorded pointers. Consider $s\to a$ and $s\to b$ of cost zero, followed by $a\to b$ and $b\to a$, also of cost zero. After the first two <edge relaxations>, both labels are zero and both <shortest-path predecessors> are $s$. If equality also allows pointer replacement, the last two operations can set $\pi(b)=a$ and $\pi(a)=b$: following pointers loops forever despite correct distance labels. Keeping the old pointer on ties prevents this example; choosing among equal-cost routes requires a separate rule that preserves a route to the source. Strict improvement alone does not protect against reachable negative-cost <directed cycles>, which can force endless decreases. Equality on recorded <edges> plus the <Bellman inequalities> certifies route costs only when the pointer chains actually terminate at the source.

For all-to-one distances to $r$, record a next <vertex> $j$ whenever relaxing $i\to j$ improves $d_i$. Follow these successors forward in the original <directed graph> to reach $r$. They are <shortest-path predecessors> when viewed in the reversed <directed graph>; keeping the direction convention explicit prevents returning a route whose arrows point the wrong way.

= Graph property
{parent=Graph theory}

A <graph> property is a class of finite <graphs> closed under isomorphism. Its labelled order-$n$ slice consists of members on <vertex> set $[n]$. Closure under taking arbitrary subgraphs and closure under taking only induced subgraphs lead respectively to <subgraph-closed graph properties> and <hereditary graph properties>.

= Common-center graph property
{parent=Graph property}
{title2=$\mathrm{STAR}_n(G)$}

= STAR graph property
{c}
{synonym}

All edges of a simple undirected <graph> have at least one common endpoint. The empty <graph> is included when there is at least one vertex, and isolated vertices are permitted. This is the star predicate with isolated vertices allowed; a connected <star graph> is a special case. Its <decision-tree depth> is the full number $\binom n2$ of edge bits for $n\geq3$.

= Certificates for the common-center graph property
{parent=Common-center graph property}
{title2=$C_1=\binom{n-1}{2},\quad C_0=3$}

For $n\geq3$, a full <star graph> requires every nonincident edge to be certified absent, and fixing those bits suffices. Rejection is certified by at most three present edges with empty common intersection; a triangle needs all three. Hence <query certificate complexity> is $\max\{3,\binom{n-1}{2}\}=\Theta(n^2)$ for this property.

= Alternating count of common-center graphs
{parent=Common-center graph property}
{title2=$\sum_G(-1)^{|E(G)|}\mathrm{STAR}_n(G)=(n-1)(n-2)/2$}

For $n\geq3$, count the empty <graph> once, all one-edge <graphs> once, and each larger accepted <graph> under its unique center. The latter weighted contribution is $n(n-2)$, giving $1-\binom n2+n(n-2)$. Its nonzero value proves evasiveness by the <alternating-sum criterion for decision-tree evasiveness>.

= Speed of a graph property
{parent=Graph property}
{title2=$|\mathcal P_n|$}

The speed counts the labelled <graphs> with a given property on <vertex> set $[n]$. For proper unbounded <hereditary graph properties>, its base-two logarithm has leading term $(1-1/r)\binom n2$, where $r$ is the property's colouring number.

= Hereditary graph property
{parent=Graph property}

A hereditary <graph> property is closed under taking <induced subgraphs>. It can be described by forbidden induced <graphs>. Unlike subgraph closure, this condition allows <edges> to be essential to membership. Its <colouring number of a hereditary graph property> governs its quadratic enumeration rate.

= Colouring number of a hereditary graph property
{parent=Hereditary graph property}
{title2=$r(\mathcal P)=\max\{a:\mathcal C(a,b)\subseteq\mathcal P\text{ for some }b\}$}

= Coloring number of a hereditary graph property
{synonym}

This parameter measures the largest completely unrestricted clique-independent partition class contained in the property. It is zero for bounded-order hereditary classes and infinite for all <graphs>. For proper unbounded classes it is a positive integer; it differs from the degeneracy-based colouring number of an individual <graph>.

= Hereditary graph enumeration theorem
{parent=Colouring number of a hereditary graph property}
{title2=$|\mathcal P_n|=2^{(1-1/r(\mathcal P)+o(1))\binom n2}$}

= Alekseev-Bollobás-Thomason theorem
{c}
{synonym}

For a proper hereditary <graph> property with unbounded orders, the colouring number determines the leading labelled speed. The lower bound comes from one contained <clique-independent partition class>. For the upper bound, forbid one induced <graph> from each next-size partition type and use the <induced regularity template lemma>. Its intermediate-pair <graph> is clique-free, and sparse or almost-complete pairs have negligible entropy. Bounded-order properties have colouring number zero and are outside this formula.

= Clique-independent partition class
{parent=Hereditary graph property}
{title2=$\mathcal C(a,b)$}

= C(a,b) graph class
{c}
{synonym}

Graphs in $\mathcal C(a,b)$ admit a partition into $a$ total classes, of which $b$ are designated <cliques> and $a-b$ are designated independent sets. Cross <edges> are arbitrary and empty classes are allowed. Balanced cross-edge choices give quadratic labelled speed coefficient $1-1/a$. Comparing this count with every $(a+1)$-class type shows that the hereditary colouring number of this class is exactly $a$.

= Subgraph-closed graph property
{parent=Graph property}

= Monotone graph property in extremal enumeration
{synonym}

Membership is preserved under deleting <vertices> and <edges>. This is the decreasing meaning of monotonicity in extremal <graph> enumeration; it differs from an increasing <monotone graph property> used for random-graph thresholds. For a proper unbounded class, its least excluded chromatic number determines the leading quadratic logarithm of its labelled speed.

= Enumeration of subgraph-closed graph properties
{parent=Subgraph-closed graph property}
{title2=$\log_2|\mathcal Q_n|=(1-1/r+o(1))\binom n2$}

Here $r+1$ is the least chromatic number of an excluded <graph>, for a proper unbounded subgraph-closed property. All subgraphs of a balanced <Turan graph> give the lower bound. For the upper bound, a regularity reduced <graph> is clique-free, so its dense pairs have the <Turan theorem> <edge> bound; sparse pairs contribute only small binary entropy and the bounded partition description costs only a linear number of bits.

= Orientation of a graph
{parent=Graph theory}

A choice of direction for each edge of an undirected <graph>, producing a <directed graph>.

= Outdegree orientation criterion
{parent=Orientation of a graph}
{title2=$d(U)\geq k|U|$}

A finite undirected <graph> can be oriented with <outdegree> at least an integer $k\geq0$ at every vertex if and only if every vertex subset $U$ is incident to at least $k|U|$ distinct edges. The <max-flow min-cut theorem> and <integral max-flow theorem> prove sufficiency by assigning distinct edges to vertices through an incidence <flow network>; each assigned edge is oriented away from that vertex. Necessity follows by counting outgoing edges from $U$.

= Graph intersection
{parent=Graph theory}
{title2=$E(F\cap G)=E(F)\cap E(G)$}

For two <graphs> on the same <vertex set>, their graph intersection retains exactly the <edges> belonging to both.

= Kneser graph
{c}
{parent=Graph theory}
{title2=$KG(n,k)$}
{wiki}

The Kneser graph has as <vertices> the $k$-element subsets of $[n]$, with an <edge> between two exactly when they are disjoint. A <graph colouring> of this <graph> partitions the $k$-sets into <intersecting families>.

= Lovász theorem on Kneser graphs
{c}
{parent=Kneser graph}
{title2=$\chi(KG(n,k))=n-2k+2$}

= Kneser conjecture
{c}
{synonym}

For integers $n\geq2k\geq2$, the <chromatic number> of the <Kneser graph> is $n-2k+2$. The upper bound colours by the least element among the first $n-2k+1$ elements, with one final colour for all other sets. The <Gale hemisphere lemma> and the <Lusternik-Schnirelmann-Borsuk theorem> give the matching lower bound.

= Cut vertex
{parent=Graph theory}
{title2=$k(G-v)>k(G)$}

= Articulation point
{synonym}

A <cut vertex>, also called an <articulation point>, is a <vertex> $v$ of an <undirected graph> whose deletion increases the number of <connected components of a graph>. Write $k(G)$ for that count, including isolated <vertices>, and $G-v$ for the <graph> obtained by removing $v$ and every incident <edge>. Then the test is $k(G-v)>k(G)$, even when the original <graph> is disconnected. By contrast, deleting a <bridge in a graph> removes just one <edge> and keeps both endpoint <vertices>. These are different failure models. https://algs4.cs.princeton.edu/41graph/[Sedgewick and Wayne, Algorithms, §4.1: bridges and articulation points]{ref}

First take two <triangles in a graph>, $ABC$ and $ADE$, sharing only the <vertex> $A$. This <simple graph> has
$$
V=\{A,B,C,D,E\},\qquad E=\{AB,AC,BC,AD,AE,DE\}.
$$
Here $XY$ abbreviates the <unordered pair> $\{X,Y\}$. Removing $A$ leaves the two <connected components of a graph> $\{B,C\}$ and $\{D,E\}$, so $k(G-A)=2>1=k(G)$. Removing any other <vertex> leaves a <connected graph>. Thus $A$ is the only <cut vertex>. Every <edge> lies on a <triangle in a graph>, so deleting any single <edge> still leaves a <graph path> between its endpoints along the other two <edges>. There are no <bridges in a graph>.

\Image[articulation-without-bridges.png]
{title=Two <triangles in a graph> share a <cut vertex> $A$, although no <edge> is a <bridge in a graph>}

The generator computes the <adjacency lists> and checks every <vertex> deletion, giving the following complete table:

|| Deleted <vertex> $v$
|| Original <graph neighbours> of $v$
|| $k(G-v)$

| $A$
| $B,C,D,E$
| $2$

| $B$
| $A,C$
| $1$

| $C$
| $A,B$
| $1$

| $D$
| $A,E$
| $1$

| $E$
| $A,D$
| $1$

Now take two disjoint <triangles in a graph>, $ABC$ and $DEF$, joined by the <edge> $CD$:
$$
V=\{A,B,C,D,E,F\},\qquad E=\{AB,AC,BC,CD,DE,DF,EF\}.
$$
Deleting only $CD$ preserves all six <vertices> and leaves <connected components of a graph> $\{A,B,C\}$ and $\{D,E,F\}$: $CD$ is the only <bridge in a graph>. Deleting $C$ also removes $AC$ and $BC$, leaving <connected components of a graph> $\{A,B\}$ and $\{D,E,F\}$. Deleting $D$ instead leaves $\{A,B,C\}$ and $\{E,F\}$. Thus $C,D$ are exactly the <cut vertices>.

\Image[graph-articulation-versus-bridge.png]
{title=<Edge> deletion keeps its endpoints; <cut vertex> deletion removes all incident <edges>}

Again the <adjacency lists> and all <vertex> deletion counts are computed directly:

|| Deleted <vertex> $v$
|| Original <graph neighbours> of $v$
|| $k(G-v)$

| $A$
| $B,C$
| $1$

| $B$
| $A,C$
| $1$

| $C$
| $A,B,D$
| $2$

| $D$
| $C,E,F$
| $2$

| $E$
| $D,F$
| $1$

| $F$
| $D,E$
| $1$

For a disconnected example, adjoin an isolated <vertex> $Z$ to the first <graph>. Now $k(G)=2$, deleting $A$ gives $k(G-A)=3$, and deleting $Z$ gives $k(G-Z)=1$. The original <graph> was already disconnected, but $A$ is still a <cut vertex>; $Z$ is not. The empty <graph> has count zero, so deleting the only <vertex> of a one-<vertex> <graph> does not create a <cut vertex>. Nor is either endpoint of a single isolated <edge> a <cut vertex>, although that <edge> is a <bridge in a graph>.

In a communications network, a <cut vertex> models a router whose failure separates surviving routers. The shared-$A$ example shows why having an alternative <graph path> around each individual <edge> does not protect against failure of the common router. An additional <edge> from $B$ to $D$ removes this particular vulnerability: after deleting $A$, $B,D$ still join the two sides. A <bridge in a graph> instead models a vulnerable link. This distinction tells us whether to add another cable between existing devices or a route avoiding an indispensable device; it describes <connectivity (graph theory)>, rather than traffic capacity or failure probabilities.

The beautiful structural consequence is that a <graph> splits into <biconnected components> joined at <cut vertices>. Within each nontrivial robust piece, a single <vertex> failure cannot separate the other <vertices>; between pieces, the shared <cut vertices> reveal the bottlenecks. Also, a <Hamiltonian> <graph> has no <cut vertex>: deleting any <vertex> from its <Hamilton cycle> leaves a <Hamiltonian path> through all remaining <vertices>.

Historically, Tarjan's June 1972 paper developed <depth-first search> as a tool for finding <biconnected components> in time linear in the numbers of <vertices> and <edges>. https://epubs.siam.org/doi/10.1137/0201010[Robert Tarjan, Depth-First Search and Linear Graph Algorithms, SIAM Journal on Computing 1(2), 146–160 (1972)]{ref} In his 1983 recollection, Tarjan credited Hopcroft with the original linear-time <biconnected component> <algorithm> during their collaboration beginning in summer 1970, and described the published version as a refinement. The historical lesson is how a carefully organized traversal turns many separate deletion experiments into one computation. https://garfield.library.upenn.edu/classics1983/A1983QY47200001.pdf[Robert E. Tarjan, Citation Classic recollection (1983)]{ref}

= Biconnected component
{parent=Cut vertex}
{wiki}

A <biconnected component> is a maximal connected <subgraph> with no <cut vertex> of its own. We use the block convention that also includes each <bridge in a graph> with its two endpoint <vertices>, and each isolated <vertex>, as a <biconnected component>. A <biconnected component> with at least three <vertices> stays connected after any single <vertex> deletion. Distinct <biconnected components> share at most one <vertex>; the <vertices> belonging to more than one <biconnected component> are exactly the <cut vertices>. The shared-$A$ example has two <biconnected components>, its two <triangles in a graph>. The joined-$CD$ example has three: $ABC$, the <bridge in a graph> $CD$ with its endpoints, and $DEF$. The <edges> partition into these pieces, while the shared <cut vertices> occur in several pieces. https://algs4.cs.princeton.edu/41graph/[Sedgewick and Wayne, Algorithms, §4.1, biconnected components exercises]{ref}

= DFS discovery time
{c}
{parent=Cut vertex}
{title2=$\operatorname{disc}(v)$}

The <DFS discovery time> of a <vertex> is its first-visit number in <depth-first search>, assigned by an increasing counter. Discovery <edges> form a <rooted tree> in each <connected component of a graph>. For the joined-$CD$ example, starting at $A$ and visiting <graph neighbours> in <lexicographic order> gives first visits $A,B,C,D,E,F$, numbered $1,2,3,4,5,6$; its discovery <edges> are $AB,BC,CD,DE,EF$. These numbers encode the chosen traversal, not an invariant of the <graph>.

= DFS lowpoint
{c}
{parent=Cut vertex}
{title2=$\operatorname{low}(v)$}

For a finite <simple graph> that is an <undirected graph>, the <DFS lowpoint> of $v$ is the smallest <DFS discovery time> reachable by following zero or more discovery <edges> down its <rooted tree>, then at most one non-discovery <edge> to an ancestor. Staying at $v$ is allowed. Ignore the reverse traversal of the discovery <edge> to the <parent in a rooted tree>: it is the same <edge>, not an escape route. Start with $\operatorname{low}(v)=\operatorname{disc}(v)$. On returning from a <child in a rooted tree> $w$, replace $\operatorname{low}(v)$ by $\min\{\operatorname{low}(v),\operatorname{low}(w)\}$. For a non-discovery <edge> from $v$ to an earlier ancestor $a$, replace it by $\min\{\operatorname{low}(v),\operatorname{disc}(a)\}$. This summarizes how far upward the explored branch can reconnect. https://sites.cs.ucsb.edu/~gilbert/cs240a/notes/TarjanDFS.pdf[Tarjan (1972), §3, LOWPT recurrence, pp. 151–153]{ref}

The <cut vertex> criterion has a separate case for each <root of a tree> in the discovery <forest>:
* A nonroot <vertex> $u$ is a <cut vertex> exactly when some <child in a rooted tree> $w$ satisfies $\operatorname{low}(w)\geq\operatorname{disc}(u)$.
* A <root of a tree> is a <cut vertex> exactly when it has at least two <children in a rooted tree>, counting discovery <edges>, rather than all <graph neighbours>.
* A discovery <edge> $uw$ is a <bridge in a graph> exactly when $\operatorname{low}(w)>\operatorname{disc}(u)$. A non-discovery <edge> is never a <bridge in a graph>.

Why does equality matter? If $\operatorname{low}(w)=\operatorname{disc}(u)$, the branch below $w$ can reconnect to $u$, so the discovery <edge> $uw$ lies on a <graph cycle> and is not a <bridge in a graph>. Deleting the <vertex> $u$ destroys that reconnection too. If $u$ has a <parent in a rooted tree>, the branch is then separated from that <parent in a rooted tree>, explaining the non-strict <cut vertex> test. For a <root of a tree>, there is no earlier side to separate from; distinct discovery branches cannot have an <edge> between them, since <depth-first search> would have explored them as one branch. This gives the two-<children in a rooted tree> rule. These are proof outlines of the criteria. https://algs4.cs.princeton.edu/41graph/Biconnected.java.html[Sedgewick and Wayne, Biconnected.java: discovery, lowpoint and root tests]{ref}https://algs4.cs.princeton.edu/41graph/[Sedgewick and Wayne, Algorithms, §4.1: bridge detection]{ref}

In the joined-$CD$ example with <DFS discovery times> $1,2,3,4,5,6$, the <DFS lowpoints> in label order are $1,1,1,4,4,4$. For $C$ and its <child in a rooted tree> $D$, $4>3$: $CD$ is a <bridge in a graph>, and $C$ is a <cut vertex>. For $D$ and its <child in a rooted tree> $E$, $4=4$: $D$ is a <cut vertex>, although $DE$ is not a <bridge in a graph>. The <root of a tree> $A$ has only one <child in a rooted tree>, $B$, so it is not a <cut vertex>. In the shared-$A$ example, the <root of a tree> $A$ has two <children in a rooted tree>, $B$ and $D$, and is a <cut vertex>, while every <DFS lowpoint> is $1$ and no <edge> passes the strict <bridge in a graph> test.

Restart <depth-first search> at every undiscovered <vertex> to cover a disconnected <graph>, applying the <root of a tree> rule separately in each <connected component of a graph>. With <adjacency lists> and constant-cost label operations, computing all <DFS lowpoints>, <cut vertices> and <bridges in a graph> has <time complexity> $O(|V|+|E|)$ in <Big O notation>, because each <vertex> is discovered once and each <edge> is inspected twice. The simple parent-skipping rule assumes a <simple graph>. In a <multigraph>, skip only the actual parent <edge>, identified individually: a parallel <edge> to the same <parent in a rooted tree> is a valid reconnection and can prevent the first <edge> from being a <bridge in a graph>. The criteria here concern <undirected graphs>; a <directed graph> requires a different connectivity analysis.

\Include[hypergraph]{parent=Graph theory}

= Walk in a graph
{parent=Graph theory}
{title2=$v_0,e_1,v_1,\ldots,e_m,v_m$}

= Walk
{synonym}

A <walk> records a journey through a <graph> as a finite alternating <sequence>
$$
W=(v_0,e_1,v_1,\ldots,e_m,v_m),\qquad m\geq0,
$$
where each <edge> $e_i$ joins the consecutive <vertices> $v_{i-1}$ and $v_i$. Both <vertices> and <edges> may repeat. Its length is $m$, counting <edge> traversals with repetitions, rather than counting different <edges> or measuring the drawing. Here a <graph path> means a <walk> with no repeated <vertices>; a <trail> instead forbids repeated <edges>. These conventions distinguish a journey's record from the <subgraph> consisting of the connections it used.https://ptwiddle.github.io/MAS341-Graph-Theory-2017/lecturenotes/lecture3.html[Paul Johnson, MAS341, Lecture 3: walks and trails]{ref}

Unless stated otherwise, the examples below use a finite <simple graph> that is an <undirected graph>, so an <edge> may be traversed in either direction. In a <multigraph>, retain each <edge>'s individual identity as well as its endpoint <vertices>: two parallel <edges> are different choices for the same step. If <self-loops> are allowed, traversing one leaves the current <vertex> unchanged but adds one to the length. In a <directed graph>, each step must follow its <directed edge> from its initial to its terminal <vertex>, giving a <directed walk>; reversing a journey need not be possible.https://people.math.binghamton.edu/zaslav/Oldcourses/581.S99/notes.html[Thomas Zaslavsky, Supplementary Notes for Graduate Graph Theory: edge identities and walks]{ref}

We allow the zero-length <walk> $(v)$ at every <vertex> $v$. It uses no <edges>, so it is also a <trail> and a <graph path>, and its coinciding endpoints make it a <closed walk>. This means staying put is a legitimate journey; a <graph> with no <vertices> has no such <walk>. Positive-length restrictions will be stated explicitly.<Mathematics for Computer Science by Eric Lehman and others>[Lehman, Leighton and Meyer, Mathematics for Computer Science, §11.8.1: length-zero convention]{ref}

Use the same constructed <graph> throughout these three sections:
$$
V=\{A,B,C,D,E,F\},\qquad E=\{AB,BC,CA,AD,DE,EA,AF\}.
$$
Here $AB$ denotes the <unordered pair> $\{A,B\}$, and $CA=AC$. Two <triangles in a graph>, $ABC$ and $ADE$, share $A$, with one extra <edge> $AF$. These are schematic connections, not measured roads; the highlighted connections in the diagram show which <edges> are used, while the numbered <sequence> records their order.

\Image[walks-trails-closed-walks-route-comparison.png]
{title=One constructed <graph>: repeated <edges> in a <walk>, repeated <vertices> in a <trail>, and a <graph path>}

The length-six <walk> $B,C,A,B,C,A,F$ uses $BC$ and $CA$ twice, so it is not a <trail>. The length-six <trail> $B,C,A,D,E,A,F$ returns to $A$ but uses six different <edges>, so it is not a <graph path>. The length-three <graph path> $B,C,A,F$ repeats neither <vertices> nor <edges>. Thus
$$
\{\text{graph paths}\}\subsetneq\{\text{trails}\}\subsetneq\{\text{walks}\}
$$
for this particular <graph>. The restrictions answer different routing questions: revisiting a junction is harmless for many journeys, whereas reusing a serviced road segment can matter for an inspection route. Travel costs require a <weighted graph>; a <walk>'s cost adds the weight of each traversal, even when an <edge> is used again.

Every finite <walk> from $s$ to $t$ contains a <graph path> from $s$ to $t$ after deleting detours. For a proof outline, if $v_i=v_j$ with $i<j$, delete the intervening <closed walk>. The retained steps still meet at the same <vertex>, and the length strictly decreases. Repeating terminates with no repeated <vertices>, or with the zero-length <graph path> when $s=t$. This explains why <reachability> can be formulated using either <walks> or <graph paths>. With nonnegative <edge> weights, deletion also cannot increase cost, connecting the argument to the <shortest path problem>; with negative weights that cost conclusion fails.https://ptwiddle.github.io/MAS341-Graph-Theory-2017/lecturenotes/lecture3.html[Paul Johnson, MAS341, Lecture 3: deleting repeated-vertex detours]{ref}

= Trail in a graph
{parent=Walk in a graph}

= Trail
{synonym}

A <trail> is a <walk> whose <edge> <sequence> $e_1,\ldots,e_m$ has no repetitions. Its <vertices> may repeat, including its first and last <vertices>. The zero-length <walk> is a <trail> because it repeats no <edge>. Every <graph path> is a <trail>, but the converse fails: the <trail> in the shared example is
$$
B,BC,C,CA,A,AD,D,DE,E,EA,A,AF,F.
$$
Its six <edges> $BC,CA,AD,DE,EA,AF$ are pairwise distinct, although $A$ occurs twice. Visiting a junction again and traversing a road segment again are different events.https://people.math.binghamton.edu/zaslav/Oldcourses/581.S99/notes.html[Thomas Zaslavsky, Supplementary Notes for Graduate Graph Theory: trails and paths]{ref}

In an <undirected graph>, traversing $AB$ and then $BA$ repeats the same <edge>; direction of travel does not give it a new identity. In a <multigraph> with distinct parallel <edges> $p,q$ joining $X$ and $Y$, the <closed walk> $(X,p,Y,q,X)$ is a <trail>, whereas $(X,p,Y,p,X)$ is not. Both have the same <vertex> <sequence> $X,Y,X$. In a <directed graph>, separate <directed edges> $X\to Y$ and $Y\to X$ may instead be used once each; record their identities and obey their directions. Thus testing a <trail> means checking the actual <edge> records, not merely checking consecutive endpoint labels.

For a finite <graph>, a <trail> has length at most $|E|$; unrestricted <walks> can have arbitrarily large length whenever a positive-length <closed walk> exists, since it can be repeated. An <Eulerian trail> is the special <trail> that uses every <edge>, so its length is exactly $|E|$. Our length-six <trail> omits $AB$ and is therefore not an <Eulerian trail>. In a road-inspection model, a <trail> describes servicing its selected segments once while allowing returns to junctions; requiring service of every segment leads to the <Eulerian trail> question. If extra travel between serviced segments is necessary, the complete journey can be a <walk> even when each segment is serviced only once. This distinction keeps the service requirement separate from the route actually travelled.

= Closed walk
{parent=Walk in a graph}
{title2=$v_m=v_0$}

A <closed walk> is a <walk> with equal endpoint <vertices>, $v_m=v_0$. Under our convention this includes the zero-length <walk> $(v)$; returning after traversing at least one <edge> requires $m>0$. Closure imposes no other restriction on repeated <vertices> or <edges>. In a <directed graph>, a <closed directed walk> must also follow every <directed edge> in its permitted direction.<Mathematics for Computer Science by Eric Lehman and others>[Lehman, Leighton and Meyer, Mathematics for Computer Science, §§9.2 and 11.8.1: closed walks]{ref}

\Image[walks-trails-closed-walks-return-comparison.png]
{title=Returning to $A$: backtracking <closed walk>, closed <trail> with a repeated internal <vertex>, and <graph cycle>}

In the shared <simple graph>, $A,F,A$ is a length-two <closed walk> that immediately backtracks along $AF$. It is not a <trail>, since $AF$ is used twice, and it is not a <graph cycle>. The length-six <closed walk> $A,B,C,A,D,E,A$ is a <trail>, since its six <edges> are distinct, but is not a <graph cycle>: it returns to $A$ midway through the journey. The length-three <closed walk> $A,B,C,A$ is a <graph cycle>, with all <vertices> distinct apart from the required endpoint repetition. In a <simple graph> that is an <undirected graph>, a <graph cycle> has length at least three; the two-step backtrack is excluded.<Mathematics for Computer Science by Eric Lehman and others>[Lehman, Leighton and Meyer, Mathematics for Computer Science, §11.8.1: cycles and backtracking]{ref}

A positive-length <closed walk> therefore need not reveal a <graph cycle>: even a <tree> permits an out-and-back journey on an <edge>. A positive-length closed <trail> in a <simple graph> does contain a <graph cycle>. For a proof outline, choose a shortest positive-length consecutive portion of the <trail> whose endpoint <vertices> coincide. It cannot repeat an internal <vertex>, since that would give a shorter such portion; it cannot have length one or two because <self-loops> and repeated <edges> are excluded. It is consequently a <graph cycle>. Cutting it out leaves another closed <trail>, possibly of length zero, so repetition partitions the original <edges> into <graph cycles>. In our six-step example these are the two <triangles in a graph>. A closed <trail> using every <edge> would be an <Euler circuit>; here $AF$ is omitted.

Counting return journeys makes <closed walks> useful even when they repeat <edges>. For a finite <simple graph> with <adjacency matrix> $M$, the <walk count from powers of an adjacency matrix> gives $(M^k)_{uv}$ length-$k$ <walks> from $u$ to $v$, for every <nonnegative integer> $k$. A proof outline is that <matrix multiplication> appends one allowed step, summing over the previous endpoint; $M^0$ is the <identity matrix>, recording the unique zero-length <walk> at each <vertex>. Consequently
$$
(M^k)_{vv}=\#\{\text{length-}k\text{ closed walks starting at }v\},\qquad \operatorname{tr}(M^k)=\sum_{v\in V}(M^k)_{vv}.
$$
The <trace> counts <closed walks> with a specified starting <vertex> and traversal order, not distinct <graph cycles>. These formulas use adjacency entries, not arbitrary travel costs in a <weighted graph>.https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/14-15-networks-spring-2022/mit14_15s22_lec2.pdf[Alexander Wolitzky, Graph Theory and Social Networks, slides 9–10: powers of adjacency matrices]{ref}

In our example, $(M^2)_{AA}=5$: the five length-two <closed walks> are $A,B,A$, $A,C,A$, $A,D,A$, $A,E,A$, and $A,F,A$. Also $(M^3)_{AA}=4$, going round either <triangle in a graph> in either direction, and $\operatorname{tr}(M^3)=12$: each of the two <triangles in a graph> supplies three choices of starting <vertex> and two directions. These exact counts for the constructed <graph> illustrate why return counts and <graph cycle> counts differ.

A <closed walk> naturally models a delivery or inspection journey that returns to its depot. Repeated <vertices> let it combine several excursions; repeated <edges> let it serve a dead end and return. For an <Euclidean travelling salesman tour>, one can first construct a <closed walk> visiting all locations and then use the <triangle inequality> to shortcut repeated visits without increasing total length. That argument assumes direct travel between the retained locations is permitted, as in a <complete graph> weighted by <Euclidean distance>; an arbitrary road network need not contain those shortcut <edges>.

= Undirected graph
{parent=Graph theory}

An undirected <graph> has edges that are unordered pairs of vertices. In a simple undirected graph there are no loops or multiple edges. The <skeleton of a directed graph> forgets the directions of its edges and is an undirected graph.

= Graph minor
{parent=Graph theory}
{wiki}
{title2=$H\preccurlyeq G$}

A <graph minor> compresses connected regions of a <graph> into single <vertices> while retaining selected connections between them. For finite <simple graphs>, $H\preccurlyeq G$ means that some sequence of deleting <vertices>, deleting <edges>, and performing <edge contractions> produces $H$ up to <graph isomorphism>. Deleting a <vertex> also deletes all its incident <edges>; deleting an <edge> leaves its endpoints. The <edge contraction> convention here removes resulting <self-loops> and duplicate <edges>.<Graph Theory by Reinhard Diestel/The Basics (author-hosted chapter preview)>{ref}

For an exact small example, let $G$ be the <cycle graph> $C_4$ with <vertices> $u,v,a,b$ and <edges> $uv,va,ab,bu$. Contract $uv$ to a new <vertex> $w$. The surviving <edges> are $wa,ab,bw$, giving the <complete graph> $K_3$. Thus $K_3\preccurlyeq C_4$, although $C_4$ has no <triangle in a graph> as a <subgraph>. The <branch sets of a graph minor> are $\{u,v\},\{a\},\{b\}$, with the original <edges> $va,ab,bu$ witnessing all three required adjacencies. The first panel of the <edge contraction> diagram shows this operation.

A <subgraph> uses existing <vertices> and <edges>; a <topological minor> permits a <graph subdivision> of the target as a <subgraph>, so target <edges> become <graph paths> with mutually disjoint interiors and no target <vertex> in an interior. An ordinary <graph minor> permits whole connected <branch sets of a graph minor> in place of target <vertices>. Every <topological minor> is a <graph minor>, by contracting the replacement <graph paths>.<Graph Theory by Reinhard Diestel/The Basics (author-hosted chapter preview)>[Proposition 1.7.3(i)]{ref}

The converse fails. Take the <tree> with <edges> $uv,ua,ub,vc,vd$. Contracting $uv$ gives the <star graph> $K_{1,4}$. Yet the original <tree> has <maximum degree> three, whereas every <graph subdivision> of $K_{1,4}$ retains a centre of <degree of a vertex> four. Hence $K_{1,4}$ is a <graph minor> but not a <topological minor> of this <tree>. The difference is that a connected <branch set of a graph minor> may gather connections incident to several original <vertices>.

The counts of <vertices> and <edges> never increase under these operations. However, the $C_4$ example shows that being a <bipartite graph> is not preserved: its <graph minor> $K_3$ contains an <odd cycle>. Its <chromatic number> also increases from two to three. A <connected graph> stays connected under an <edge contraction>, because the image of each <graph path> still connects its endpoints, but deletion can disconnect it. Thus one must check the particular <graph property> before treating it as preserved by taking <graph minors>.

Being a <planar graph> is preserved: in a crossing-free drawing, shrink a contracted <edge> and bring its incident <edges> into the merged <vertex>; deletion merely removes parts of the drawing. This makes a nonplanar <graph minor> a certificate that the original <graph> is nonplanar. More strongly, for a finite <simple graph>, being a <planar graph> is equivalent to having neither the <complete graph> $K_5$ nor the <complete bipartite graph> $K_{3,3}$ as a <graph minor>. Finding the corresponding <branch sets of a graph minor> settles nonplanarity even when no literal $K_5$ or $K_{3,3}$ <subgraph> is present. The converse is a substantial theorem, rather than a consequence of preservation alone.<Graph Theory by Reinhard Diestel/Planar Graphs (author-hosted chapter preview)>[Theorem 4.4.6, equivalence of planarity and exclusion of K5 and K3,3 minors]{ref}

= Topological minor
{parent=Graph minor}
{title2=$H\preccurlyeq_{\mathrm{top}}G$}

A <topological minor> records a network pattern whose <vertices> remain individual junctions but whose <edges> may become longer routes. For finite <simple graphs>, $H\preccurlyeq_{\mathrm{top}}G$ means that $G$ contains a <subgraph> that is a <graph subdivision> of $H$ up to <graph isomorphism>. Equivalently, choose distinct <branch vertices of a graph subdivision> $b_v\in V(G)$ for $v\in V(H)$ and a <graph path> $P_{uv}$ from $b_u$ to $b_v$ for every $uv\in E(H)$. These are <internally vertex-disjoint paths>, and no internal <vertex> of any $P_{uv}$ is a <branch vertex of a graph subdivision>. Only endpoints corresponding to a shared target <vertex> may be shared.<Graph Theory by Reinhard Diestel/The Basics (author-hosted chapter preview)>[§1.7, printed p. 18: subdivisions; printed p. 21: topological-minor embeddings]{ref}

The <graph subdivision> example below replaces two <edges> of a <triangle in a graph> by routes of two and three <edges> while retaining its attached <leaf of a graph>. If the host <graph> also contains an extra <edge> $BD$, the same routes still witness a <topological minor>: delete $BD$ when selecting the <subgraph>. An <induced subgraph> is not required. However, routes that merely connect the right pairs are insufficient: using a <branch vertex of a graph subdivision> as an intermediate junction, or making two routes share an internal <vertex>, can change the local network structure.

Every <topological minor> is a <graph minor>. In each <branch path of a graph subdivision>, contract all but one <edge>; the disjoint interiors let this shorten each route without merging distinct <branch vertices of a graph subdivision>. This recovers $H$ after deleting unused <vertices> and <edges>.<Graph Theory by Reinhard Diestel/The Basics (author-hosted chapter preview)>[Proposition 1.7.3(i), printed p. 21]{ref} The converse fails because an ordinary <branch set of a graph minor> can gather several junctions into one.

For the <tree> $T$ with <vertices> $u,v,a,b,c,d$ and <edges> $uv,ua,ub,vc,vd$, contracting $uv$ produces a <star graph> $K_{1,4}$: the <branch sets of a graph minor> are $\{u,v\},\{a\},\{b\},\{c\},\{d\}$. Both $u,v$ have <degree of a vertex> three; each of the other four <vertices> is a <leaf of a graph> with <degree of a vertex> one. The new centre $w$ has four distinct <graph neighbours> $a,b,c,d$; its <degree of a vertex> is $3+3-2=4$, in agreement with the <edge contraction> formula.

\Image[topological-minor-tree-contraction-degree-obstruction.png]
{title=A <graph minor> can combine two junctions: contracting $uv$ creates a <star graph> with a centre of <degree of a vertex> four}

Yet $K_{1,4}$ is not a <topological minor> of $T$. In a <graph subdivision> of $K_{1,4}$, four <branch paths of a graph subdivision> must leave the centre through four different <edges>; sharing their first internal <vertex> would violate internal disjointness. Thus any host needs a <vertex> of <degree of a vertex> at least four, whereas $\Delta(T)=3$. More generally, $H\preccurlyeq_{\mathrm{top}}G$ implies $\Delta(H)\leq\Delta(G)$, where $\Delta$ is the <maximum degree of a graph>. This bound concerns the host <graph>; extra unused <edges> may make its <branch vertices of a graph subdivision> have larger <degrees of vertices> than their target counterparts.

This distinction matters when a network's individual junctions have a limited number of connections: replacing a connection by a route preserves the number of routes meeting at each chosen junction, while contracting a connected region can combine connections from separate junctions. It also explains why a small nonplanar pattern can hide inside long routes. By <Kuratowski's theorem>, a finite <simple graph> is a <planar graph> exactly when it has neither the <complete graph> $K_5$ nor the <complete bipartite graph> $K_{3,3}$ as a <topological minor>. The obstruction is a <graph subdivision> inside the network, regardless of the number of intermediate <vertices>.

= Rooted branch-set reduction
{parent=Graph minor}
{title2=$m=h-q/2$}

Let $q\ge2$ be even, let $S=\{x_1,\ldots,x_q\}$, and suppose a <graph> has $h$ disjoint nonempty <vertex> <sets> $C_1,\ldots,C_h$, where $h\ge d+3q/2$. Suppose each $G[C_i]$ is connected or every one of its <graph components> meets $S$, and each $C_i$ is adjacent to all but at most $d$ of the other $C_j$ not meeting $S$. Assume also that no <rooted separation of a graph> of order below $q$ avoids $d+1$ of these <sets>. Then there are $m=h-q/2$ disjoint connected <vertex> <sets> $D_1,\ldots,D_m$ such that $x_i\in D_i$ for $1\le i\le q$ and each of these first $q$ <sets> is adjacent to all but at most $d$ of $D_{q+1},\ldots,D_m$.

Here adjacency of <sets> means existence of an <edge> between them. A <rooted separation of a graph> is $(A,B)$ with $A\cup B=V(G)$, $S\subseteq A$, no <edges> between $A\setminus B$ and $B\setminus A$, and order $|A\cap B|$. It avoids $C_i$ when $A\cap C_i=\varnothing$. We prove the reduction by <induction>, first on the <vertex> count and then on the <edge> count; any counterexample chosen minimal in this ordering will be impossible.

We may delete <edges> joining two <vertices> of $S$. They cannot affect <rooted separations of a graph>, since $S$ lies wholly on the first side. Within a branch <set>, their deletion can only split a <graph component> into <graph components> each containing an endpoint in $S$, so the branch-set hypothesis also persists. An isolated <vertex> outside $S$ and all branch <sets> can be deleted. An isolated <vertex> $v\in S$ is also impossible, since $(S,V(G)\setminus\{v\})$ has order $q-1$ and avoids at least $h-q\ge d+q/2\ge d+1$ <sets>. Thus in a minimal counterexample $S$ is independent, and every isolated <vertex> outside $S$ belongs to a singleton branch <set>.

Consider a <rooted separation of a graph> $(A,B)$ of order exactly $q$ avoiding at least $d+1$ branch <sets>, and put $S'=A\cap B$. Restrict to $G'=G[B]-E(G[S'])$ and $C'_i=C_i\cap B$. Each $C'_i$ is nonempty: any $C_i$ not itself avoided is adjacent to at least one of the $d+1$ avoided <sets>, and the endpoint of such an <edge> in $C_i$ must lie in $B$. If a <graph component> of $G'[C'_i]$ misses $S'$, it lies in $B\setminus A$ and is a whole <graph component> of $G[C_i]$, with no <vertex> of $S$. The original hypothesis then forces $G[C_i]$ to be connected and $C'_i=C_i$, entirely outside $A$. Thus each restricted <set> is connected or all its <graph components> meet $S'$. In particular every $C'_j$ missing $S'$ equals an original <set> outside $A$, so all its required adjacencies from $C'_i$ persist.

A <rooted separation of a graph> $(A',B')$ of $G'$ avoiding $d+1$ restricted <sets> lifts to $(A\cup A',B')$ in $G$, of the same order: an avoided restricted <set> misses $S'$, hence is an original <set> outside $A$. The <edges> removed inside $S'$ do not obstruct lifting, since $S'\subseteq A'$. This proves the required separation hypothesis for $G'$. If $G'$ is smaller, <induction> gives the desired connected <sets> rooted at $S'$. Moreover $G[A]$ has $q$ disjoint <graph paths> from $S$ to $S'$, by the <Menger theorem>. Indeed a separator of smaller order between these <sets> would give a <rooted separation of a graph> of $G$ of smaller order still avoiding those $d+1$ original <sets>. Truncate the <graph paths> at their first visits to $S'$, label their endpoints accordingly, and adjoin each <graph path> to the corresponding rooted connected <set>. They meet $B$ only in their distinct endpoints, so this gives the desired <sets> for $G$, a contradiction. Consequently in a minimal counterexample every such separation of order $q$ has $B=V(G)$ and $A=S$, an <independent set>.

Now contract any <edge> whose endpoints do not belong to two different branch <sets>. Its endpoints cannot both lie in $S$, so the image of $S$ still has size $q$. The branch-set conditions persist. If the contraction created a forbidden <rooted separation of a graph> of order below $q$, its lift would have order below $q$, except possibly when the contracted <vertex> lies in its separator. In that case the lifted order increases by one and is at most $q$. Equality would put the contracted <edge> inside $A$, contradicting the just-proved independence of $A$ for an order-$q$ separation avoiding $d+1$ <sets>. Thus the separation hypothesis also persists.
