Applying <induction> in the contracted <graph> and expanding the contracted <vertex> would produce the required disjoint connected <sets>. Hence **every <edge> in a minimal counterexample joins two different branch <sets>**.

Every <vertex> outside the branch <sets> would be isolated, since every <edge> joins two branch <sets>; such an isolated <vertex> could be deleted. Thus the branch <sets> cover the <graph>. Each nonsingleton branch <set> is therefore an <independent set>, and all its <vertices> belong to $S$, because each of its singleton <graph components> must meet $S$. Let $C$ be the union of the nonsingleton branch <sets>, and write $c=|C|\le q$. There is a <matching in a graph> from $C$ into $V(G)\setminus S$. Otherwise the <Hall marriage theorem> gives $X\subseteq C$ with neighbourhood $Y\subseteq V(G)\setminus S$ satisfying $|Y|<|X|$. Since $S$ is independent, $(S\cup Y,V(G)\setminus X)$ is a <rooted separation of a graph> of order $q-|X|+|Y|<q$. It avoids every singleton branch <set> outside $S\cup Y$, of which there are at least
$$
|V(G)|-q-|Y|\ge |V(G)|-q-c+1\ge h-\frac c2-q+1\ge h-\frac{3q}2+1\ge d+1.
$$
The penultimate counting bound uses that the nonsingleton <sets> number at most $c/2$, so $|V(G)|-c\ge h-c/2$. This contradicts the separation hypothesis.

Use that <matching in a graph> to make a two-vertex connected <set> for each terminal in $C$; terminals outside $C$ remain singleton <sets>. There are at least
$$
|V(G)|-q-c\ge h-\frac{3q}2=m-q
$$
unused <vertices>, all singleton original branch <sets>. Choose $m-q$ of them for the remaining $D_i$. Every rooted $D_i$ contains a singleton original branch <set>: either its terminal, or its terminal's matched <neighbour>. That singleton misses at most $d$ of the selected nonterminal singleton <sets>. This constructs the desired family and completes the proof.

= Complete graph minor density threshold
{parent=Graph minor}
{title2=$c(t)$}

The complete graph minor density threshold is
$$
c(t)=\inf\{c:\text{every nonempty finite graph }G\text{ with }e(G)\geq c|G|\text{ has a }K_t\text{ minor}\}.
$$
The density here is <edge> count divided by order, half the average <degree of a vertex>. For large $t$, this threshold has order $t\sqrt{\log t}$, with the logarithm taken to base $e$.

= Dense minor with bounded order and high minimum degree
{parent=Complete graph minor density threshold}

For each positive integer $k$, a nonempty <graph> satisfying $e(G)\geq11k|G|$ has a <graph minor> $H$ satisfying
$$
|H|\leq11k+2,\qquad 2\delta(H)\geq |H|+4k-1.
$$
The density hypothesis is a lower bound. Reversing its inequality would be false for an edgeless <graph>. This auxiliary lemma can be used to establish a $7t\sqrt{\log t}$ upper bound for the <complete graph minor density threshold>.

= Branch set of a graph minor
{parent=Graph minor}

= Branch sets of a graph minor
{synonym}

A <branch set of a graph minor> is the connected region representing one target <vertex>. For finite <simple graphs> $G$ and $H$, a witness to $H\preccurlyeq G$ assigns a nonempty <set> $B_x\subseteq V(G)$ to each $x\in V(H)$, such that the <sets> are pairwise disjoint, each <induced subgraph> $G[B_x]$ is a <connected graph>, and every $xy\in E(H)$ has a witnessing <edge> of $G$ between $B_x$ and $B_y$. Extra <edges> between <branch sets of a graph minor> are allowed: they can be deleted. Unused <vertices> are also allowed.<Minor Containment and Disjoint Paths in almost-linear time by Tuukka Korhonen and others>{ref}

\Image[graph-minor-connected-branch-set-adjacency.png]
{title=Connected <branch sets of a graph minor> and their witnessing <edges>, with an optional <edge> deletion}

In the diagram, the host <graph> has <vertex set> $\{1,2,3,4,5,6\}$ and <edges>
$$
12,\ 34,\ 56,\ 23,\ 45,\ 61,\ 24.
$$
Choose $B_A=\{1,2\}$, $B_B=\{3,4\}$, and $B_C=\{5,6\}$. Their internal <edges> $12,34,56$ establish connectedness. The <edges> $23,45,61$ witness $AB,BC,CA$, respectively, so they form a model of the <complete graph> $K_3$. The additional <edge> $24$ also joins $B_A$ to $B_B$; it supplies another witness for the same target <edge>, not a fourth target <edge>. Contracting $12,34,56$ and merging duplicate <edges> gives exactly $\{AB,BC,CA\}$.

The same <branch sets of a graph minor> also witness the <path graph> $A,B,C$: retain the witnesses $23,45$, and delete $61$ to remove the unwanted $AC$ adjacency. This demonstrates why the definition requires an <edge> for every target adjacency, without forbidding other adjacencies in the host <graph>. For a <complete graph> $K_t$, there are no unwanted pairs: its model consists of $t$ disjoint connected <branch sets of a graph minor> with an <edge> between every pair.

To turn a model into an operation sequence, keep a <spanning tree> inside each $G[B_x]$, keep one witnessing <edge> for each <edge> of $H$, and delete all other <edges> and unused <vertices>. Contract each chosen <spanning tree> to one <vertex>. Disjointness prevents two target <vertices> from merging; connectedness provides the <edge contractions> that merge each individual region. Conversely, track the original <vertices> merged into every surviving <vertex> during a deletion-and-<edge contraction> sequence. They form disjoint connected <branch sets of a graph minor>, and each surviving <edge> comes from an original <edge> between its two regions. A list of these regions, their connecting <trees>, and their witnessing <edges> therefore gives a directly checkable certificate.

= Random branch-set construction of a complete graph minor
{parent=Branch set of a graph minor}

In a dense <graph> with many <common neighbours> for every pair of <vertices>, choose disjoint random small sets. A set is good if few <vertices> have no neighbour in it. The <Markov inequality> controls the number of bad sets, and random sampling makes almost every pair of good sets adjacent. Add unused <common neighbours> to connect each set and to repair the remaining missing adjacencies. If the number of used <vertices> stays below every common-neighbour count, this constructs disjoint <branch sets of a graph minor> representing a large <complete graph>.

= Edge contraction
{parent=Graph minor}
{wiki}
{title2=$G/e$}

An <edge contraction> merges the two endpoints of an existing <edge>. For a finite <simple graph> $G$ and $e=uv$, replace $u,v$ by a new <vertex> $w$. Retain every <edge> with neither endpoint in $\{u,v\}$, and join $w$ to every member of
$$
N_{G/e}(w)=(N_G(u)\cup N_G(v))\setminus\{u,v\}.
$$
Here $N_G(u)$ and $N_G(v)$ denote the original open <graph neighbourhoods>, and $N_{G/e}(w)$ is the new <graph neighbourhood>. Remove the <self-loop> arising from $uv$ and merge duplicate <edges>, so the result $G/e$ is again a <simple graph>. This is not deletion of $uv$, which keeps both original <vertices>.<Graph Theory by Reinhard Diestel/The Basics (author-hosted chapter preview)>{ref}

\Image[graph-edge-contraction-common-neighbour-count.png]
{title=<Edge contraction> of a four-cycle and a <triangle in a graph>: <common neighbours> determine the lost <edge> count}

The left example contracts $uv$ in the <cycle graph> $C_4$ to give $\{wa,ab,bw\}$. Its endpoints have no <common neighbours>, so four <edges> become three. The right example contracts $uv$ in the <triangle in a graph> with <edges> $uv,ua,va$. Its endpoints have the single <common neighbour> $a$, so the two <edges> $ua,va$ become one <edge> $wa$: three <edges> become one.

For any such <edge contraction>, the exact counts are
$$
|V(G/e)|=|V(G)|-1,\qquad |E(G/e)|=|E(G)|-1-|N_G(u)\cap N_G(v)|.
$$
The contracted <edge> accounts for one loss. Each <common neighbour> $z$ contributes exactly one further loss, since $uz,vz$ become a single <edge> $wz$. All other <edges> correspond one-to-one to surviving <edges>. In particular the new <degree of a vertex> is
$$
\deg_{G/e}(w)=\deg_G(u)+\deg_G(v)-2-|N_G(u)\cap N_G(v)|.
$$
These formulas depend on simplifying to a <simple graph>; a <multigraph> convention that retains duplicate <edges> has different counts. In the six-<vertex> <branch set of a graph minor> diagram, contracting $12$ loses one <edge>, contracting the resulting $34$ loses two because of its <common neighbour> $A$, and contracting $56$ loses one. Hence its seven <edges> become the three <edges> of $K_3$: $7-1-2-1=3$.

An <edge contraction> can increase a <degree of a vertex> even while decreasing both total counts. For example, contracting $uv$ in the <tree> with <edges> $uv,ua,ub,vc,vd$ merges two <vertices> of <degree of a vertex> three into one of <degree of a vertex> four. This is the mechanism behind the distinction between a <graph minor> and a <topological minor>.

= Vertex cover
{parent=Graph theory}
{wiki}

A <vertex cover> chooses <vertices> that collectively touch every <edge> of a <graph>. Think of placing monitors at junctions: if a monitor watches every connection incident to its junction, a <vertex cover> watches all connections. The <minimum vertex cover> asks how few monitors suffice. This is a model of covering connections through their endpoints, rather than of monitoring distances in the drawing.

For a finite <simple graph> $G=(V,E)$, a <vertex cover> is a <subset> $C\subseteq V$ satisfying
$$
\{u,v\}\in E\Longrightarrow u\in C\ \text{or}\ v\in C.
$$
Both endpoints may be selected; the condition requires at least one. An <isolated vertex> covers no <edge>, so a <minimum vertex cover> never needs it.https://diestel-graph-theory.com/Sample.pdf[Diestel, Graph Theory, fourth electronic edition, Section 2.1, definition of vertex cover]{ref}

Take the <cycle graph> $C_5$ with <vertices> $0,1,2,3,4$ and <edges> $01,12,23,34,40$. The <set> $C=\{1,3,4\}$ is a <vertex cover>: <vertex> $1$ covers $01$ and $12$, <vertex> $3$ covers $23$ and $34$, and <vertex> $4$ covers $40$ as well as $34$. In particular, the closing <edge> $40$ is covered even though <vertex> $0$ is unselected.

The unselected <vertices> $I=V\setminus C=\{0,2\}$ form an <independent set>. This is the key connection: an <edge> missed by $C$ would have both endpoints in $I$, precisely what an <independent set> forbids. For every <subset> $C\subseteq V$,
$$
C\text{ is a vertex cover}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad V\setminus C\text{ is an independent set}.
$$
Here $V\setminus C$ is the <complement of a set> within $V$; the <graph> itself keeps all its <edges>. The <complement graph> is a different construction.https://introtcs.org/public/lec_12_NP.html[Barak, Introduction to Theoretical Computer Science, Chapter 14, vertex-cover exercise]{ref}

\Image[vertex-cover-independent-complement.png]
{title=A minimum vertex cover and its maximum independent complement on the same five-cycle}

The endpoints of any <maximal matching> form a <vertex cover>: an <edge> avoiding all endpoints would enlarge that <matching in a graph>. Consequently a <graph> with no <matching in a graph> of size $s$ has a <vertex cover> of size at most $2(s-1)$. This supplies a convenient <vertex cover>, but it need not be a <minimum vertex cover>.

= Minimum vertex cover
{parent=Vertex cover}

A <minimum vertex cover> is a <vertex cover> of smallest <cardinality> among all <vertex covers> of the same finite <simple graph>. The optimum size is the <vertex cover number>. By the <complement of a set> correspondence, a <minimum vertex cover> leaves a largest possible <independent set> unselected.

Minimum size is stronger than merely being unable to remove a selected <vertex>. In the <star graph> $K_{1,3}$, the three leaves form a <vertex cover> from which no <vertex> can be removed: each leaf covers its own <edge>. Nevertheless, the centre alone is a <minimum vertex cover> of size one. Such an inclusion-minimal <vertex cover> can therefore be much larger than a <minimum vertex cover>.

= Vertex cover number
{parent=Minimum vertex cover}
{title2=$\tau(G)$}

The <vertex cover number> records the size of a <minimum vertex cover>:
$$
\tau(G)=\min\{|C|:C\subseteq V(G),\ C\text{ is a vertex cover of }G\}.
$$
The <vertex cover number> is a number, while a <minimum vertex cover> is a <set> attaining it. For the <cycle graph> $C_5$, the <independence number> is $2$, so the complement correspondence gives $\tau(C_5)=5-2=3$. The <vertex cover> $\{1,3,4\}$ above attains this size.

Every <matching in a graph> gives a lower bound on the <vertex cover number>. Its <edges> have no shared endpoints, so covering each selected <edge> requires a different <vertex>. Thus, for any <matching in a graph> $M$ and any <vertex cover> $C$,
$$
|M|\leq |C|,\qquad \nu(G)\leq\tau(G),
$$
where $\nu(G)$ is the <matching number>. Exhibiting a <matching in a graph> and a <vertex cover> of the same size certifies that both are optimum. <Kőnig's theorem for bipartite matching> says that every finite <bipartite graph> admits this equality: $\nu(G)=\tau(G)$.https://diestel-graph-theory.com/Sample.pdf[Diestel, Graph Theory, Section 2.1, Theorem 2.1.1 and its lower-bound argument]{ref}

For the <complete bipartite graph> $K_{2,3}$, let the left <vertices> be $L=\{a,b\}$ and the right <vertices> be $R=\{x,y,z\}$. All six <edges> join $L$ to $R$. Selecting $C=L$ covers every <edge>; selecting $M=\{ax,by\}$ gives a <matching in a graph> of size two. The lower bound and this <vertex cover> give $2\leq\tau(K_{2,3})\leq2$, so $\tau(K_{2,3})=\nu(K_{2,3})=2$. The remaining <vertices> $R$ form a largest <independent set> of size three. Crossings of drawn <edges> are not extra <vertices>.

\Image[bipartite-matching-cover-certificate.png]
{title=Two disjoint matching edges certify that the two left vertices are a minimum cover of K₂,₃}

The <bipartite graph> hypothesis matters. In a <triangle in a graph>, any two <vertices> form a <minimum vertex cover>, while any two <edges> share an endpoint. Hence $\tau(K_3)=2$ and $\nu(K_3)=1$. Similarly, the <cycle graph> $C_5$ has $\tau(C_5)=3$ and $\nu(C_5)=2$. The inequality for the <matching number> remains valid, but equality can fail.

= 3-SAT reduction to vertex cover
{c}
{parent=Vertex cover}

A <polynomial-time many-one reduction> represents each variable by an adjacent literal pair and each clause by a <triangle in a graph>. Connect each occurrence vertex to its corresponding literal vertex. A <vertex cover> of size $n+2m$ must choose one literal vertex per variable and two vertices per clause, and its omitted clause vertex forces a true literal. Conversely a satisfying <Boolean valuation> supplies such a cover. This establishes <NP-hardness> of the vertex-cover decision problem from <3-SAT>.

= Binomial random graph
{title2=$G(n,p)$}
{parent=Graph theory}

The binomial random graph $G(n,p)$ has vertex set $\{1,\ldots,n\}$ and includes each of the $\binom n2$ possible edges independently with probability $p$.

= Poisson threshold for vertices lying in no triangle
{c}
{parent=Binomial random graph}
{title2=$\binom{n-1}{2}p^3=\log(n/c)\Longrightarrow T\Rightarrow\operatorname{Po}(c)$}

For a fixed set of $r$ vertices, exclude all triangles meeting that set. Their total expectation is $r\log(n/c)+o(1)$. Distinct triangle pairs sharing an edge contribute $O_r(n^3p^5)=o(1)$ to the <Janson dependency sum>. <Harris' inequality> and <Janson inequality> bound the avoidance probability between matching exponentials, giving $(c/n)^r(1+o(1))$. The <factorial-moment criterion for Poisson convergence> then gives the displayed law.

= Deferred edge exposure in greedy independent-set colouring
{parent=Binomial random graph}

Construct an <independent set> by choosing a candidate vertex, exposing only its incident edges, and retaining its non-neighbours as candidates. Once the chosen independent set is removed, every exposed edge has a removed endpoint. Thus the remaining internal edges still have their independent original laws, conditional on the revealed history. This special exposure rule permits fresh-binomial estimates for successive classes; an arbitrary adaptively selected vertex subset does not have that property.

= Strictly balanced graph
{parent=Binomial random graph}
{title2=$e(F)/v(F)<e(H)/v(H)$}

A finite <graph> $H$ with at least one <edge> is strictly balanced when every proper nonempty <subgraph> $F$ has smaller edge-to-vertex ratio. It is connected and has no isolated <vertices>: otherwise a component of at least the average density, or deletion of isolated <vertices>, violates the inequality. The density condition suppresses overlapping copies at the sparse appearance scale.

= Poisson limit for strictly balanced subgraph counts
{c}
{parent=Strictly balanced graph}
{title2=$X_H(G(n,cn^{-v/e}))\Rightarrow\operatorname{Po}(c^e/a)$}

Here $v,e,a$ are the numbers of <vertices>, <edges> and <graph automorphisms> of $H$. Vertex-disjoint ordered $r$-tuples contribute $(n)_{rv}p^{re}/a^r\to(c^e/a)^r$ to the <factorial moment>. Adding a copy overlapping the previous union in $f$ <vertices> and $g$ <edges> changes the power of $n$ by $-f+(v/e)g\le0$, strictly when the intersection is a proper nonempty <subgraph>. The first overlap is strict because $H$ is connected and the previous copies are disjoint. Finitely many overlap types therefore contribute $o(1)$. The <factorial-moment criterion for Poisson convergence> gives the result.

= Missing common-neighbour count in a binomial random graph
{parent=Binomial random graph}
{title2=$\mathbb EX=\binom n2(1-p^2)^{n-2}$}

Let $X$ count unordered <vertex> pairs with no <common neighbour> in a <binomial random graph>. Each other <vertex> provides the required pair of <edges> independently, with success probability $p^2$. A fixed pair has no common neighbour with probability $(1-p^2)^{n-2}$. Summing its indicators gives the displayed <expectation>.

= Diameter bound for a fixed-density binomial random graph
{parent=Missing common-neighbour count in a binomial random graph}
{title2=$\Pr(\operatorname{diam}G(n,p)\leq2)\to1$}

For fixed $0<p<1$, the <missing common-neighbour count in a binomial random graph> has <expectation> tending to zero. The <Markov inequality> therefore shows that every pair has a <common neighbour> with probability tending to one. On that event every <graph distance> is at most two, so the <graph> is <connected> and its <graph diameter> is at most two.

= Chromatic number of a binomial random graph
{parent=Binomial random graph}
{title2=$\mathbb E\chi(G(n,p))\sim n/(2\log_{1/(1-p)}n)$}

For a fixed probability strictly between zero and one, the largest independent set has leading size $2\log_{1/(1-p)}n$, giving a lower bound for colouring. The matching upper bound uses <independent sets in every large subset of a dense random graph>, then repeatedly colours and removes such sets. A sufficiently strong probability estimate makes the failure contribution negligible for the expectation.

= Independent sets in every large subset of a dense random graph
{parent=Chromatic number of a binomial random graph}
{title2=$|U|\ge n/(\log n)^2\Longrightarrow\alpha(G[U])\ge(2-\gamma)\log_{1/(1-p)}n$}

For fixed $p$ and $\gamma>0$, <Janson inequality> bounds the failure probability for a given large <vertex> subset by $\exp(-c|U|^2/(\log n)^4)$. A union bound over all at most $2^n$ subsets still tends to zero. This uniform statement is essential because <vertex> sets left by a greedy colouring procedure depend on the <graph>; one cannot assume each adaptively chosen remainder is an independent fresh random <graph>.

= Vertex exposure for chromatic number
{parent=Binomial random graph}
{title2=$\Pr(|\chi-\mathbb E\chi|\ge\lambda\sqrt n)\le2e^{-2\lambda^2}$}

Group random <edges> by their larger endpoint. These independent coordinates each change <edges> incident to only one <vertex>. Removing that <vertex> leaves the same <graph> under any two outcomes, so their <chromatic numbers> differ by at most one. The <McDiarmid inequality> with $n$ coordinate ranges of length one gives the displayed concentration bound.

= Chromatic number of the half-density binomial random graph
{parent=Binomial random graph}
{title2=$\chi(G(n,1/2))\sim\frac n{2\log_2n}$}

For the <binomial random graph> $G(n,1/2)$, the <chromatic number> is $(1+o(1))n/(2\log_2n)$ <with high probability>. A <first moment method> bounds the <independence number> above. <Edge-disjoint clique packing> in the <complement graph>, followed by an <edge-exposure martingale> and a <union bound> over moderately large <vertex> sets, supplies <independent sets> for <greedy colouring by removing independent sets>.

= Clique count in a binomial random graph
{parent=Binomial random graph}

If $X_t$ counts copies of the <complete graph> $K_t$ in $G(n,p)$, then
$$
\mathbb EX_t=\binom ntp^{\binom t2}.
$$
Consequently $p n^{2/(t-1)}\to0$ implies $\mathbb P(X_t>0)\to0$ by the <first moment method>.

= Fixed-size clique appearance threshold
{parent=Clique count in a binomial random graph}
{title2=$\mu_n=\binom nkp^{\binom k2}$}

For fixed $k\ge2$, a <binomial random graph> has no $k$-<cliques> with probability tending to one if $\mu_n\to0$, and has at least one with probability tending to one if $\mu_n\to\infty$. The first assertion uses the <first moment method>. The second uses the <overlap formula for the variance of a clique count>: overlapping $k$-sets with $l\ge2$ contribute $O(n^{-l}p^{-\binom l2})$, and each contribution tends to zero above this threshold. The fixed-size qualification matters.

= Growing clique size can invalidate a first-moment appearance criterion
{parent=Fixed-size clique appearance threshold}
{title2=$k=n-1,\quad p=1-(\log n)/n^2$}

With these parameters the expected number of $(n-1)$-cliques is asymptotic to $\sqrt n$, yet the probability of any such <clique> tends to zero. The complement graph has about $\tfrac12\log n$ edges, and with probability tending to one two of them are vertex disjoint. Deleting one vertex cannot remove both edges. Thus divergence of the expected <clique> count alone does not give an appearance theorem for arbitrary growing <clique> size.

= Overlap formula for the variance of a clique count
{parent=Clique count in a binomial random graph}
{title2=$\operatorname{Var}X/(\mathbb EX)^2$}

For the number $X$ of $k$-<cliques> in a <binomial random graph> $G(n,p)$, with $0<p<1$ and $\mu=\mathbb EX$,
$$
\frac{\operatorname{Var}X}{\mu^2}=\sum_{j=2}^k\frac{\binom kj\binom{n-k}{k-j}}{\binom nk}\left(p^{-\binom j2}-1\right).
$$
Two $k$-sets overlapping in $j$ vertices share $\binom j2$ edges. Overlaps of zero or one vertex give independent indicators; $j=k$ includes the diagonal terms. The identity is useful for the <second moment method>.

= Growing-clique overlap bound
{parent=Overlap formula for the variance of a clique count}
{title2=$r\leq n^{1/3},\ \mu_r\to\infty,\ \mu_{r+1}\to0\Longrightarrow\operatorname{Var}X_r=o(\mu_r^2)$}

Write $\mu_r=\binom nrp^{\binom r2}$ and $T_s=\binom rs\binom{n-r}{r-s}p^{-\binom s2}/\binom nr$. The first mean condition implies $p^{-1}\leq(en/r)^{2/(r-1)}$ eventually. For $2\leq s\leq r/2$, this yields $T_s\leq(C/s)^s$, and each fixed-$s$ term tends to zero when $n\geq r^3$. The second mean condition implies $p\leq((r+1)/n)^{2/r}$ eventually. Writing $s=r-j$ gives $T_{r-j}=\mu_r^{-1}\binom rj\binom{n-r}j p^{j(2r-j-1)/2}\leq\mu_r^{-1}(C/j)^j$ for $1\leq j\leq r/2$, and $T_r=\mu_r^{-1}$. Both bounding series are summable. The <overlap formula for the variance of a clique count> therefore gives relative variance tending to zero; the <second moment method> proves an $r$-<clique> exists <with high probability>.

= Triangle count in a binomial random graph
{parent=Binomial random graph}

If $X$ counts triangles in $G(n,p)$, then
$$
\mathbb EX=\binom n3p^3
$$
and
$$
\operatorname{var}X
\leq\binom n3p^3
+2\binom n2\binom{n-2}2p^5.
$$
Thus $np\to\infty$ implies $\operatorname{var}X/(\mathbb EX)^2\to0$, and the <second moment method> gives $\mathbb P(X>0)\to1$.

= Triangle variance in a binomial random graph
{parent=Triangle count in a binomial random graph}
{title2=$\operatorname{Var}X=\binom n3p^3(1-p^3)+12\binom n4p^5(1-p)$}

For the <triangle count> in a <binomial random graph>, distinct triangle indicators are independent unless they share an <edge>. A shared-edge pair has <covariance> $p^5(1-p)$, and there are $12\binom n4$ ordered pairs of that type. The individual indicator variances give the first term. Equivalently,
$$
\mathbb EX^2=\binom n3\sum_{\ell=0}^3\binom3\ell\binom{n-3}{3-\ell}p^{6-\binom\ell2},
$$
by counting ordered pairs according to the number of shared <vertices>.

= Triangle-existence threshold in a binomial random graph
{parent=Triangle variance in a binomial random graph}
{title2=$p\asymp n^{-1}$}

If $np\to0$, the <triangle count in a binomial random graph> has <expectation> at most $(np)^3/6\to0$, so the <Markov inequality> shows there are no <triangles in a graph> with probability tending to one. If $np\to\infty$, the <triangle variance in a binomial random graph> divided by the squared mean is $O((np)^{-3})+O((n^2p)^{-1})\to0$. The <second moment method> then gives at least one <triangle in a graph> with probability tending to one. At the intermediate scale $np\to c\in(0,\infty)$, a nontrivial <Poisson limit for triangles in a binomial random graph> describes the count.

= Poisson limit for triangles in a binomial random graph
{c}
{parent=Triangle count in a binomial random graph}
{title2=$\binom n3p^3\to\alpha\Longrightarrow X\to\operatorname{Poisson}(\alpha)$}

Convergence holds in <total variation distance>. A triangle indicator depends only on its three edges, so its dependency neighborhood consists of itself and the $3(n-3)$ triangles sharing an edge. The <Poisson approximation with dependency neighborhoods> gives errors $b_1=O(n^4p^6)$ and $b_2=O(n^4p^5)$. Under the displayed scaling $p$ is of order $n^{-1}$, so both vanish. The probability of at least one triangle therefore tends to $1-e^{-\alpha}$.

= Triangle with an attached leaf in a binomial random graph
{parent=Binomial random graph}

Partition the vertices into two sets of comparable size. If $np\to\infty$, the first set contains a triangle with probability tending to one. Conditional on any triangle chosen using only internal edges, the probability that none of its vertices has a neighbour in the second set is at most
$$
(1-p)^{3\lfloor n/2\rfloor}
\leq e^{-3p\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\longrightarrow0.
$$
Hence $G(n,p)$ contains a triangle with an attached leaf with probability tending to one.

\Include[graph]{parent=Graph theory}

= Graph coloring
{parent=Graph theory}
{wiki}

= Graph colouring
{synonym}

A <graph coloring> assigns labels called colours to elements of a <graph>, subject to specified constraints. Here we focus on <proper vertex colouring> of a <simple graph> $G=(V,E)$: it is an <undirected graph> with a <finite set> of <vertices>, and a colouring with at most $k$ colours is a <function>
$$
c:V\to\{1,\ldots,k\},\qquad \{u,v\}\in E\Longrightarrow c(u)\ne c(v).
$$
The colour labels need not be visual colours: they can represent time slots or resources. Unused labels are allowed. Properness concerns <graph adjacency>, so moving <vertices> in a drawing does not change whether the <graph coloring> is valid.https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/6-1200j-mathematics-for-computer-science-spring-2024/mit6_1200j_s24_lec11.pdf[MIT, Graphs and Coloring, Section 2.1]{ref}

Each <colour class> $c^{-1}(i)$ is an <independent set>: no <edge> has both endpoints in it. Conversely, a <set partition> of $V$ into <independent sets> gives a proper <graph coloring> by assigning one colour to each part. Thus <graph coloring> groups mutually compatible objects, and its <chromatic number> asks for the fewest such groups.

For an illustrative scheduling model, let each <vertex> represent an activity of equal duration and each <edge> represent a pair that cannot occur in the same time slot. A proper <graph coloring> is then a schedule respecting those pairwise conflicts. If the conflicts form a <cycle graph> $C_4$ with <edges> $12,23,34,41$, activities $1,3$ can share one slot and $2,4$ another. This model assumes interchangeable slots and includes only the pairwise conflicts encoded by the <graph>.https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/6-042j-mathematics-for-computer-science-fall-2010/1ccaf180bcd244fddd32e60789fa922f_h9wxtqoa1jY.pdf[Leighton, MIT Graph Theory and Coloring, transcript pages 8–10]{ref}

The word "colouring" also occurs in <edge coloring>, which requires different colours on <edges> sharing an endpoint. The <chromatic number> discussed here always refers to <proper vertex colouring>.

= Proper vertex coloring
{parent=Graph coloring}
{title2=$\{u,v\}\in E\Longrightarrow c(u)\ne c(v)$}

= Proper vertex colouring
{synonym}

A proper vertex coloring assigns labels to the <vertices> of a <graph> so that <vertices> joined by an <edge> have different labels. Each <colour class> is an <independent set>. The <chromatic number> is the least number of labels needed for this form of <graph coloring>.

= Semi-random palette refinement for triangle-free colouring
{parent=Graph coloring}

Maintain lists of available colours, tentatively colour small random portions, erase conflicts and prune colours used by neighbours. In a <triangle-free graph>, a vertex's neighbours form an <independent set>, which enables local estimates. Track list size and average remaining neighbour congestion per colour; prune unusually congested colours before the next round. Concentration and the <Lovász local lemma> preserve these invariants until lists have enough slack for completion. Tracking averages matters because triangle-free graphs can still contain many four-cycles and need not have uniform per-colour congestion.

= Colour savings from sparse neighbourhoods
{parent=Graph coloring}

If each neighbourhood misses a fixed positive fraction of the possible pairs at a large degree bound, randomly assign about half that many colours and erase vertices with monochromatic incident edges. A nonadjacent neighbour pair can share a retained colour, giving a <neighbour-colour saving>. Write the number of successful repeated colours as a difference of two <certifiable functions>: colours with a nonadjacent pair, minus those for which a neighbour loses that colour. Both change by at most two under one-coordinate changes and have constant-size certificates. <Talagrand's convex distance inequality> and the <Lovász local lemma> make their linear expected saving simultaneous at all high-degree vertices; low-degree vertices have slack already. This yields a colouring using a fixed positive fraction fewer than the degree bound.

= Coloring entropy weight
{parent=Graph coloring}
{title2=$w(G)=|V(G)|\min_\chi H(\chi(Z))$}

For a uniformly chosen vertex $Z$, minimize the <information entropy> of its color over proper colorings. Multiply by the number of vertices to define this weight. Minimizing the color entropy gives the strongest contribution from that graph in the <entropy weight of a graph cover> inequality.

= Entropy weight of a graph cover
{parent=Coloring entropy weight}
{title2=$\sum_iw(G_i)\geq n\log_2(n/\alpha)$}

If graphs $G_i$ cover the edges of an $n$-vertex graph with <independence number> at most $\alpha$, their coloring entropy weights satisfy the displayed bound. A random vertex's color is recorded in every covering graph containing it; independent dummy colors with matching marginal laws fill the other positions. Conditional on all recorded colors, compatible vertices form an <independent set>, leaving conditional entropy at most $\log_2\alpha$. The <mutual information> carried by the colors is at most $n^{-1}\sum_iw(G_i)$, proving the bound.

= Greedy coloring
{parent=Graph coloring}
{wiki}

= Greedy graph coloring
{synonym}

= Greedy colouring
{synonym}

For a finite <simple graph>, choose an order of its <vertices> and process them one at a time. Give each <vertex> the smallest positive colour label not already used by its coloured neighbours, and keep all earlier assignments. This is <greedy coloring>: each step makes a locally available choice. The resulting <graph coloring> is proper because, for each <edge>, its later endpoint avoids the colour of its earlier endpoint.https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/6-042j-mathematics-for-computer-science-fall-2010/1ccaf180bcd244fddd32e60789fa922f_h9wxtqoa1jY.pdf[Leighton, MIT Graph Theory and Coloring, transcript pages 12–13]{ref}

If the <maximum degree of a graph> is $\Delta(G)$, a <vertex> has at most $\Delta(G)$ previously coloured neighbours, forbidding at most that many distinct colours. At least one label among $1,\ldots,\Delta(G)+1$ remains available. Consequently every order gives
$$
\chi(G)\leq\text{number of colours used by greedy colouring}\leq\Delta(G)+1.
$$
This is an upper bound, not a promise that <greedy coloring> attains the <chromatic number>.https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/6-1200j-mathematics-for-computer-science-spring-2024/mit6_1200j_s24_lec11.pdf[MIT, Graphs and Coloring, Theorem 2]{ref}

\Image[greedy-coloring-order.png]
{title=Two orders for <greedy coloring> of the same <path graph> $P_4$}
{description=The <edges> are $12,23,34$ in both drawings. Each dot shows its assigned colour number, with the <vertex> label above and its processing step below. The order $1,2,3,4$ uses two colours; the order $1,4,2,3$ uses three.}

Consider the <path graph> $P_4$ with <edges> $12,23,34$. In the order $1,2,3,4$, <greedy coloring> assigns the colours $1,2,1,2$ respectively. In the order $1,4,2,3$, it assigns colours $1,1,2,3$ in processing order: <vertices> $1,4$ both get colour $1$, then <vertex> $2$ gets colour $2$, and finally <vertex> $3$ sees colours $2$ and $1$ at its two neighbours and must take colour $3$. Written in <vertex> order $1,2,3,4$, this second colouring is $1,2,3,1$.

The <path graph> has <chromatic number> two: the first assignment achieves two colours, and any <edge> rules out one. Its <maximum degree of a graph> is two, so three colours in the second assignment still satisfy the bound. The extra colour comes entirely from the processing order. The <crown graph> gives a larger family where a poor order can make <greedy coloring> use arbitrarily many colours although the <chromatic number> remains two.

= Extension of a partial colouring by neighbour-colour savings
{parent=Greedy coloring}
{title2=$K-d(v)+S_v\ge1$}

In a proper partial <graph coloring>, let $R_v$ count coloured neighbours and $D_v$ count their distinct colours. The saving $S_v=R_v-D_v$ leaves $K-D_v$ available colours for only $d(v)-R_v$ initially uncoloured neighbours. If the displayed inequality holds at every vertex, ordinary greedy completion succeeds. Each additionally coloured neighbour removes at most one available colour while also reducing the number of uncoloured neighbours by one.

= Neighbour-colour saving
{parent=Extension of a partial colouring by neighbour-colour savings}
{title2=$S_v=R_v-D_v$}

The number of coloured neighbours of $v$ minus the number of distinct colours on those neighbours. Repeated colours among pairwise nonadjacent neighbours reduce the number of unavailable colours without increasing the number of neighbours still to colour.

= Three-colourability problem
{parent=Graph coloring}

= Three-colorability problem
{synonym}

This <decision problem> asks whether a finite <undirected graph> has a <graph colouring> with three colours. It is <NP-complete>: a colouring certifies membership in <NP>, while <3-SAT> reduces using <Boolean-pair colouring gadgets> and <three-colour clause gadgets>.

= Three-colour clause gadget
{parent=Graph coloring}

= Three-color clause gadget
{synonym}

Take auxiliary <graph triangles> $a,b,c$ and $d,t,e$, join $a$ to $d$, and add edges $bx,cy,ez$. When $t$ has colour $T$ and the inputs $x,y,z$ have colours in $\{T,F\}$, this graph admits an extension to a <graph colouring> with three colours exactly when at least one input is $T$. All-false inputs force $a=d=F$, contradicting their edge. Conversely, use $(a,b,c,d,e)=(T,F,B,F,B)$ if $x=T$, $(T,B,F,F,B)$ if $x=F,y=T$, and $(F,T,B,B,F)$ if $x=y=F,z=T$. The extension property is necessary for both directions of a <polynomial-time many-one reduction>.

= Boolean-pair colouring gadget
{c}
{parent=Graph coloring}

= Boolean-pair coloring gadget
{c}
{synonym}

Two <graph triangles> sharing a vertex $B$ force their two opposite edges to use the same two colours in a <graph colouring> with three colours. A palette <graph triangle> $T,F,B$ and another <graph triangle> $u,\bar u,B$ therefore force the literal pair to have opposite Boolean colours. This gadget is used in a reduction from <3-SAT> to the <three-colourability problem>.

= Greedy colouring by removing independent sets
{parent=Graph coloring}
{title2=$\chi(G)\leq |V(G)|/r+m$}

If every <vertex> subset of size at least $m$ contains an <independent set> of size $r$, repeatedly colour and remove an $r$-element <independent set> while at least $m$ <vertices> remain. Giving each remaining <vertex> its own colour uses at most $|V|/r+m$ colours.

= Lovász number
{parent=Graph coloring}
{c}
{wiki}
{title2=$\vartheta(G)$}

= Lovász theta function
{c}
{synonym}

The Lovász number of a finite simple <graph> is the <semidefinite program> value
$$
\vartheta(G)=\max\{\operatorname{tr}(JB):B\succeq0,\ \operatorname{tr}B=1,\ B_{ij}=0\text{ for }ij\in E(G)\},
$$
where $J$ is the <all-ones matrix>. Its value on the <complement graph> gives a lower bound for the <chromatic number>.

= Orthonormal representation of a graph
{parent=Lovász number}

= Orthonormal representation
{synonym}

Assign a real unit vector $u_v$ to each graph vertex, with $u_v\perp u_w$ whenever distinct vertices are nonadjacent. A handle is a further unit vector $h$. In the handle formulation of the <Lovász number>, one minimizes $\max_v|h\cdot u_v|^{-2}$ over representations and handles. Orthonormal here does not mean every pair of assigned vectors is orthogonal.

= Tensor-product capacity bound from an orthonormal representation
{parent=Orthonormal representation of a graph}

Tensor products of the assigned vectors represent strong graph powers, and tensor products of the handle have squared inner products equal to products of the original ones. The vectors assigned to an independent set are orthonormal. <Bessel inequality> then bounds its size by the largest inverse squared handle inner product. Taking roots and minimizing proves $c(G)\leq\vartheta(G)$.

= Complement theta number
{parent=Lovász number}
{title2=$\bar\vartheta(G)=\vartheta(\bar G)$}

For a finite simple <graph> with at least one vertex, the complement theta number has the equivalent <semidefinite program> formulations
$$
\bar\vartheta(G)=\min\left\{t:
\begin{pmatrix}t&\mathbf1^T\\\mathbf1&Z\end{pmatrix}\succeq0,
\ Z_{ii}=1,\ Z_{ij}=0\text{ for }ij\in E(G)\right\}
$$
and
$$
\bar\vartheta(G)=\min\{t:U\succeq0,\ U_{ii}=t-1,\ U_{ij}=-1\text{ for }ij\in E(G)\}.
$$
The <Schur complement> and $U=tZ-J$ prove the equivalence; any feasible $t$ is at least one, so division by $t$ is valid. For a <graph> with an edge, $U/(t-1)$ is the <Gram matrix> of a <strict vector coloring>. Consequently $\bar\vartheta(G)$ is the strict vector chromatic number, and an ordinary $k$-<graph colouring> gives $\bar\vartheta(G)\leq k$ by assigning the colors the vertices of a <regular simplex>.

= Vector coloring
{parent=Graph coloring}

A vector $k$-coloring, for $k>1$, assigns a <unit vector> $v_i$ to each <vertex> of a <graph>, such that $\langle v_i,v_j\rangle\leq-1/(k-1)$ on every <edge>. A <graph colouring> with $k$ colors gives a vector $k$-coloring by placing the colors at the vertices of a <regular simplex>. The <Gram matrix> of these <vectors> allows <semidefinite programming> to search for such a representation.

= Strict vector coloring
{parent=Vector coloring}

A strict vector $k$-coloring requires equality $\langle v_i,v_j\rangle=-1/(k-1)$ on every <edge>. The least admissible $k$ is the <complement theta number> for a <graph> with an edge. Allowing merely an inequality defines the potentially smaller vector chromatic number.

= Random hyperplane rounding
{parent=Vector coloring}

For <unit vectors> $u,v$ and a random normal $a$ with independent <standard normal distribution> coordinates,
$$
\mathbb P\{\operatorname{sign}\langle a,u\rangle\ne\operatorname{sign}\langle a,v\rangle\}
=\frac{\arccos\langle u,v\rangle}{\pi}.
$$
The <Gaussian distribution> is invariant under <orthogonal transformations>. Projecting onto the <plane> spanned by $u,v$ therefore gives a uniformly distributed direction. If the angle between $u,v$ is $\theta$, the sign-disagreement directions form two sectors of total angle $2\theta$ out of $2\pi$. The endpoint cases $u=v$ and $u=-v$ give probabilities zero and one directly.

= Semicoloring
{parent=Graph coloring}

A $k$-semicoloring of a finite <graph> assigns one of $k$ colors to every <vertex> and is a proper <graph colouring> on an <induced subgraph> containing at least half the <vertices>. This is weaker than a proper coloring of the whole <graph>, and is useful as an intermediate step in a <randomized algorithm>.

= Semicoloring by independent hyperplanes
{parent=Semicoloring}

Suppose a <graph> with $n$ <vertices> and $m>0$ <edges> has a <vector coloring> with adjacent inner products at most $-1/2$. Take $r$ independent copies of <random hyperplane rounding> and use the $r$ signs as a color. Each <edge> remains monochromatic with probability at most $3^{-r}$, so the expected number $B$ of monochromatic <edges> is at most $m3^{-r}$ by <linearity of expectation>.

Choose $r=\max\{0,\lceil\log_3(4m/n)\rceil\}$. Then <Markov inequality> gives $\mathbb P(B>n/2)\leq1/2$. On success, delete one endpoint of every monochromatic <edge>. This <random alteration method> leaves at least $n/2$ <vertices> properly colored with
$$
k=2^r=O\left(\max\{1,(m/n)^{\log_3 2}\}\right)=O(n^{\log_3 2}).
$$
The removed <vertices> can retain their original colors, as the <semicoloring> only requires the retained <induced subgraph> to be proper. A failed draw can be detected and repeated, with at most two trials on average. The case $m=0$ needs just one color. This is the elementary independent-hyperplane construction in https://arxiv.org/abs/cs/9812008[Karger, Motwani and Sudan's paper on approximate graph coloring].

= Edge coloring
{parent=Graph coloring}
{wiki}
{title2=$c:E(G)\to\{1,\ldots,k\}$}

= Edge colouring
{synonym}

An <edge colouring> assigns a label called a colour to every <edge of a graph>: it is a <function> $c:E(G)\to\{1,\ldots,k\}$. Here this definition allows arbitrary assignments. Two <edges> sharing a <vertex> may receive the same colour. A <proper edge colouring> imposes the additional condition that such <edges> receive different colours. Some textbooks use "<edge colouring>" as shorthand for this proper version; check the convention before applying a theorem.<Line graphs and edge coloring>[Line graphs and edge coloring, slide 1: arbitrary labeling versus proper edge coloring]{ref}

The distinction matters in <Ramsey theory>. In a red-blue <edge colouring> of a <complete graph>, a <triangle in a graph> is monochromatic when its three <edges> have the same colour. These are arbitrary <edge colourings>: forbidding equal colours on <edges> sharing a <vertex> would forbid the very monochromatic <triangles in a graph> being sought. For example, assigning red to every <edge> of $K_3$ is a valid arbitrary <edge colouring>, although it is not a <proper edge colouring>. Colour labels can also stand for time slots: then <proper edge colouring> turns incompatible jobs into separate rounds.

= Proper edge coloring
{parent=Edge coloring}
{title2=$e\ne f,\ e\cap f\ne\varnothing\Longrightarrow c(e)\ne c(f)$}

= Proper edge colouring
{synonym}

A <proper edge colouring> gives different labels to any two distinct <edges> sharing a <vertex>. We work with finite <undirected graphs> without <self-loops>; distinct parallel <edges> in a <multigraph> must also have different colours. For a <simple graph>, whose <edges> are two-element <sets>, the condition is
$$
e\ne f,\quad e\cap f\ne\varnothing\quad\Longrightarrow\quad c(e)\ne c(f).
$$
Every <colour class> $M_i=\{e\in E(G):c(e)=i\}$ is a <matching in a graph>. Conversely, a <set partition> of the <edges> into <matchings in a graph> supplies a <proper edge colouring>. Thus minimizing colours means grouping all the <edges> into as few <matchings in a graph> as possible.

The <line graph> makes the relationship with <proper vertex colouring> explicit: replace each original <edge> by a <vertex>, joining two new <vertices> exactly when their original <edges> share an endpoint. A <proper edge colouring> of $G$ becomes a <proper vertex colouring> of its <line graph> $L(G)$, so $\chi\prime(G)=\chi(L(G))$. For a <multigraph>, retain a distinct new <vertex> for each parallel <edge>.<Line graphs and edge coloring>[Line graphs and edge coloring, slide 1: line graph identity]{ref}

= Edge coloring as scheduling
{parent=Proper edge coloring}

Suppose each job occupies two distinct resources for one whole slot, and each resource can serve at most one job in that slot. Make resources <vertices> and jobs <edges>. Two jobs conflict exactly when their <edges> share a <vertex>, so a valid schedule is a <proper edge colouring>. Each slot is a <matching in a graph>; the <chromatic index> is the minimum number of slots. The colour labels specify the slots, rather than physical colours. This model assumes all jobs are available initially, with equal duration, interchangeable slots and no additional conflicts. The school-timetable motivation in <Graph Theory by Reinhard Diestel> (author-hosted Colouring preview, opening paragraphs, pp. 123–124) uses this resource-pair interpretation.

Here is a complete example. Two technicians $A,B$ must each use three instruments $X,Y,Z$ once. Each job needs its technician and its instrument simultaneously. The six jobs are the <edges> of the <complete bipartite graph> $K_{2,3}$:
$$
E=\{AX,AY,AZ,BX,BY,BZ\},\qquad
M_1=\{AX,BY\},\quad M_2=\{AY,BZ\},\quad M_3=\{AZ,BX\}.
$$
Within each $M_i$, the two jobs use different technicians and different instruments, so $M_i$ is a <matching in a graph>. The three <matchings in a graph> together cover every job exactly once. Technician $A$ has three jobs, so fewer than three slots cannot suffice; the displayed schedule uses three. This is an optimal <proper edge colouring> with <chromatic index> three, witnessed by both a schedule and a resource-workload lower bound.

\Image[edge-coloring-bipartite-job-slots.png]
{title=Six jobs on a <complete bipartite graph> become three scheduling slots, each a <matching in a graph>}
{description=The <vertices> are resources, and each numbered <edge> is one job. The slot table uses the same colour labels as the <proper edge colouring>. A technician or instrument appears at most once within a column. Crossings of drawn <edges> introduce no resource and no conflict.}

Repeated jobs requiring the same resource pair become distinct parallel <edges> of a <multigraph>, rather than one <edge>. Additional restrictions change the problem: prescribed allowed slots lead to <list edge coloring>, while unequal durations, deadlines, or jobs needing more than two resources require a richer <scheduling> model. Selecting a largest <matching in a graph> solves a single-round question; a shortest complete schedule must organize all jobs across rounds.

= List edge coloring
{parent=Edge coloring}

Each <edge> has a list of allowed colors; a list edge coloring chooses an allowed color on each edge, giving different colors to incident edges. The <list-chromatic index> is the uniform list-size threshold guaranteeing success.

= List-chromatic index
{parent=List edge coloring}
{title2=$\chi\prime_\ell(G)$}

The list-chromatic index is the least integer $q$ such that every assignment of at least $q$ allowed colors to each <edge> admits a <list edge coloring>. Uniform identical lists show it is at least the <chromatic index>. The <Galvin theorem for bipartite list edge coloring> gives equality for <bipartite graphs>.

= Galvin theorem for bipartite list edge coloring
{c}
{parent=List-chromatic index}
{title2=$\chi\prime_\ell(G)=\chi\prime(G)=\Delta(G)$}

A <bipartite graph> has equal <list-chromatic index>, <chromatic index> and <maximum degree of a graph> $\Delta$. Start with a proper $\Delta$-<edge coloring>. Orient its <line graph> toward smaller colors at one part and toward larger colors at the other. An edge of color $c$ has at most $(c-1)+(\Delta-c)=\Delta-1$ outgoing neighbours. Every induced orientation has a <digraph kernel> obtained from a <stable matching in a bipartite graph> with those preferences. Apply the <kernel list-coloring lemma>.

= Chromatic index
{parent=Edge coloring}
{title2=$\chi\prime(G)$}

The <chromatic index> $\chi\prime(G)$, also called the <edge chromatic number>, is the least number of labels in a <proper edge colouring> of a finite <undirected graph> without <self-loops>. Equivalently, it is the smallest number of <matchings in a graph> whose <set partition> covers all the <edges>. We take $\chi\prime(G)=0$ when there are no <edges>. For an arbitrary <edge colouring>, one colour always suffices whenever an <edge> exists, so properness is essential to this definition. The <chromatic number> $\chi(G)$ instead colours <vertices>.<Graph Theory by Reinhard Diestel>[Colouring, author-hosted chapter preview, p. 124: chromatic index and line graph]{ref}

To establish an exact <chromatic index>, supply both a <proper edge colouring> and a reason that fewer colours cannot work. Every <edge> at a fixed <vertex> must have its own colour, giving the lower bound $\chi\prime(G)\geq\Delta(G)$, where $\Delta(G)$ is the <maximum degree of a graph>. The bound need not be attained: it counts a single resource's workload, while conflicts can also accumulate around a <graph cycle>.

\Image[edge-coloring-triangle-even-cycle.png]
{title=Worked <proper edge colourings>: a <triangle in a graph> needs three colours, while an even <cycle graph> needs two}
{description=Numbers beside <edges> are colour labels; letters at dots are <vertices>. Both <graphs> have <maximum degree of a graph> two. The <triangle in a graph> has three pairwise conflicting <edges>, while opposite <edges> of the square can share a colour.}

For the <triangle in a graph> $K_3$ with <edges> $ab,bc,ca$, every pair of <edges> shares a <vertex>, so all three require different labels. The assignment $c(ab)=1,c(bc)=2,c(ca)=3$ works, proving $\chi\prime(K_3)=3$ although $\Delta(K_3)=2$. In its <line graph>, those three <edges> become a three-<vertex> <clique>. Treating this as three pair-resource jobs makes the obstruction tangible: each resource has only two jobs, but every pair of jobs conflicts, so no slot can contain two jobs.

For the square <cycle graph> $C_4$ with <edges> $ab,bc,cd,da$, use $c(ab)=c(cd)=1$ and $c(bc)=c(da)=2$. Each <colour class> is a <matching in a graph>, and each <vertex> has one <edge> of each colour. Since $\Delta(C_4)=2$, this proves $\chi\prime(C_4)=2$. More generally, alternation closes on any even <cycle graph> $C_{2m}$, $m\geq2$. On an <odd cycle> $C_{2m+1}$, $m\geq1$, two-colour alternation would make the last and first <edges> share a colour at their common <vertex>; a third colour on the last <edge> suffices. Thus even <cycle graphs> have <chromatic index> two and odd <cycle graphs> have <chromatic index> three. These exact examples show why <maximum degree of a graph> alone does not determine the answer.

= Chromatic index bounds
{parent=Chromatic index}
{title2=$\Delta(G)\leq\chi\prime(G)\leq\Delta(G)+1$}

For every finite <simple graph>, <Vizing's theorem> gives
$$
\Delta(G)\leq\chi\prime(G)\leq\Delta(G)+1.
$$
Here $\Delta(G)$ is the <maximum degree of a graph>, taken as zero when there are no <vertices>. A <bipartite graph> has the stronger equality $\chi\prime(G)=\Delta(G)$. The six-job <complete bipartite graph> above attains this lower bound, while the <triangle in a graph> attains the extra-colour alternative. The simple-<graph> restriction in the upper bound matters.<Graph Theory by Reinhard Diestel>[Colouring, author-hosted chapter preview, §5.3, p. 132: Proposition 5.3.1 and Theorem 5.3.2]{ref}

The <bipartite graph> equality still holds for finite loopless <multigraphs>, with parallel <edges> counted separately in each <vertex degree>. For instance, $r$ parallel jobs between two resources need exactly $r$ slots: every pair conflicts, and $\Delta=\chi\prime=r$. For a general finite loopless <multigraph>, however, the <simple graph> upper bound can fail. Put two parallel <edges> on each side of a <triangle in a graph>. All six <edges> pairwise share a <vertex>, so each needs its own colour; every <vertex> has <degree of a vertex> four. Consequently $\chi\prime=6>\Delta+1=5$. A multiplicity-aware bound is $\chi\prime(G)\leq\Delta(G)+\mu(G)$, where $\mu(G)$ is the largest number of parallel <edges> between a pair of <vertices>. The doubled <triangle in a graph> has $\mu=2$ and attains that bound. These statements exclude <self-loops>; the two-distinct-resource scheduling model excludes them too.<Line graphs and edge coloring>[Line graphs and edge coloring, slide 2: bipartite multigraph equality, multiplicity bound and fat triangle]{ref}

For historical orientation, <Graph Theory by Reinhard Diestel> (Colouring preview, §5.3, p. 132) dates the bipartite equality to 1916 and <Vizing's theorem> to 1964. These are textbook attributions; the original papers have not been inspected here. The results state existence of optimal or nearly optimal <proper edge colourings>, rather than saying that arbitrary greedy choices will find them. The <list-chromatic index> asks the further question of success for every sufficiently large family of allowed-colour lists.

= Tait coloring
{parent=Edge coloring}
{c}

= Tait colouring
{c}
{synonym}

A proper three-color <edge coloring> of a <cubic graph>. Label its colors by the nonzero elements of $\mathbb F_2^2$: the incident colors at each <vertex> then sum to zero. On a planar map without <bridges in a graph>, integrating these labels on the <dual graph> gives a four-color face coloring. On a <torus>, a nonzero period can obstruct this integration.

= Chromatic number
{parent=Graph coloring}
{wiki}
{title2=χ(G)}

For a nonempty finite <simple graph> $G$, the <chromatic number> $\chi(G)$ is the least positive <integer> $k$ for which $G$ has a proper vertex <graph coloring> with at most $k$ colours. To establish an exact <chromatic number>, give a colouring using that many colours and explain why fewer cannot suffice. A colouring alone gives only an upper bound.<Mathematics for Computer Science by Eric Lehman and others>[Lehman, Leighton and Meyer, Mathematics for Computer Science, Definition 11.7.1]{ref}

\Image[graph-coloring-cycle-examples.png]
{title=Optimal <graph colorings> of the <cycle graphs> $C_3,C_4,C_5$}
{description=Numbers inside dots are colour labels, and labels outside are <vertices>. The <cycle graph> $C_4$ alternates two colours. The <odd cycles> $C_3,C_5$ need three, although $C_5$ is a <triangle-free graph>.}

These small <graphs> show why the <chromatic number> measures more than the presence of <triangles in a graph>:

* The <cycle graph> $C_3$, also the <complete graph> $K_3$, has <chromatic number> three. Every pair of <vertices> is adjacent, so three distinct colours are necessary and sufficient.
* The <cycle graph> $C_4$ has <chromatic number> two. Around the <graph cycle>, the assignment $1,2,1,2$ is proper, and a single colour would violate every <edge>.
* The <cycle graph> $C_5$ has <chromatic number> three. With two colours, alternation around the <graph cycle> would force the first and fifth <vertices> to have the same colour despite their joining <edge>. The assignment $1,2,1,2,3$ works. Thus a <triangle-free graph> can still require more than two colours.
* The <complete graph> $K_n$ has <chromatic number> $n$: all $n$ <vertices> are pairwise adjacent, so each must have its own colour.https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/6-042j-mathematics-for-computer-science-fall-2005/5926091844fc3d60fa18eb75c9a2bc03_slides5w.pdf[Meyer, MIT Graph Coloring, slides 18–19]{ref}

For a <cycle graph> of even length, alternating two colours closes consistently; for an <odd cycle>, it does not, and a third colour on the final <vertex> suffices. More generally, the <two-colourability criterion for bipartite graphs> says that $\chi(G)\leq2$ exactly when $G$ is a <bipartite graph>, and the <odd-cycle characterization of bipartite graphs> identifies the obstruction as an <odd cycle>. A nonempty <graph> with no <edges> has <chromatic number> one; a <bipartite graph> with at least one <edge> has <chromatic number> exactly two.

In the pairwise-conflict scheduling model of <graph coloring>, $\chi(G)$ is the least number of interchangeable slots needed. This turns a drawing into an optimization question: how many groups of compatible activities are unavoidable? <Greedy coloring> constructs a valid grouping quickly, but its number of colours can exceed the <chromatic number>.

= Chromatic number of Euclidean space
{parent=Chromatic number}
{title2=$\chi(\mathbb R^n)$}

This is the least number of colors needed to color all points of <Euclidean space> so that points at <Euclidean distance> one receive different colors. Any finite unit-distance graph embedded in the space gives a lower bound. If $p$ is prime, the characteristic vectors of all $(2p-1)$-subsets of $[4p-1]$, divided by $\sqrt{2p}$, give such a graph: its edges are exactly pairs intersecting in $p-1$ points. The <prime-power modular intersection bound> bounds an independent set by $\binom{4p-1}{p-1}$, and hence gives $\chi(\mathbb R^{4p-1})\geq\binom{4p-1}{2p-1}/\binom{4p-1}{p-1}$.

= Colour-critical vertex
{parent=Chromatic number}
{title2=$\chi(F-v)=\chi(F)-1$}

A vertex $v$ is colour-critical if deleting it lowers the <chromatic number> by one. If $\chi(F)=r+1$ and $\chi(F-v)=r$, an embedding of $F-v$ into a complete $r$-partite graph inside one neighbourhood extends to an embedding of $F$. This supplies maximum-degree obstructions in <extremal graph theory>.

= Mycielskian
{c}
{parent=Chromatic number}
{wiki}

= Mycielski construction
{c}
{synonym}

The Mycielski construction replaces a graph $G$ with a graph $\mu(G)$ having one shadow vertex for each original vertex and one further vertex adjacent to every shadow. It satisfies
$$
\chi(\mu(G))=\chi(G)+1
$$
and preserves triangle-freeness. Iterating it from the five-cycle gives triangle-free graphs of arbitrarily large chromatic number.

= Graphs of arbitrarily high girth and chromatic number
{parent=Chromatic number}

For every $g,k\geq3$, there is a finite graph with no cycle of length at most $g$ and with chromatic number at least $k$. A probabilistic proof samples a sparse binomial random graph, observes that it has few short cycles and no large independent set, and deletes one vertex from every short cycle.

= Brooks' theorem
{c}
{parent=Chromatic number}
{wiki}

A connected graph of maximum degree $\Delta$ has chromatic number at most $\Delta$ unless it is a complete graph or an odd cycle.

= Edge chromatic number
{title2=$\chi'(G)$}
{parent=Graph coloring}

The edge chromatic number is the least number of colours required to colour edges so that incident edges receive different colours.

= Vizing's theorem
{c}
{parent=Edge chromatic number}
{wiki}

= Vizing theorem
{c}
{synonym}

Every finite simple graph of maximum degree $\Delta$ has edge chromatic number $\Delta$ or $\Delta+1$.

= Vizing fan lemma
{c}
{parent=Vizing's theorem}

In a partial proper edge colouring with one uncoloured edge incident with a vertex $x$, a Vizing fan is a sequence of neighbours of $x$ whose incident edge colours are missing at earlier fan vertices. With one more colour than the maximum degree, fan rotations and a two-colour <Kempe chain> free one common colour at the endpoints of the uncoloured edge.

= Kempe chain
{c}
{parent=Graph coloring}
{wiki}

For two colours in a proper graph colouring, a Kempe chain is a connected component of the subgraph using only those colours. Interchanging the two colours throughout one component preserves properness.

= Chromatic polynomial
{title2=$P_G(t)$}
{parent=Graph coloring}
{wiki}

The chromatic polynomial turns a <graph coloring> problem into a counting problem: **how many choices remain when adjacent <vertices> must have different colours?** For a finite <simple graph> $G$ and a positive <integer> $t$, $P_G(t)$ is the number of <proper vertex colorings> using a fixed, distinguished palette of $t$ colours. A colouring need not use every available colour, and exchanging two palette colours generally gives a different colouring. Although $P_G$ is a <polynomial>, its values at arbitrary noninteger arguments are not counts of colourings.

Consider the three-vertex <path graph> $A$--$B$--$C$. Choose $A$ in $t$ ways, then $B$ in $t-1$ ways, then $C$ in $t-1$ ways: $A$ and $C$ may agree because they are not adjacent. The <multiplication principle> gives
$$
P_{P_3}(t)=t(t-1)^2=t^3-2t^2+t.
$$
Adding the <edge> $AC$ makes the three-vertex <complete graph> $K_3$. Now $C$ must avoid both preceding colours, so
$$
P_{K_3}(t)=t(t-1)(t-2)=t^3-3t^2+2t.
$$
With three available colours, the <path graph> has **12 colourings**, whereas the triangle has **6**. With two colours the counts are $2$ and $0$: the extra constraint makes the triangle impossible to colour.

\Image[chromatic-polynomial-path-colorings.png]
{title=All twelve proper colourings of a three-vertex path using a three-colour palette}

For a nonempty <graph>, the <chromatic number> records only the first positive <integer> $t$ for which $P_G(t)>0$. The <chromatic polynomial> also counts the choices for every larger palette. For the path the <chromatic number> is $2$, and for the triangle it is $3$. Counting assignments also gives a useful interpretation for scheduling: if adjacent <vertices> represent tasks that must use different slots, the <chromatic polynomial> counts the valid assignments to $t$ named slots. Adding a conflict <edge> can only remove assignments.

The <chromatic polynomial> also remembers some of the graph's structure. For a <simple graph> with $n\geq 1$ <vertices> and $m$ <edges>, its leading terms are $t^n-mt^{n-1}$; its <degree of a polynomial> gives the number of <vertices>, and its next coefficient gives the number of <edges>. It does not determine the graph completely. A four-vertex <path graph> and a four-vertex <star graph> are not related by a <graph isomorphism>, but both are <trees> and have <chromatic polynomial> $t(t-1)^3$.

The early connection to map colouring appears in George D. Birkhoff's article https://www.jstor.org/stable/1967597[A Determinant Formula for the Number of Ways of Coloring a Map, Annals of Mathematics 14 (1912–1913), pp. 42–46]{ref}. For a derivation of the counting recurrence and its leading coefficients, see https://ptwiddle.github.io/MAS341-Graph-Theory-2017/lecturenotes/lecturex9.html[Paul Johnson's MAS 341 lecture on chromatic polynomials].

= Chromatic polynomial determines a Turán graph
{parent=Chromatic polynomial}

If a simple graph has the same <chromatic polynomial> as the <Turán graph> $T_r(n)$, it has the same number of vertices and edges, and the same <chromatic number> $r$. It is therefore $K_{r+1}$-free and attains equality in <Turán's theorem>. The equality case forces it to be isomorphic to $T_r(n)$.

= Deletion-contraction recurrence for the chromatic polynomial
{parent=Chromatic polynomial}
{title2=$P_G(t)=P_{G-e}(t)-P_{G/e}(t)$}

Deleting an edge removes one colour constraint. <Edge contraction> describes exactly the newly admitted colourings that violate that constraint. If $e$ joins distinct <vertices> $u,v$ of a <simple graph> $G$, write $G-e$ for the <graph> with that <edge> deleted and $G/e$ for the <graph> with $u,v$ identified and $e$ removed. Parallel edges produced by the contraction may be reduced to one edge, since they impose the same condition on a <proper vertex coloring>. Then
$$
P_G(t)=P_{G-e}(t)-P_{G/e}(t).
$$
To see why, divide the <proper vertex colorings> of $G-e$ into two classes. Those with different colours at $u,v$ are exactly the colourings of $G$. Those with equal colours at $u,v$ correspond one-to-one to colourings of $G/e$: the merged <vertex> receives their common colour. Subtracting the second class leaves the first.

For the triangle, delete $AC$ to get a three-vertex <path graph>, or contract $AC$ to get two <vertices> joined by an <edge> after removing the redundant parallel edge. Consequently,
$$
P_{K_3}(t)=t(t-1)^2-t(t-1)=t(t-1)(t-2).
$$
At $t=3$, this says **$6=12-6$**. Of the twelve path colourings pictured above, six give $A,C$ the same colour; these are precisely the ones ruled out when we restore $AC$.

\Image[chromatic-polynomial-deletion-contraction.png]
{title=Deleting and contracting a triangle edge separates the allowed and newly forbidden colourings}

This recurrence is useful because it reduces a constrained counting problem to smaller <graphs>. A <graph> on $n$ <vertices> with no <edges> has $P_G(t)=t^n$, since every <vertex> chooses its colour independently. Repeated <edge> deletion and <edge contraction> reduce to this case and show that the counting function is a <polynomial>. The same recurrence gives its leading coefficients. The method is conceptually simple, although the number of recursive cases can still grow rapidly.

= Chromatic polynomial after attaching a leaf
{parent=Chromatic polynomial}

Attaching a new <vertex> that is a <leaf of a graph> multiplies the <chromatic polynomial> by $t-1$: after colouring the old graph, the new <vertex> may receive any colour except its neighbour's. Starting with one <vertex> and repeatedly adding a <leaf of a graph> constructs any finite <tree>. Thus a <tree> with $n\geq 1$ <vertices> has
$$
P_T(t)=t(t-1)^{n-1}.
$$
In contrast, every pair of <vertices> in a <complete graph> must receive different colours, so the <multiplication principle> gives the <falling factorial>
$$
P_{K_n}(t)=t(t-1)\cdots(t-n+1).
$$
For positive <integers> $t<n$ this product vanishes, expressing the impossibility of assigning distinct colours to all $n$ <vertices>.

= Independent set
{disambiguate=graph theory}
{parent=Graph theory}
{wiki}

= Independent set
{synonym}

An <independent set> selects mutually compatible objects when an <edge> of a <graph> represents a conflict. For example, if <vertices> are activities and <edges> join activities that cannot occur together, an <independent set> is a collection that can share one time slot. Finding the largest such collection is measured by the <independence number>.https://introtcs.org/public/lec_12_NP.html[Barak, Introduction to Theoretical Computer Science, Chapter 14, The independent set problem]{ref}

In a finite <simple graph> $G=(V,E)$, an <independent set> is a <subset> $I\subseteq V$ with no <edge> joining two selected <vertices>:
$$
u,v\in I,\ u\ne v\Longrightarrow\{u,v\}\notin E.
$$
The empty <set> and every singleton <set> are <independent sets>. In the <cycle graph> $C_5$ drawn under <vertex cover>, $\{0,2\}$ is an <independent set>, but $\{0,4\}$ is not: the closing <edge> $40$ joins those two <vertices>. Choosing nonconsecutive labels is insufficient unless the wraparound <edge> is checked.

An <independent set> of maximum size has <cardinality> equal to the <independence number>. Being unable to add another <vertex> only makes an <independent set> inclusion-maximal. In the <star graph> $K_{1,3}$, the centre alone is inclusion-maximal, since it is adjacent to every leaf, but the three leaves form a larger <independent set>. This distinction mirrors the difference between an inclusion-minimal <vertex cover> and a <minimum vertex cover>.

Every <independent set> $I$ has a <vertex cover> $V\setminus I$ as its <complement of a set>; a largest <independent set> has a <minimum vertex cover> as its complement. In every <proper vertex colouring>, each <colour class> is an <independent set>, so <graph coloring> partitions the <vertices> into mutually compatible groups.

= Independent transversal
{parent=Independent set (graph theory)}

For a <graph> whose vertices are partitioned into parts, an independent transversal is an <independent set> containing exactly one vertex from each part. If a cycle's parts each have twelve vertices, choose one vertex uniformly and independently per part. A cycle edge joining distinct parts is selected at both ends with <probability> $1/144$. Each <event> depends on at most $47$ others, since each part meets at most $24$ cycle edges. The <Lovász local lemma> applies because $48e/144<1$, proving existence for an arbitrary such partition.

= Degree-ordered independent-set entropy bound
{parent=Independent set (graph theory)}
{title2=$i(G)\leq\prod_i(2^{b_i+1}-1)^{1/d_i}$}

Order vertices by nonincreasing positive degrees $d_i$, and let $b_i$ count preceding neighbors. The <Madiman-Tetali inequality> applied to preceding-neighbor sets and $b_i$ copies of singleton $i$ bounds the entropy of a uniformly chosen <independent set>. In each local term, all-zero neighbor assignment permits two choices at $i$, while every other assignment permits at most one. The maximum-entropy bound gives $\log_2(2^{b_i+1}-1)$ for that term. Complete bipartite graphs $K_{d,d}$ attain equality when one part is ordered before the other.

= Independence number
{title2=$\alpha(G)$}
{parent=Independent set (graph theory)}
{wiki}

The <independence number> is the largest <cardinality> of an <independent set> in a finite <simple graph>:
$$
\alpha(G)=\max\{|I|:I\subseteq V(G),\ I\text{ is an independent set of }G\}.
$$
It answers "how many mutually compatible objects can be selected at once?", while the <chromatic number> asks how many compatible groups are needed for all the <vertices>. Diestel uses this notation for a largest <independent set>.https://diestel-graph-theory.com/Sample.pdf[Diestel, Graph Theory, Chapter 2, Exercise 23]{ref}

For the <cycle graph> $C_5$, the <independent set> $\{0,2\}$ shows that $\alpha(C_5)\geq2$. Three selected <vertices> would require at least three unselected <vertices> separating them around the <cycle graph>, which has only five <vertices>. Thus $\alpha(C_5)=2$. There are five largest <independent sets>, obtained by rotating $\{0,2\}$ around the <cycle graph>, and five corresponding <minimum vertex covers>.

For the <complete bipartite graph> $K_{2,3}$, an <independent set> cannot contain a <vertex> from each side, since every such pair is joined by an <edge>. The larger side has three <vertices>, so $\alpha(K_{2,3})=3$. A <triangle in a graph> has <independence number> one. A <graph> with no <edges> and $n$ <vertices> has <independence number> $n$, including $\alpha(G)=0$ when $n=0$.

The complement correspondence between <vertex covers> and <independent sets> gives, with $n=|V(G)|$,
$$
\tau(G)+\alpha(G)=n.
$$
Every extra <vertex> that can be left in an <independent set> is one fewer <vertex> needed in a <minimum vertex cover>. This identity holds for all finite <simple graphs>, including the <triangle in a graph> and the <cycle graph> $C_5$; it does not require the <bipartite graph> hypothesis of <Kőnig's theorem for bipartite matching>.

For a nonempty <graph>, every <colour class> of a <proper vertex colouring> contains at most $\alpha(G)$ <vertices>. Counting all the <vertices> therefore gives
$$
\chi(G)\geq\frac{|V(G)|}{\alpha(G)}.
$$
Since the <chromatic number> is an <integer>, the bound rounds up: for the <cycle graph> $C_5$, $\chi(C_5)\geq\lceil5/2\rceil=3$. It is attained by assigning colour $A$ to $0,2$, colour $B$ to $1,3$, and colour $C$ to $4$. All five <edges>, including $40$, have differently coloured endpoints.

= Caro-Wei bound
{c}
{parent=Independence number}
{title2=$\alpha(G)\geq\sum_v\frac1{1+\deg v}$}

For a finite simple <graph>, $\alpha(G)\geq\sum_{v\in V(G)}1/(1+\deg v)\geq |V(G)|^2/(|V(G)|+2|E(G)|)$. Randomly order its <vertices> and retain each <vertex> preceding all its <graph neighbours>. This gives an <independent set> with the first expression as its <expected value>. The second inequality is the <Cauchy-Schwarz inequality>.

= Triangle-free graph with sub-power independence number
{parent=Independence number}

For every sufficiently large $n$, there is a triangle-free graph $G$ on $n$ vertices with $\alpha(G)<n^{0.7}$. One construction samples $G(2n,(2n)^{-0.69})$: with positive probability it has fewer than $n$ triangles and no independent set of size $n^{0.7}$. Delete one vertex from each triangle and then take an induced $n$-vertex subgraph.

= Greedy independent-set bound
{parent=Independent set (graph theory)}

Every finite graph of maximum degree at most $\Delta$ has an <independent set> of size at least $|V|/(\Delta+1)$. Greedily choose a vertex and delete its closed neighbourhood.

= Locally sparse graph independence bound
{parent=Independent set (graph theory)}

There is an absolute $c>0$ such that if an $n$-vertex graph has maximum degree at most $d$ and every vertex neighbourhood spans at most $d^2/f$ edges, where $2\leq f\leq d^2$, then
$$
\alpha(G)\geq c\frac{n\log f}{d}.
$$
The triangle-free case is commonly called Shearer's independence bound.

= Shearer independence bound for a triangle-free graph
{c}
{parent=Locally sparse graph independence bound}

Every triangle-free graph on $n$ vertices with maximum degree at most $d$ has
$$
\alpha(G)\geq c\frac{n\log d}{d}
$$
for an absolute constant $c>0$.

= Leaf of a graph
{parent=Graph theory}

A leaf is a vertex of degree one.

Thus a leaf is a <vertex of a graph> with <degree of a vertex> equal to one.

= Edge of a graph
{parent=Graph theory}

= Edge
{synonym}

An edge joins two vertices of a graph; in a simple graph it is an unordered pair of distinct vertices.

An <edge of a graph> joins endpoints in a <graph>. For a <simple graph> it is an unordered pair of distinct <vertices of a graph>; for a <directed graph> its endpoints are ordered.

= Path in a graph
{parent=Graph theory}

= Graph path
{synonym}

A path of length $t$ is a sequence of $t+1$ distinct vertices in which consecutive vertices are adjacent.

= Hamiltonian path
{parent=Path in a graph}
{c}
{wiki}

= Hamilton path
{c}
{synonym}

A <Hamiltonian path> is a <path in a graph> that visits every <graph vertex> exactly once. Removing any <edge> from a <Hamilton cycle> produces a <Hamiltonian path>. Conversely, a <Hamiltonian path> closes to a <Hamilton cycle> exactly when its endpoints are joined by an <edge>.

= Edge-disjoint paths
{parent=Path in a graph}

Paths are edge-disjoint when no graph edge occurs in two of them. They may share internal vertices. If $r$ edge-disjoint client-to-server paths exist, fewer than $r$ failed links cannot destroy them all. For simultaneous link and node failures one instead needs <internally vertex-disjoint paths>, since one shared internal node could destroy several edge-disjoint paths at once.

= Ray in a graph
{parent=Path in a graph}
{title2=$(v_0,v_1,\ldots)$}

= Graph ray
{synonym}

A <graph ray> is an infinite <sequence> $v_0,v_1,\ldots$ of distinct <graph vertices> with an <edge> between consecutive <graph vertices>. Every infinite connected <locally finite graph> contains a <graph ray> from each <graph vertex>, by the <König infinity lemma> applied to its finite self-avoiding <graph paths>. An open <graph ray> witnesses an infinite <percolation cluster>.

= Path graph
{parent=Path in a graph}
{title2=$P_n$}
{wiki}

A path graph $P_n$ has $n$ vertices in a single chain and $n-1$ edges. Its endpoint <graph distance> is $n-1$ and, with unit resistances, its endpoint <effective resistance> is also $n-1$.

= Cycle in a graph
{parent=Path in a graph}

= Graph cycle
{synonym}

= Cycles in a graph
{synonym}

A cycle is a closed path: its final vertex is joined back to its initial vertex, and no other vertex is repeated.

= Induced cycle
{parent=Cycle in a graph}

An <induced cycle> is a <cycle in a graph> whose <vertices> induce exactly its cycle <edges>, with no <edge> between nonconsecutive <vertices>. A triangle is an <induced cycle> of length three. A <chordal graph> forbids <induced cycles> of length at least four.

= Girth
{parent=Cycle in a graph}
{wiki}

The girth of a graph containing a cycle is the length of its shortest cycle. A forest is conventionally assigned infinite girth.

= Odd cycle
{parent=Cycle in a graph}

An odd cycle is a <cycle in a graph> with an <odd number> of edges.

= Cycle graph
{title2=$C_n$}
{parent=Cycle in a graph}
{wiki}

The cycle graph $C_n$ has vertices $v_1,\ldots,v_n$ and edges $v_iv_{i+1}$, with indices read cyclically.

= Wheel graph
{parent=Cycle graph}
{wiki}
{title2=$W_n$}

A wheel graph is the <join of graphs> of a <cycle graph> and a single <vertex>. Every wheel with a rim of at least three <vertices> has a $K_4$ <graph minor>: partition the rim into three nonempty consecutive connected sets and use its centre as the fourth <branch set of a graph minor>.

= Hamilton cycle
{parent=Cycle graph}
{c}

= Hamiltonian cycle
{c}
{synonym}

A <Hamilton cycle>, also called a <Hamiltonian cycle>, is a <cycle in a graph> containing every <vertex> of the <graph>. Here the convention is a finite <simple graph> whose <edges> are undirected, with $n=|V|\geq3$. A traversal is a <sequence>
$$
v_0,v_1,\ldots,v_{n-1},v_n=v_0,
$$
where $v_0,\ldots,v_{n-1}$ are all the distinct <vertices> and $\{v_i,v_{i+1}\}\in E$ for $0\leq i<n$. Thus "once" means once before the closing return: the start appears again at the end, and there are exactly $n$ traversed <edges>. The <graph> may contain additional unused <edges>.<Graph Theory by Reinhard Diestel/Hamilton Cycles>[Diestel, Graph Theory, Chapter 10, opening paragraph, printed p. 323: Hamilton cycles, Hamilton paths, and the contrast with Euler tours]{ref}

A <Hamiltonian path> visits all <vertices> once without requiring a return. Removing an <edge> from a <Hamilton cycle> gives a <Hamiltonian path>; a particular <Hamiltonian path> closes only if its endpoints are adjacent. An <Euler circuit> instead traverses every <edge> exactly once and can revisit <vertices> midway. The two requirements are independent, as these original labelled examples show.

\Image[hamilton-euler-vertex-edge-comparison.png]
{title=Two <graphs> separate <Hamilton cycle> vertex coverage from <Euler circuit> edge coverage; arrows mark traversal order, not directed <edges>}
{id=hamilton-euler-vertex-edge-comparison}

For the <complete graph> $K_4$, use $V=\{A,B,C,D\}$ and $E=\{AB,AC,AD,BC,BD,CD\}$, where $AB$ abbreviates the unordered pair $\{A,B\}$. The route $A,B,C,D,A$ is a <Hamilton cycle>: it visits every <vertex> and uses four of the six <edges>, omitting $AC$ and $BD$. It is not an <Euler circuit>, and the <graph> admits no <Euler circuit> at all: every <vertex degree> is three, contradicting the <Euler circuit criterion>. The crossing of the drawn diagonals is not another <vertex>.

Conversely, take two <triangles in a graph> sharing only $X$, with $V=\{X,A,B,C,D\}$ and $E=\{XA,AB,BX,XC,CD,DX\}$. The route $X,A,B,X,C,D,X$ is an <Euler circuit>, using all six <edges> once; the <vertex degrees> are $4,2,2,2,2$. It cannot be a <Hamilton cycle>, since $X$ occurs midway as well as at the start and end. More strongly, no <Hamilton cycle> exists: $X$ is a <cut vertex>, and deleting it leaves the two separate <edges> $AB$ and $CD$. Nevertheless, $A,B,X,C,D$ is a <Hamiltonian path>, whose endpoints are not adjacent.

= Hamiltonian graph
{c}
{parent=Hamilton cycle}

A <Hamiltonian graph> is a <graph> containing a <Hamilton cycle>. This describes existence of a spanning <graph cycle>, rather than requiring every <edge> of the <graph> to belong to that <graph cycle>.
