In the finite <simple graph> convention, $K_n$ is a <Hamiltonian graph> for $n\geq3$, because any ordering of its <vertices> closes using available <edges>.

= Necessary conditions for a Hamilton cycle
{parent=Hamilton cycle}

A <Hamiltonian graph> must be a <connected graph> and have <minimum degree> $\delta(G)\geq2$: each <vertex> has two distinct neighbours on its <Hamilton cycle>. It also has no <cut vertex>. Indeed, deleting any <vertex> from the <Hamilton cycle> leaves a <graph path> through every remaining <vertex>, so the remaining <graph> is connected. These are quick ways to rule out a <Hamilton cycle>.<Graph Theory by Reinhard Diestel/Hamilton Cycles>[Diestel, Graph Theory, §10.1, printed pp. 323–324: necessary minimum degree two and 2-connectivity]{ref}

Even these conditions together are not sufficient: the <complete bipartite graph> $K_{2,3}$ is connected, has <minimum degree> two and no <cut vertex>, but has no <Hamilton cycle>. Every <graph cycle> in a <bipartite graph> alternates between its two parts, using equally many <vertices> from each; it cannot use all two and all three.

In the other direction, <Dirac's theorem> gives a sufficient condition: a finite <simple graph> with $n\geq3$ and $\delta(G)\geq n/2$ has a <Hamilton cycle>. Failure of this inequality proves nothing by itself: the <cycle graph> $C_5$ has a <Hamilton cycle> despite $\delta(C_5)=2<5/2$.<Graph Theory by Reinhard Diestel/Hamilton Cycles>[Diestel, Graph Theory, §10.1, Theorem 10.1.1, printed p. 324]{ref}

= Weighted Hamilton cycle
{parent=Hamilton cycle}
{title2=$L(C)=\sum_{e\in E(C)}w_e$}

A <weighted Hamilton cycle> is a <Hamilton cycle> in a <weighted graph>, with cost equal to the sum of its traversed <edge> weights. Here weights represent symmetric nonnegative costs, rather than conductances. Asking whether a <Hamilton cycle> exists is a feasibility question; asking for the cheapest one is an instance of the <travelling salesman problem>. A <complete graph> supplies every possible connection between distinct <vertices>, making feasibility automatic for $n\geq3$, while choosing the cheapest visiting order remains a separate <mathematical optimization> problem.

\Image[weighted-hamilton-cycle-four-vertex-tours.png]
{title=A fictitious <weighted graph> on four <vertices>: its three <Hamilton cycles>, identified under reversal and cyclic rotation, have costs 14, 19 and 21}
{id=weighted-hamilton-cycle-four-vertex-tours}

Assign the <complete graph> $K_4$ the symmetric <edge> costs $w_{AB}=2$, $w_{BC}=3$, $w_{CD}=4$, $w_{AD}=5$, $w_{AC}=6$ and $w_{BD}=7$, in arbitrary units. Fix the start at $A$ and identify each <Hamilton cycle> with its reverse. The three possibilities are
$$
\begin{aligned}
A,B,C,D,A &: 2+3+4+5=14,\\
A,B,D,C,A &: 2+7+4+6=19,\\
A,C,B,D,A &: 6+3+7+5=21.
\end{aligned}
$$
These exhaust the possibilities: the <permutations> of the other three <vertices> give $3!=6$ orders, paired into three reversals. Hence the perimeter tour really is optimal for this toy instance. The diagram's geometric lengths are not costs, and no <triangle inequality> is assumed.

The usefulness of this model is to distinguish visiting required sites from covering required connections. If tasks are assigned to <vertices> and the route must return after one visit to each, a <weighted Hamilton cycle> captures the ordering requirement. If the job instead requires each <edge> once, the relevant object is an <Euler circuit>. The <shortest path problem> asks only for a cheapest route between specified endpoints. A 1954 primary research paper gave a concrete motivation for the <travelling salesman problem>: certify that a particular tour through 49 cities has minimum road-distance cost.<Solution of a Large-Scale Traveling-Salesman Problem by George B. Dantzig and others>[Solution of a Large-Scale Traveling-Salesman Problem by G. Dantzig, R. Fulkerson and S. Johnson, abstract; Journal of the Operations Research Society of America 2(4), pp. 393–410 (1954)]{ref}

These are abstract route models. If a cost stands for a whole <graph path> through an underlying network, travelling between consecutive task <vertices> can pass through intermediate network <vertices> more than once. One visit per task therefore does not imply one visit per physical junction. One-way connections require a <directed graph>; changing costs or extra timing constraints require a richer model than the fixed symmetric toy instance.

= Icosian game
{c}
{parent=Hamilton cycle}
{wiki}

The <Icosian game> made the <vertex>-visit puzzle tangible. The report of its 1857 presentation describes a plane projection of a regular <dodecahedron>, with holes at the <vertices> for ivory pins. The Trinity College Dublin archive connects this game with the search for closed routes visiting each <vertex> exactly once. This puzzle supplies a historical illustration of the <Hamilton cycle> requirement.<The Icosian Calculus, Hamilton papers archive>[The Icosian Calculus, Hamilton papers archive: opening historical introduction and quoted report of the 1857 British Association presentation: dodecahedron projection and ivory pins]{ref}

= Pancyclic graph
{parent=Cycle graph}
{wiki}

An $n$-vertex graph is pancyclic when it contains $C_\ell$ for every $3\leq\ell\leq n$.

= Long path from minimum degree
{parent=Path in a graph}

Every connected $n$-vertex graph of minimum degree $\delta$ contains a path of length at least $\min(2\delta,n-1)$.

= Erdős-Gallai path edge bound
{parent=Path in a graph}
{c}

An $n$-vertex graph containing no path of length $t$ has at most $(t-1)n/2$ edges.

This path-length bound is a different result from the <Erdős-Gallai theorem> on degree sequences.

= Subgraph
{parent=Graph theory}
{wiki}

A subgraph is obtained from a graph by selecting some of its vertices and edges while retaining every selected edge's endpoints.

= Spanning subgraph
{parent=Subgraph}
{wiki}

A spanning subgraph retains every vertex of the original graph and some or all of its edges. Adding the omitted edges can only enlarge the <graph neighbourhood> and the <external vertex boundary> of each fixed vertex set.

= Induced subgraph
{parent=Subgraph}
{wiki}

= Full subgraph
{synonym}

The subgraph induced by a vertex set $W$ contains every edge of the original graph whose two endpoints lie in $W$.

= Three-colourable two-thirds subgraph lemma
{parent=Subgraph}

Every finite graph $G$ has a three-colourable subgraph with at least $2e(G)/3$ edges. Give vertices three independent uniform colours and retain edges whose endpoints have different colours. Each edge survives with probability $2/3$, so the expected number surviving is $2e(G)/3$.

= Join
{disambiguate=graph theory}
{parent=Graph theory}
{wiki}

= Join of graphs
{synonym}

The join $\Gamma_1*\Gamma_2$ is obtained from the <disjoint union of graphs> $\Gamma_1\sqcup\Gamma_2$ by adding every edge between a vertex of $\Gamma_1$ and a vertex of $\Gamma_2$.

= Disjoint union of graphs
{parent=Graph theory}
{wiki}

The disjoint union $\Gamma_1\sqcup\Gamma_2$ has the vertices and edges of the two graphs and no edge joining the two parts.

= Complete graph
{parent=Graph theory}
{wiki}

The complete graph $K_r$ has $r$ vertices and every possible edge between distinct vertices.

= Clique
{disambiguate=graph theory}
{parent=Complete graph}
{wiki}

= Clique
{synonym}

= Complete subgraph
{synonym}

A clique in a graph is a vertex set whose induced subgraph is complete. A copy of $K_r$ is therefore an $r$-vertex clique.

= Maximal clique
{parent=Clique (graph theory)}

A maximal clique is a <clique> not strictly contained in another clique. This is distinct from a largest clique, whose size equals the <clique number>. In a triangulated <probabilistic graphical model>, maximal cliques are the natural nodes of a <junction tree>.

= Edge-disjoint clique packing
{parent=Clique (graph theory)}
{title2=$Z_r(G)$}

An edge-disjoint clique packing is a collection of $r$-<cliques> no two of which share an <edge>; they may share <vertices>. Its maximum cardinality $Z_r(G)$ changes by at most one when a single <edge> is toggled. Removing that <edge> destroys at most one packed <clique>, which makes this variable suitable for an <edge-exposure martingale>.

= Clique-packing amplification of moment bounds
{parent=Edge-disjoint clique packing}
{title2=$\Pr(Z_k=0)\le\exp(-c m^2/k^8)$}

Let $X$ count $k$-<cliques> in a <binomial random graph> on $m$ <vertices> and $Y$ count distinct ordered pairs sharing an <edge>. The <clique conflict graph> gives $Z_k\ge X^2/(X+Y)$: order its <vertices> randomly and keep each one preceding all its neighbours, then apply the <Cauchy-Schwarz inequality>. Another application gives $\mathbb EZ_k\ge(\mathbb EX)^2/(\mathbb EX+\mathbb EY)$. If $\mathbb EY/(\mathbb EX)^2=O(k^4/m^2)$ and $1/\mathbb EX$ is no larger, then $\mathbb EZ_k=\Omega(m^2/k^4)$. Changing one <edge> changes $Z_k$ by at most one, so the <Azuma-Hoeffding inequality> for an <edge-exposure martingale> yields the displayed exponential absence bound. This supports a <union bound> over exponentially many adaptive candidate subsets.

= Clique conflict graph
{parent=Edge-disjoint clique packing}

The clique conflict graph has one <vertex> for each $r$-<clique> of a fixed <graph>, and joins two when they share an <edge>. Its <independent sets> correspond exactly to <edge-disjoint clique packings>. The <Caro-Wei bound> converts clique-count and overlap estimates into a packing lower bound.

= Clique number
{parent=Clique (graph theory)}
{title2=$\omega(G)$}

The maximum number of vertices in a <clique> of a <graph>. If the graph contains $K_k$ and no $K_{k+1}$, its clique number is exactly $k$, since every larger <clique> contains a $(k+1)$-<clique>.

= Adjacency-matrix quadratic relation for a complete graph
{parent=Complete graph}

For the adjacency matrix $A$ of $K_n$,
$$
A=J-I,
\qquad
A^2=(n-2)A+(n-1)I.
$$
Hence $I,A,A^2$ are linearly dependent for every $n\geq2$.

= Complete multipartite graph
{parent=Graph theory}
{wiki}

A complete multipartite graph partitions its vertices into independent sets and joins every pair of vertices from different parts.

= Octahedral graph
{parent=Complete multipartite graph}
{title2=$K_{2,2,2}$}

The octahedral graph is the <graph> obtained from $K_6$ by removing a <perfect matching>; equivalently it is $K_{2,2,2}$. Its <chromatic number> is three, and it contains two vertex-disjoint <triangles in a graph>. A <complete bipartite graph> with a single additional internal edge cannot contain it: every triangle in that host uses the additional edge.

= Balancing a multipartite edge-triangle objective
{parent=Complete multipartite graph}
{title2=$F=\sum_{i<j}a_ia_j-c\sum_{i<j<k}a_ia_ja_k$}

With two part sizes of fixed sum and all other sizes fixed, this objective has form constant plus $(1-cB)a_ia_j$, where $B$ is the sum of the other sizes. A nonpositive coefficient permits merging the pair, while a positive coefficient favours balancing it. Choose an optimum with fewest nonzero parts: all remaining coefficients are positive, and the integer sizes differ by at most one. For real sizes they are exactly equal. This explains extremizers among <Turan graphs>.

= Balanced complete multipartite blow-up
{parent=Complete multipartite graph}
{title2=$K_r(t)$}

The balanced complete multipartite blow-up $K_r(t)$ replaces each <vertex> of the <complete graph> $K_r$ by an <independent set> of $t$ <vertices>, placing every possible <edge> between different sets and none inside a set. Every fixed <graph> of <chromatic number> at most $r$ embeds into $K_r(t)$ for sufficiently large $t$.

= Laplacian matrix
{c}
{title2=$L$}
{parent=Graph theory}
{wiki}

= Graph Laplacian
{c}
{synonym}

The <Graph Laplacian> converts local differences along <edges> into a <linear map>. Throughout this section, $G=(V,E)$ is a finite <undirected graph> with no <self-loops> or repeated <edges>, and each <edge> has weight one. In a fixed order of <vertices>, let $A$ be the <adjacency matrix> and $D$ the <degree matrix>. The combinatorial <Graph Laplacian> is
$$
L=D-A,\qquad (Lx)_i=\sum_{j:\{i,j\}\in E}(x_i-x_j).
$$
Thus the value at a <vertex> is compared with its neighbours. Every row sums to zero, so $L\mathbf1=0$: a constant <vector> produces no differences. This definition and the associated <Graph Laplacian quadratic form> are developed in Spielman's authored survey.https://www.cs.yale.edu/homes/spielman/PAPERS/SGTChapter.pdf[Spielman, Spectral Graph Theory, Sections 16.3.1–16.3.2]{ref}

For the three-<vertex> <path graph> $A-B-C$, the <vertex degrees> are $1,2,1$, and in that order
$$
A=\begin{pmatrix}0&1&0\\1&0&1\\0&1&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad
D=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\0&2&0\\0&0&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
L=\begin{pmatrix}1&-1&0\\-1&2&-1\\0&-1&1\end{pmatrix}.
$$
Direct multiplication gives three <eigenvectors> and their <eigenvalues>:
$$
L\begin{pmatrix}1\\1\\1\end{pmatrix}=0,\qquad
L\begin{pmatrix}1\\0\\-1\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1\\0\\-1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
L\begin{pmatrix}1\\-2\\1\end{pmatrix}=3\begin{pmatrix}1\\-2\\1\end{pmatrix}.
$$
The constant <eigenvector> has <eigenvalue> zero; the other two describe progressively stronger neighbour disagreement. Dividing these <eigenvectors> by $\sqrt3,\sqrt2,\sqrt6$, respectively, gives an <orthonormal basis>. The four-<vertex> <cycle graph> is already worked out under <circulant matrix>, where <Fourier diagonalization of a circulant matrix> gives its <eigenvalues>.

Every formula here uses $L=D-A$ without degree normalization. For comparison, Chung's treatment uses the degree-scaled <matrix> $D^{-1/2}LD^{-1/2}$, with a convention for isolated <vertices>; its <eigenvalues> must not be substituted into the examples here.https://www.math.ucsd.edu/~fan/cbms.pdf[Chung, Lectures on Spectral Graph Theory, Section 1.2]{ref}

= Weighted graph Laplacian
{title2=$L_w$}
{parent=Laplacian matrix}
{c}

For a finite <undirected graph> without <self-loops>, give each <edge> a symmetric nonnegative weight $w_{ij}=w_{ji}$. In the <weighted graph>, set $(A_w)_{ij}=w_{ij}$ for an <edge> and zero otherwise, and $(D_w)_{ii}=\sum_jw_{ij}$ with all other entries zero. The <weighted graph Laplacian> is
$$
L_w=D_w-A_w,\qquad
(L_wx)_i=\sum_jw_{ij}(x_i-x_j),\qquad
x^TL_wx=\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E}w_{ij}(x_i-x_j)^2.
$$
Each <edge> appears once in the <quadratic form>. For the <path graph> $A-B-C$ with weights $w_{AB}=2$ and $w_{BC}=3$,
$$
L_w=\begin{pmatrix}2&-2&0\\-2&5&-3\\0&-3&3\end{pmatrix},\qquad
x=\begin{pmatrix}2\\0\\-1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
x^TL_wx=2(2-0)^2+3(0-(-1))^2=11.
$$
The <weighted graph Laplacian> is still a <positive semidefinite matrix>. Its <kernel> consists of <vectors> constant on each <connected component of a graph> after zero-weight <edges> are removed: an <edge> of weight zero imposes no constraint. In an <electrical network>, taking the weights to be <electrical conductances> makes $x^TL_wx$ the total power dissipated by <Joule heating> when $x$ gives the <voltages> at the <vertices>. This physical interpretation explains why stronger connections penalize disagreement more heavily.https://www.cs.yale.edu/homes/spielman/PAPERS/SGTChapter.pdf[Spielman, Spectral Graph Theory, Section 16.3.2: resistor networks]{ref}

= Degree matrix
{parent=Laplacian matrix}
{title2=$D=\operatorname{diag}(d_1,\ldots,d_n)$}

The <degree matrix> is the <diagonal matrix> whose $i$th diagonal entry is the <degree of a vertex> $i$. For a finite <simple graph>, $d_i=\sum_jA_{ij}$, where $A$ is its <adjacency matrix>. For a <weighted graph Laplacian>, use the sum of incident <edge> weights instead of the number of incident <edges>.

= Graph Laplacian quadratic form
{parent=Laplacian matrix}
{title2=$x^TLx=\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E}(x_i-x_j)^2$}

Assign a real value $x_i$ to each <vertex>. The <Graph Laplacian quadratic form> measures disagreement across <edges>:
$$
x^TLx=\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E}(x_i-x_j)^2\geq0.
$$
This makes the <Graph Laplacian> a <positive semidefinite matrix>. The <energy> is small when neighbours have similar values, even if distant <vertices> have very different values; it depends on connections rather than where the <graph> is drawn. In the three-<vertex> <path graph>, take $x=(2,0,-1)^T$. Then
$$
Lx=(2,-1,-1)^T,\qquad
x^TLx=2\cdot2+0\cdot(-1)+(-1)\cdot(-1)=5,
$$
and the two <edges> contribute $(2-0)^2=4$ and $(0-(-1))^2=1$.

\Image[graph-laplacian-edge-energy.png]
{title=Edge disagreement and eigenvectors of the three-vertex path Laplacian}

For a short proof, expand $x^T(D-A)x$. Each <edge> contributes $x_i^2+x_j^2$ through the <degree matrix> and $-2x_ix_j$ through the <adjacency matrix>, yielding $(x_i-x_j)^2$. The sum over unordered <edges> has no factor $1/2$; a sum over all ordered pairs does:
$$
x^TLx=\frac12\sum_{i,j}A_{ij}(x_i-x_j)^2.
$$
Equivalently, let $B$ be an <oriented incidence matrix>, with one row per <edge>. Then $Bx$ records endpoint differences and $L=B^TB$, independently of the chosen orientations. This translates a local comparison into a global <quadratic form> and makes its nonnegativity visible.

= Kernel of a graph Laplacian
{parent=Laplacian matrix}
{title2=$\dim\ker L=\text{number of connected components}$}

The <kernel> of the <Graph Laplacian> consists exactly of <vectors> constant on each <connected component of a graph>. Indeed, $Lx=0$ implies $x^TLx=0$, so the <Graph Laplacian quadratic form> forces equality at the endpoints of every <edge>; equality then propagates along <graph paths>. Conversely, these equalities make every entry of $Lx$ zero. Thus the <dimension> of the <kernel>, equivalently the multiplicity of the zero <eigenvalue>, counts the <connected components of a graph>.

For the disconnected <graph> with just two <edges>, $AB$ and $CD$, in order $A,B,C,D$,
$$
L=\begin{pmatrix}1&-1&0&0\\-1&1&0&0\\0&0&1&-1\\0&0&-1&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
\ker L=\operatorname{span}\left\{\begin{pmatrix}1\\1\\0\\0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0\\0\\1\\1\end{pmatrix}\right\}.
$$
The two displayed <vectors> form a <basis> of the <kernel>. The other two <eigenvectors> $(1,-1,0,0)^T$ and $(0,0,1,-1)^T$ each have <eigenvalue> two, so the full list of <eigenvalues>, including multiplicities, is $0,0,2,2$. Separate <connected components of a graph> allow independent constant levels; a single global constant is only one of these possibilities.

\Image[graph-laplacian-connected-components.png]
{title=Two disconnected edges give two independent zero eigenvectors}

= Diffusion on a graph
{parent=Laplacian matrix}
{title2=$\dot x=-Lx$}

The continuous-time <diffusion> model on a finite <undirected graph> uses the <Graph Laplacian> in the <ordinary differential equation>
$$
\dot x=-Lx,\qquad \dot x_i=\sum_{j:\{i,j\}\in E}(x_j-x_i).
$$
Across each <edge>, the higher value sends an amount proportional to the difference to the lower value. The two contributions cancel in the sum over <vertices>, giving <conservation of mass>:
$$
\frac{d}{dt}\sum_i x_i(t)=-\mathbf1^TLx(t)=0.
$$
If the entries represent masses, start with nonnegative entries; signed entries can instead describe deviations from a common level. For the three-<vertex> <path graph>, the initial <vector> $x(0)=(1,0,-1)^T$ is an <eigenvector> with <eigenvalue> one, so the exact solution is
$$
x(t)=e^{-t}(1,0,-1)^T.
$$
More generally, an <eigenvector> with <eigenvalue> $\lambda$ decays by $e^{-\lambda t}$. The <spectral theorem for real symmetric matrices> supplies an <orthonormal basis> of these <eigenvectors>, and the <kernel of a graph Laplacian> identifies the surviving terms. On a nonempty <connected graph>,
$$
\lim_{t\to\infty}x(t)=\bar x\mathbf1,\qquad \bar x=\frac1{|V|}\sum_i x_i(0).
$$
The limiting value is the original <arithmetic mean>. On a disconnected <graph>, each <connected component of a graph> converges to its own original <arithmetic mean>. The same <matrix> therefore measures neighbour disagreement, detects disconnected regions and governs relaxation toward agreement.

= Laplacian spectrum of a complete graph
{parent=Laplacian matrix}

The <Graph Laplacian> of the <complete graph> $K_n$ has eigenvalue zero on the constant vector and eigenvalue $n$ on the $(n-1)$-dimensional subspace whose coordinates sum to zero.

= Minimum-cost flow problem
{parent=Graph theory}
{wiki}

= Minimum-cost flow
{synonym}

A minimum-cost flow minimizes a linear edge cost subject to vertex flow balances and edge capacity intervals. Subtracting every lower capacity from its edge flow converts all lower bounds to zero while shifting the balance vector and objective by constants.

= Two-resource path packing as minimum-cost circulation
{parent=Minimum-cost flow problem}

Consider nonnegative route variables with constraints $u\leq a$, $v\leq b$, $v+w\leq c$, $w+z\leq d$, and a linear reward $p_u u+p_v v+p_w w+p_z z$. It is a <minimum-cost flow> problem: use arcs $s\to t$ of capacity $a$ and cost $-p_u$, $s\to L$ of capacity $c$ and cost zero, $L\to t$ of capacity $b$ and cost $-p_v$, $L\to R$ of unlimited capacity and cost $-p_w$, $s\to R$ of unlimited capacity and cost $-p_z$, and $R\to t$ of capacity $d$ and cost zero. Close the network by an unlimited zero-cost arc $t\to s$. The four forward path flows are exactly $u,v,w,z$, so conservation gives the resource constraints. This reduction does not assert that general multi-commodity flow is ordinary minimum-cost flow.

= Feasible flow
{parent=Minimum-cost flow problem}
{title2=$Bf=b,\quad\ell\leq f\leq u$}

A feasible flow satisfies every <flow balance> and every capacity interval of a network problem. Feasibility is distinct from optimality: <capacitated flow optimality conditions> additionally certify that no allowed balanced flow displacement reduces the cost. With only one independent <graph cycle>, every balanced flow is determined by a single cycle parameter, and its feasible set is the intersection of the induced parameter intervals.

= Capacitated flow optimality conditions
{parent=Minimum-cost flow problem}
{title2=$r_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$}

For positive capacities and a feasible flow, optimality is equivalent to <network dual potentials> for which the <network reduced cost> is nonnegative at a lower bound, zero in the interior, and nonpositive at an upper bound. These signs make every feasible displacement nonnegative in total cost. They are also equivalent to absence of negative-cost cycles in the <residual network>. A zero-capacity edge is fixed and imposes no reduced-cost sign condition.

= Flow balance
{title2=$\sum_j f_{ij}-\sum_jf_{ji}=b_i$}
{parent=Minimum-cost flow problem}

A flow balance prescribes net outgoing minus incoming flow at a vertex. Positive $b_i$ is supply and negative $b_i$ is demand; zero is ordinary <flow conservation>. Summing all balances requires total supply to equal total demand. In a <directed graph>, these equations are represented by the <oriented incidence matrix>.

= Uncapacitated minimum-cost flow
{title2=$\min\{c^Tf:Bf=b,\ f\geq0\}$}
{parent=Minimum-cost flow problem}

An uncapacitated <minimum-cost flow> has nonnegative edge flows without finite upper capacities. The <flow balances> prescribe net supply at each vertex. For the <oriented incidence matrix> convention with tail $+1$ and head $-1$, vertex <network dual potentials> have <network reduced costs> $c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$. Nonnegative <network reduced costs> and <complementary slackness> certify a feasible optimum. A feasible negative-cost directed cycle makes the problem unbounded below.

= Network dual potential
{title2=$\pi_i$}
{parent=Uncapacitated minimum-cost flow}

A vertex potential in the dual of an <uncapacitated minimum-cost flow> yields the edge inequalities $\pi_i-\pi_j\leq c_{ij}$. Its objective is $\sum_i\pi_i b_i$. Adding a common constant leaves all <network reduced costs> and the objective unchanged when total net supply is zero. Potentials tight on a <spanning tree> are fixed up to this common constant.

= Dual network potentials from shortest-path distances
{parent=Network dual potential}
{title2=$\pi_i=-d_i,\quad c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j\ge0$}

In a directed network with no negative-cost directed cycle, add a source having zero-cost arcs to all vertices and compute shortest-path distances $d_i$. The inequalities $d_j\le d_i+c_{ij}$ make $\pi_i=-d_i$ dual feasible for <uncapacitated minimum-cost flow>. Shortest-path tree arcs are tight. An arbitrary spanning tree also fixes tight candidate potentials up to a constant, but it does not guarantee the non-tree inequalities; these must be checked.

= Network reduced cost
{title2=$r_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$}
{parent=Network dual potential}

With the tail-positive incidence convention, a network edge's reduced cost is $r_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$. For <uncapacitated minimum-cost flow>, dual feasibility requires $r_{ij}\geq0$ and <complementary slackness> requires $f_{ij}r_{ij}=0$. A negative non-tree reduced cost identifies an improving <graph cycle> pivot in the <network simplex algorithm>. This sign convention differs from the row-column potentials of a <transportation problem>.

= Network simplex algorithm
{parent=Minimum-cost flow problem}

= Network simplex
{synonym}

= Network simplex method
{synonym}

The network simplex algorithm specializes the <simplex algorithm> to network flows. A basis is represented by a spanning tree with nonbasic flows at their bounds; potentials give <reduced costs>, and entering arcs produce cycle pivots. <Degeneracy in linear programming> is particularly common in assignment instances.

= Network simplex tree basis
{parent=Network simplex algorithm}

For a connected capacitated <minimum-cost flow> network, a <spanning tree> supplies basic flow variables. Every non-tree edge is fixed at its lower or upper bound. <Flow balance> determines the tree flows; these must also obey <capacity constraints>. An entering edge creates one cycle, and adjusting flow on that cycle preserves balance until a bound is hit and an edge leaves the tree.

= Extremal graph theory
{parent=Graph theory}
{wiki}

= Supersaturation
{parent=Extremal graph theory}

Supersaturation strengthens an extremal existence theorem by forcing many copies of a forbidden graph when the edge count exceeds its extremal threshold by a fixed density. For a fixed clique, the <Erdős-Stone theorem> and averaging over bounded-size random vertex subsets give a positive multiple of $n^{|V(F)|}$ copies.

= Clique supersaturation by sampling
{parent=Supersaturation}
{title2=$e(G)\ge(1-1/r+\epsilon)\binom n2\Longrightarrow k_{r+1}(G)\ge\eta n^{r+1}$}

Choose a fixed sample size $m$ for which the normalized <Turan theorem> bound lies below the given density by a positive margin. The expected <edge> count in a random $m$-vertex subset then forces a positive proportion of subsets to contain a forbidden-size <clique>. Each <clique> belongs to a known number of subsets, so double counting gives a positive constant times $n^{r+1}$ <cliques>.

= Extremal number
{title2=$\operatorname{ex}(n,H)$}
{parent=Extremal graph theory}

The extremal number $\operatorname{ex}(n,H)$ is the largest number of edges in an $n$-vertex graph containing no subgraph isomorphic to $H$.

= Extremal graph for disjoint cliques
{parent=Extremal number}

For fixed positive integers $r,s$ and sufficiently large $n$, the unique extremal <graph> forbidding $s$ vertex-disjoint copies of the <complete graph> $K_{r+1}$ is the <join of graphs> $K_{s-1}+T_r(n-s+1)$, up to <isomorphic graphs>. Each forbidden <clique> would require a distinct <vertex> of $K_{s-1}$.

= Monotonicity of the normalized extremal number
{parent=Extremal number}

For every fixed graph $H$, the sequence
$$
\frac{\operatorname{ex}(n,H)}{\binom n2}
$$
is nonincreasing once $n\geq2$. Indeed, choose a uniformly random $m$-vertex <induced subgraph> of an extremal $H$-free graph on $n$ vertices. It remains $H$-free and has expected edge count
$$
\operatorname{ex}(n,H)\frac{\binom m2}{\binom n2},
$$
which cannot exceed $\operatorname{ex}(m,H)$.

= Mantel theorem
{c}
{parent=Extremal number}

Every triangle-free graph on $n$ vertices has at most $\lfloor n^2/4\rfloor$ edges.

This is the triangle-free case of <Turán's theorem>.

= Triangle lower bound one edge above the Mantel threshold
{parent=Mantel theorem}
{c}

Every graph on $2n$ vertices with at least $n^2+1$ edges contains at least $n$ triangles. An induction deletes the endpoints of an edge lying in no triangle; if every edge lies in a triangle, the <edge-triangle incidence bound> is already sufficient.

= Turán's theorem
{c}
{parent=Extremal graph theory}
{wiki}

= Turan theorem
{c}
{synonym}

Among $n$-vertex graphs containing no $K_{r+1}$, the maximum number of edges is attained by the complete $r$-partite graph whose part sizes differ by at most one, denoted $T_r(n)$.

= Edit-distance stability for clique-free graphs
{parent=Turán's theorem}
{title2=$e(G)\ge t_r(n)-k\Longrightarrow |E(G)\triangle E(H)|\le3k$}

A clique-free <graph> near the <Turan theorem> <edge> bound is close to a complete multipartite <graph>. Choose a maximum-degree <vertex>, split its neighbourhood from the remaining <vertices>, and apply induction inside the neighbourhood. The degree bound implies that missing cross <edges> are at least twice the number of <edges> inside the remaining class. This inequality controls all three types of edits and gives the constant three.

= Bipartite stability from few odd cycles
{parent=Edit-distance stability for clique-free graphs}
{title2=$|E(G)\triangle E(H)|\le11\epsilon\binom n2$}

Fix an odd cycle length. If the <graph> has <edge> density at least $1/2-\epsilon$ and sufficiently few copies of that cycle, <odd-cycle copies from positive triangle density> forces few <triangles in a graph>. The <triangle removal lemma> deletes at most $\epsilon\binom n2$ <edges>, after which the clique-free edit bound applies with two parts. Absorbing its linear rounding error into the density margin gives the displayed bound for large order.

= Turán graph
{title2=$T_r(n)$}
{c}
{parent=Turán's theorem}
{wiki}

The Turan graph $T_r(n)$ is the <complete multipartite graph> on $n$ vertices with $r$ parts whose sizes differ by at most one.

= Triangle support line between bipartite and tripartite Turan graphs
{parent=Turán graph}

For $n\geq3$, put $e_i=e(T_i(n))$, $t_3=k_3(T_3(n))$, and $c_n=(e_3-e_2)/t_3$. Every <graph> on $n$ <vertices> satisfies
$$
e(G)-c_n k_3(G)\leq e_2.
$$
Thus an <edge> count $e_2+\theta(e_3-e_2)$ forces at least $\theta t_3$ <triangles in a graph>. This is an exact finite bound, with the rounding in the <Turan graph> retained. To prove it, use <edge-triangle symmetrization>. The inequality $c_n\geq2/n$ allows merging the two smallest classes when there are at least four classes. With three classes, fixing the middle class makes the objective affine in the product of the other two sizes; either merge them or balance them. Only $T_2(n)$ and $T_3(n)$ need remain.

= Zykov symmetrization
{c}
{parent=Turán's theorem}

Zykov symmetrization replaces one of two nonadjacent vertices by a clone of the other. Cloning the vertex of larger degree does not decrease the edge count and does not create a larger clique. Iteration reduces the extremal problem for clique-free graphs to complete multipartite graphs.

= Edge-triangle symmetrization
{parent=Zykov symmetrization}

For each real $c$, some maximizer of $e(G)-c k_3(G)$ among <graphs> on $n$ <vertices> is <complete multipartite graph>. Among maximizers maximize $\sum_v d(v)^2$. Cloning either of two nonadjacent <vertices> preserves the objective, since maximality forces their local contributions $d(v)-c e(G[N(v)])$ to agree. If their <vertex neighbourhoods> differ, the sum of the two changes in the squared-<degree of a vertex> objective is twice the size of their symmetric difference, a contradiction. Nonadjacency therefore partitions the <vertices> into classes with identical <vertex neighbourhoods>.

= Quadratic Turan edge bound
{parent=Turán's theorem}
{c}

Every $n$-vertex graph containing no $K_{r+1}$ satisfies
$$
e(G)\leq\left(1-\frac1r\right)\frac{n^2}{2}.
$$

= Rhombus-free edge bound
{parent=Turán's theorem}

A graph-theoretic rhombus is two triangles sharing an edge. Every rhombus-free graph on $n\geq4$ vertices has at most
$$
e(T_2(n))=\left\lfloor\frac{n^2}{4}\right\rfloor
$$
edges. If the graph is triangle-free this is Turán's theorem. Otherwise remove a triangle: every remaining vertex has at most one neighbor in it, and induction bounds the edge count by
$\lfloor(n-3)^2/4\rfloor+n\leq\lfloor n^2/4\rfloor$.

= Triangular prism graph
{parent=Rhombus-free edge bound}

The triangular prism has six vertices and nine edges. Each edge lies in at most one triangle, so it has no rhombus; its two triangles show that it is not $K_{3,3}=T_2(6)$.

This is the <prism graph> built from two triangles.

= Erdős-Stone theorem
{c}
{parent=Extremal graph theory}
{wiki}

For every graph $H$ with chromatic number $r+1$,
$$
\operatorname{ex}(n,H)=\left(1-\frac1r+o(1)\right)\binom n2.
$$

= Logarithmic Erdős-Stone theorem
{parent=Erdős-Stone theorem}
{title2=$t\ge\lfloor d(r,\epsilon)\log n\rfloor$}

= Erdős-Stone-Bollobás theorem
{c}
{synonym}

An <edge> density exceeding the <Turan theorem> threshold for $K_{r+1}$ by a fixed positive amount forces a balanced $K_{r+1}(t)$ subgraph with logarithmic part size. Combine <clique supersaturation by sampling> with the <dense clique family blow-up lemma>. Dense binomial random <graphs> show that the logarithmic order is optimal for a uniform density guarantee.

= Logarithmic clique blow-up from minimum degree
{parent=Logarithmic Erdős-Stone theorem}
{title2=$\delta(G)\ge(1-1/r+\varepsilon)n\Longrightarrow K_{r+1}(\lfloor d\log n\rfloor)\subseteq G$}

A fixed positive excess over the <Turan theorem> threshold forces logarithmic balanced <complete multipartite graphs>. There is an elementary induction using <common neighbourhood from bipartite density>: first obtain a logarithmic $r$-partite subgraph from the induction hypothesis, and select disjoint transversal $r$-cliques in it. Each such clique has at least $r\varepsilon n$ <common neighbours>, by the <union bound> on non-neighbour sets. Apply the bipartite common-neighbour count to these cliques as left-side objects. Their selected union and a large common neighbourhood form the extra part.

= Random obstruction to larger clique blow-ups
{parent=Logarithmic Erdős-Stone theorem}
{title2=$\mathbb E\#K_s(t)\le n^{st}p^{\binom s2t^2}$}

In a <binomial random graph> with fixed <edge> probability below one, the displayed first-moment bound tends to zero for $t=C\log n$ and sufficiently large fixed $C$. At the same time the <edge> density concentrates near $p$. Thus a positive fixed density cannot force balanced complete multipartite subgraphs whose part size grows faster than logarithmically.

= High-minimum-degree multipartite stability subgraph
{parent=Erdős-Stone theorem}

For fixed positive integers $r,t$, a <graph> $G$ on $n$ <vertices> with $e(G)=(1-1/r+o(1))n^2/2$ and no <balanced complete multipartite blow-up> $K_{r+1}(t)$ contains an $r$-partite <subgraph> $H$ with $n-o(n)$ <vertices>, balanced classes of size $n/r+o(n)$, and <minimum degree of a graph> $\delta(H)=(1-1/r+o(1))n$.

One proof first removes a vanishing fraction of <vertices> of low <degree of a vertex>, finds a slowly growing $K_r(L)$ by the <Erdős-Stone theorem>, and assigns almost every remaining <vertex> to a root class in which it has fewer than $t$ <neighbours of a vertex>. After discarding a further vanishing fraction, a $K_{t,t}$ inside an assigned class would extend to $K_{r+1}(t)$ using the root classes. Thus internal <edges> and missing cross-class <edges> both number $o(n^2)$. Removing the <vertices> with unusually many missing cross-class <edges> gives the stated <minimum degree of a graph>.

= Minimum degree of an extremal forbidden-subgraph graph
{parent=High-minimum-degree multipartite stability subgraph}

If a fixed <graph> $F$ has <chromatic number> $r+1$, every extremal $F$-free <graph> $G$ on $n$ <vertices> has <minimum degree of a graph> $(1-1/r+o(1))n$. Use a <high-minimum-degree multipartite stability subgraph> $H$: replacing any <vertex> by one adjacent to all but one class of $H$ preserves $F$-freeness. Any supposed new copy of $F$ can replace the new <vertex> by an unused <common neighbour> in the omitted class. Extremality therefore gives the lower bound on every <degree of a vertex>; the <Erdős-Stone theorem> gives the upper bound on the average <degree of a vertex>.

= Kővári–Sós–Turán theorem
{c}
{parent=Extremal graph theory}
{wiki}

For each fixed positive integer $t$,
$$
\operatorname{ex}(n,K_{t,t})=O\left(n^{2-1/t}\right).
$$
The standard proof counts $t$-element subsets of <graph neighbourhood>[graph neighbourhoods] in a bipartite subgraph. If one side has degrees $d_y$, the absence of $K_{t,t}$ gives
$$
\sum_y\binom{d_y}{t}\leq(t-1)\binom nt.
$$
<convexity> then bounds the number of edges.

= Flow network
{parent=Graph theory}
{title2=$(G,c,s,t)$}
{wiki}

A <flow network> is a <directed graph> $G=(V,E)$ with distinct source and sink <vertices> $s,t$ and a nonnegative <flow network edge capacity> $c_{uv}$ on each directed <edge> $(u,v)$. In the finite setting here, every <flow network edge capacity> is a finite real number. A <feasible flow> assigns a nonnegative amount $f_{uv}$ to each original directed <edge>, satisfies the <capacity constraints>, and obeys <flow conservation> at every other <vertex>:
$$
0\leq f_{uv}\leq c_{uv},\qquad
\sum_{u:(u,v)\in E}f_{uv}=\sum_{w:(v,w)\in E}f_{vw}\quad(v\notin\{s,t\}).
$$
These are nonnegative arc amounts. The antisymmetric convention in <flow> instead records net amounts $\theta_{uv}=f_{uv}-f_{vu}$, taking a missing arc amount as zero. The two conventions express the same <flow conservation>; keeping them distinct makes backward <residual network> <edges> easier to interpret.

Think of <vertices> as routers, directed <edges> as communication links, <flow network edge capacities> as available bandwidth, and <flow> as a steady transmission rate. An intermediate router can distribute incoming traffic among outgoing links, but <flow conservation> prevents it from creating traffic. The <maximum flow problem> asks how much total traffic can pass from $s$ to $t$, allowing several <graph paths> to share the work. The same <flow network> language models transportation and abstract resource allocation.https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/6-854j-advanced-algorithms-fall-2008/4064d889e5033a9915327a777d12b592_notes_flow.pdf[Goemans, Network flows, introduction and maximum flow formulation]{ref}

Our running <flow network> has <vertices> $V=\{s,a,b,t\}$ and exactly five directed <edges>, with <flow network edge capacities>
$$
(c_{sa},c_{sb},c_{ab},c_{at},c_{bt})=(3,2,1,2,3).
$$
The cross <edge> $a\to b$ lets traffic entering $a$ use spare capacity on $b\to t$. With this <edge> removed, the two separate <graph paths> can carry only $\min(3,2)+\min(2,3)=4$; including it will let the <maximum flow> reach $5$. Thus a link's usefulness depends on the rest of the <flow network>, rather than just its own <flow network edge capacity>.

= Circulation in a flow network
{parent=Flow network}
{title2=$\sum_jf_{ij}=\sum_jf_{ji}\text{ at every }i$}

= Network circulation
{synonym}

A <network circulation> is a <flow> with zero net supply at every vertex. A feasible residual <network circulation> changes one feasible flow into another with the same prescribed supplies. It decomposes into directed cycles, giving the negative-cycle optimality test for <minimum-cost flow>.

= Vertex capacity
{parent=Flow network}
{title2=$\sum_{u}f_{uv}\leq c_v$}

An internal vertex capacity bounds the total <flow> through that vertex, equivalently its total inflow or outflow by <flow conservation>. Replace the vertex by an input and output vertex, direct incoming edges to the input and outgoing edges from the output, and connect input to output with an edge of the vertex capacity. This converts the constraint into an ordinary <flow network edge capacity>.

= Maximum flow problem
{parent=Flow network}
{title2=$\max_f|f|$}
{wiki}

= Maximum flow
{synonym}

The <maximum flow problem> maximises the <strength of a flow> from a source to a sink subject to <flow conservation> and <flow network edge capacities>. For the nonnegative arc amounts used in <flow network>, its objective is net outflow from the source:
$$
|f|=\sum_{v:(s,v)\in E}f_{sv}-\sum_{u:(u,s)\in E}f_{us}
=\sum_{u:(u,t)\in E}f_{ut}-\sum_{v:(t,v)\in E}f_{tv}.
$$
The equality follows by summing <flow conservation> over all internal <vertices>. Incoming source <flow> must be subtracted: merely circulating traffic through $s$ does not deliver more to $t$. The <max-flow min-cut theorem> identifies the optimum with the least <cut capacity> of a <cut of a flow network>. A <feasible flow> and a <cut of a flow network> of equal value certify optimality without requiring a particular choice of <augmenting paths>.

For the five-<edge> <flow network> with <flow network edge capacities> $(3,2,1,2,3)$, take
$$
(f_{sa},f_{sb},f_{ab},f_{at},f_{bt})=(3,2,1,2,3).
$$
Every <capacity constraint> holds with equality. <Flow conservation> at $a$ is $3=1+2$, and at $b$ it is $2+1=3$. The <strength of a flow> is $|f|=3+2=5$, also $2+3=5$ entering $t$. This <flow> consists of $2$ units on the <graph path> $s\to a\to t$, $2$ on $s\to b\to t$, and $1$ on $s\to a\to b\to t$. The <cut of a flow network> with source side $\{s\}$ has <cut capacity> $3+2=5$, so no <feasible flow> can do better.

Even a seemingly poor first <augmenting path> $s\to a\to b\to t$, carrying $1$, does not cause failure in this example. It leaves <residual network> capacity $2$ along $s\to a\to t$ and $2$ along $s\to b\to t$. These two subsequent <augmenting paths> yield values $1$, $3$, and $5$, and produce exactly the <flow> above. Backward <residual network> <edges> become essential in the unit-capacity example under <residual network>, where this same first choice blocks every unused forward route.

Discrete assignment is another reason to study <maximum flow>. Put unit-capacity <edges> from $s$ to job <vertices>, from each job to eligible worker <vertices>, and from workers to $t$. An integral <flow> selects a <matching in a graph>: each job and worker participates at most once, and the <strength of a flow> counts assigned jobs. The <integral max-flow theorem> therefore turns maximum cardinality assignment into <maximum flow>. Minimizing assignment costs is the related <assignment problem>, modeled by <minimum-cost flow>.https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/6-854j-advanced-algorithms-fall-2008/4064d889e5033a9915327a777d12b592_notes_flow.pdf[Goemans, Network flows, assignment formulation]{ref}

= Sports elimination by maximum flow
{parent=Maximum flow problem}

For a league in which every remaining game gives exactly one win, first give the target team all its remaining wins, obtaining $W$. Make a node for each unordered pair of other teams, with source capacity equal to their remaining games. Route each game-node flow to one of its two teams, and cap the team's total additional wins at $W-w_i-1$ for a strict target victory. A negative cap immediately rules out success. Otherwise an integral <maximum flow> saturating every game-source arc specifies the winners of all games and proves feasibility. Each actual game is counted once; duplicating ordered pairs changes the problem.

= Treatment capacity as a sink arc
{parent=Maximum flow problem}

In a <flow network> for treatment facilities, the amount treated at a facility is represented by a directed arc from it to the sink, with that arc's capacity equal to its treatment limit. Untreated fluid may pass through the facility independently of how much is treated there. Thus the treatment limit is not a bound on total incoming flow. <Flow conservation> at a facility equates incoming flow with outgoing untreated flow plus its sink-arc flow.

= Server connectivity under component failures
{parent=Maximum flow problem}
{title2=$k_{\max}=\lambda-1$}

To find the smallest link failure set separating a client from every server, add a common sink with sufficiently large-capacity server arcs and give original links unit capacities in both directions. The <max-flow min-cut theorem> identifies the minimum failure count $\lambda$. Unit-capacity <vertex splitting> also charges intermediary node failures. The network tolerates fewer than $\lambda$ failures and fails for some set of $\lambda$ failures. This concerns connection to at least one server, not separate connectivity to each server.

= Edmonds–Karp algorithm
{c}
{parent=Maximum flow problem}
{wiki}

This <maximum flow> algorithm repeatedly augments along a shortest residual path found by <breadth-first search>. Residual distances from the source never decrease. If the same arc becomes saturated again after being restored by a reverse augmentation, the distance to its tail has increased by at least two. Thus each arc is critical only $O(|V|)$ times, yielding $O(|V||E|)$ augmentations. Each search costs $O(|E|)$, so the total time is $O(|V||E|^2)$. Termination with no residual source-sink path supplies a <max-flow min-cut theorem> certificate.

= Maximum flow with vertex capacities
{parent=Maximum flow problem}

The <maximum flow problem> with internal <vertex capacities> reduces to the usual edge-capacitated problem by splitting each constrained vertex into input and output copies. The connecting edge carries its entire through-flow. Feasible flows correspond under this construction, preserving the source-to-sink value; the <Ford-Fulkerson algorithm> and <max-flow min-cut theorem> therefore apply to the transformed network.

= Vertex splitting
{parent=Maximum flow with vertex capacities}

Replace a vertex $v$ by $v_{\rm in},v_{\rm out}$ and an arc between them carrying its vertex capacity. Incoming arcs enter $v_{\rm in}$ and outgoing arcs leave $v_{\rm out}$. A path must cross that internal arc to use the vertex. This converts node-capacitated <maximum flow> to an ordinary <flow network>. For undirected mixed-failure cuts, normalize the source side so $v_{\rm out}$ inside implies $v_{\rm in}$ inside; then each original link contributes at most one crossing orientation.

= Flow network edge capacity
{parent=Flow network}
{title2=$c_{ij}$}

A directed edge capacity is the upper bound $c_{ij}\geq0$ on the feasible nonnegative <flow> through that edge. In a <residual network>, its forward residual capacity is $c_{ij}-f_{ij}$ and its backward residual capacity is $f_{ij}$. Increasing every edge capacity need not increase the <maximum flow> by that same amount, because a <cut of a flow network> can contain several edges.

= Capacity constraint
{parent=Flow network}

If a directed edge $e$ has capacity $c(e)$, a feasible flow satisfies $0\leq f(e)\leq c(e)$. In an antisymmetric-flow convention, this is encoded together with the residual capacity of the reverse orientation.

= Cut of a flow network
{parent=Flow network}

An source-to-sink cut is a partition $(A,V\setminus A)$ with the source in $A$ and the sink outside $A$. Its capacity is the sum of the capacities of directed edges leaving $A$.

= Minimum cut
{parent=Cut of a flow network}

A <cut of a flow network> of minimum <cut capacity> among cuts separating the prescribed source and sink. The <max-flow min-cut theorem> identifies this capacity with the maximum possible <flow> value.

= Diagonal cut in a nearest-neighbour lattice flow
{parent=Minimum cut}
{title2=$c(S)=2m$}

On $\{0,\ldots,m\}^2$, give an adjacent edge capacity equal to the larger endpoint distance $|i_1-i_2|$ from the diagonal. The set $S=\{i_1<i_2\}$ has $2m$ outgoing edges: two at each interior diagonal vertex and one at each endpoint. Every such edge has capacity one. Thus any <flow> from above to below the diagonal is at most $2m$; a feasible path decomposition attaining this value certifies optimality by <weak duality>.

= Cut capacity
{parent=Cut of a flow network}
{title2=$c(S,S^c)=\sum_{u\in S,v\notin S}c_{uv}$}

The sum of arc capacities directed from the source side to the sink side of a <cut of a flow network>.

= Flow
{title2=$\theta$}
{parent=Flow network}

= Network flow
{synonym}

A flow is an antisymmetric function on directed edges whose <divergence of a flow> vanishes away from designated sources and sinks.

= Unit flow
{parent=Flow}
{title2=$\operatorname{div}\vartheta=\mathbf1_a-\mathbf1_b$}

A unit flow from $a$ to $b$ is an antisymmetric <flow> with net outward <strength of a flow> one at $a$, net inward strength one at $b$, and zero <divergence of a flow> elsewhere. For a unit flow to infinity, only the finite source has nonzero divergence.

= Divergence of a flow
{title2=$\operatorname{div}\theta$}
{parent=Flow}

The divergence of a flow at a vertex is its total outward flow. Flow conservation means that this divergence vanishes at every interior vertex.

= Flow conservation
{parent=Divergence of a flow}

Flow conservation requires total inflow to equal total outflow at every vertex other than the source and sink, equivalently $\operatorname{div}f=0$ there.

= Strength of a flow
{parent=Flow}

The strength of a source-to-sink flow is the net amount leaving the source, equivalently the net amount entering the sink.

= Energy of a flow
{title2=$\mathcal E(\theta)$}
{parent=Flow}

For unit edge resistances, the energy of a flow is
$$
\mathcal E(\theta)=\sum_e\theta(e)^2,
$$
where every unoriented edge is counted once.

= Finite-energy flow criterion for transience
{parent=Energy of a flow}

An infinite locally finite connected graph is a <transient graph> exactly when it supports a unit flow from a vertex to infinity with finite <energy of a flow>.

= Max-flow min-cut theorem
{parent=Flow network}
{title2=$\max_f|f|=\min_{S:s\in S,\,t\notin S}c(S,V\setminus S)$}
{wiki}

For a finite <flow network> with finite nonnegative real <flow network edge capacities>, the greatest <strength of a flow> of a <feasible flow> equals the least <cut capacity> of a source-to-sink <cut of a flow network>:
$$
\max_{f\text{ feasible}}|f|
=\min_{S\subseteq V:\,s\in S,\,t\notin S}
\sum_{(u,v)\in E:\,u\in S,\,v\notin S}c_{uv}.
$$
For a <maximum flow>, the <vertices> reachable from the source in the <residual network> define a <minimum cut> whose outgoing original <edges> are saturated and whose incoming original <edges> carry zero <flow>.https://courses.csail.mit.edu/6.854/21/Notes/n06-flow.html[Karger, MIT 6.854 Notes 6, maximum-flow optimality conditions]{ref}

Why is a <cut of a flow network> an upper bound? Sum <flow conservation> on its source side $S$. Internal <edge> contributions cancel, leaving
$$
|f|=\sum_{(u,v)\in E:\,u\in S,\,v\notin S}f_{uv}
-\sum_{(u,v)\in E:\,u\notin S,\,v\in S}f_{uv}
\leq\sum_{(u,v)\in E:\,u\in S,\,v\notin S}c_{uv}
=c(S,V\setminus S).
$$
Only directed <edges> leaving $S$ contribute to <cut capacity>. Directed <edges> entering $S$ are subtracted in net <flow>, so adding their capacities to the upper bound would lose the intended bottleneck. This inequality is <weak duality>; the theorem says the bound is attained, an instance of <strong duality>. Its beauty is that a routing plan and a separating boundary independently witness each other's optimality.

In the running <flow network>, the two internal <vertices> give four possible source sides. For $S=\{s\}$, the <cut capacity> is $c_{sa}+c_{sb}=3+2=5$. For $S=\{s,a\}$ it is $c_{sb}+c_{ab}+c_{at}=2+1+2=5$. For $S=\{s,b\}$ it is $c_{sa}+c_{bt}=3+3=6$; the incoming <edge> $a\to b$ contributes nothing to that <cut capacity>. Finally, $S=\{s,a,b\}$ gives $c_{at}+c_{bt}=2+3=5$. Thus the <minimum cut> has <cut capacity> $5$, matching the explicitly computed <maximum flow>.

\Image[max-flow-cut-certificate.png]
{title=Maximum flow of value five and two minimum-cut certificates}
{description=Labels show flow/capacity on each directed edge. The shaded source sides give cuts of capacities three plus two and two plus one plus two, both equal to the flow value.}

Ford and Fulkerson's 1956 paper opens with a railway-capacity question attributed there to T. Harris and proves a minimal-cut theorem as Theorem 1. Its original formulation uses <graph paths> carrying nonnegative amounts; the <residual reachability certificate for maximum flow> below gives the finite directed <flow network> proof used here.https://doi.org/10.4153/CJM-1956-045-5[Ford and Fulkerson, Maximal Flow Through a Network, Canadian Journal of Mathematics 8 (1956), pp. 399–404]{ref}

= Residual reachability certificate for maximum flow
{parent=Max-flow min-cut theorem}

In a finite <flow network>, a <maximum flow> exists because the <feasible flow> set contains the zero <flow> and is a <compact set>: the <capacity constraints> bound every coordinate, and they and <flow conservation> define a <closed set>. The <strength of a flow> is a <continuous function>, so the <extreme value theorem> gives an attained maximum. An <augmenting path> in the <residual network> would permit positive augmentation, contradicting maximality. The source-reachable <vertices> in the <residual network> therefore define a <cut of a flow network> whose outgoing original <edges> are saturated and incoming original <edges> carry zero <flow>. <Flow conservation> makes the <strength of a flow> equal to this <cut capacity>, proving the <max-flow min-cut theorem>.

Existence and <strong duality> for finite real <flow network edge capacities> do not imply finite termination for every choice of <augmenting paths> in the <Ford-Fulkerson algorithm>. With irrational capacities, some choices can continue forever. With integer capacities, <integrality of the Ford-Fulkerson algorithm> instead forces finite termination; rational capacities reduce to that case by a common denominator. Choosing shortest <augmenting paths> as in the <Edmonds–Karp algorithm> avoids dependence on capacity magnitudes.https://courses.csail.mit.edu/6.854/21/Notes/n06-flow.html[Karger, MIT 6.854 Notes 6, section 2.1, rational and real capacities]{ref}

= Uniform capacity perturbation of a flow network
{parent=Max-flow min-cut theorem}
{title2=$v(\varepsilon)=\min_S(c(S)+\varepsilon m(S))$}

= Uniform capacity perturbations of a flow network
{synonym}

If every directed edge capacity increases by $\varepsilon$, a <cut of a flow network> with $m(S)$ outgoing edges gains $\varepsilon m(S)$. By the <max-flow min-cut theorem>, the new <maximum flow> value is
$$
v(\varepsilon)=\min_{S:s\in S,\,t\notin S}\bigl(c(S)+\varepsilon m(S)\bigr).
$$
For a finite <flow network>, this is a nondecreasing concave piecewise-linear function, and its active cut can change as $\varepsilon$ varies. Two unit-capacity edge-disjoint two-edge source-to-sink paths gain $2\varepsilon$, refuting an invariably exact $\varepsilon$ increase. If there is a directed source-to-sink path, every cut has at least one outgoing edge and the increase is at least $\varepsilon$; without a path it can be zero.

= Integral max-flow theorem
{parent=Max-flow min-cut theorem}

A flow network with integer capacities has a maximum flow whose value on every edge is an integer. The augmenting-path algorithm proves this because every augmentation preserves integrality.

= Kőnig's theorem
{disambiguate=graph theory}
{c}
{parent=Max-flow min-cut theorem}
{wiki}

= Kőnig's theorem for bipartite matching
{c}
{synonym}

In a finite bipartite graph, the maximum size of a matching equals the minimum size of a vertex cover. It follows by representing matching as an integral flow and vertex covers as finite-capacity cuts.

= Residual network
{parent=Max-flow min-cut theorem}
{title2=$G_f$}

For a <feasible flow> $f$, each original directed <edge> $u\to v$ supplies a forward <residual network> <edge> of capacity $c_{uv}-f_{uv}$ and a backward one $v\to u$ of capacity $f_{uv}$; zero-capacity residual <edges> may be omitted. The forward <edge> permits an increase, while the backward <edge> permits cancellation of existing <flow>. In particular, backward residual capacity is the amount already sent, rather than unused original capacity. If both original orientations exist, keep their residual contributions separate, or add capacities for contributions with the same direction. An <augmenting path> uses these permitted changes while preserving <flow conservation> and the <capacity constraints>.

To see why cancellation matters, give all five directed <edges> $s\to a$, $s\to b$, $a\to b$, $a\to t$, $b\to t$ unit <flow network edge capacities>. Starting from zero <flow>, an <augmenting path> $s\to a\to b\to t$ carrying $1$ yields
$$
(f_{sa},f_{sb},f_{ab},f_{at},f_{bt})=(1,0,1,0,1),\qquad |f|=1.
$$
The remaining unused original <edges> are $s\to b$ and $a\to t$, which do not form a source-to-sink <graph path>. However, the backward residual <edge> $b\to a$ has capacity $f_{ab}=1$. Consequently the <residual network> contains the <augmenting path> $s\to b\to a\to t$, with residual capacities $1,1,1$.

Augment by $1$: increase $f_{sb}$ and $f_{at}$ from $0$ to $1$, and decrease $f_{ab}$ from $1$ to $0$. The result is
$$
(f_{sa},f_{sb},f_{ab},f_{at},f_{bt})=(1,1,0,1,1),\qquad |f|=2.
$$
Now <flow conservation> is $1=0+1$ at $a$ and $1+0=1$ at $b$. The final <flow> sends one unit along each of $s\to a\to t$ and $s\to b\to t$. The <cut of a flow network> $S=\{s\}$ has <cut capacity> $1+1=2$, proving this is a <maximum flow>. Traversing $b\to a$ in the <residual network> revised the earlier routing decision; it did not send actual traffic along a nonexistent original <edge>.

\Image[residual-flow-rerouting.png]
{title=Backward residual capacity reroutes the first unit of flow}
{description=Before: one unit follows s to a to b to t. Middle: the residual augmenting path s to b to a to t cancels flow on a to b. After: two units use the separate paths, and the middle original edge carries zero flow.}

= Residual maximum flow is the remaining optimality gap
{parent=Residual network}
{title2=$f_{\mathrm{res}}^*=f^*-f$}

For an undirected capacitated network, write the net edge flow as $y_{ij}=-y_{ji}$, and the directed residual capacity as $c_{ij}-y_{ij}$. On every source–sink cut, residual capacity is the original cut capacity minus the current net flow value $f$. Taking the minimum over cuts and applying the <max-flow min-cut theorem> to each network gives the displayed remaining-flow identity.

= Augmenting path
{parent=Residual network}
{title2=$\Delta=\min_{e\in P}c_f(e)>0$}

An <augmenting path> is a source-to-sink <graph path> $P$ of positive capacities in the <residual network>. Its bottleneck is $\Delta=\min_{e\in P}c_f(e)>0$. Add $\Delta$ to original <flow> on forward residual <edges> and subtract $\Delta$ on backward residual <edges>. Each internal <vertex> still satisfies <flow conservation>, and the bottleneck guarantees every <capacity constraint>. The resulting <feasible flow> has <strength of a flow> $|f|+\Delta$. In the unit-capacity <residual network> example, $\Delta=1$, and the subtraction on $a\to b$ is precisely what allows the value to rise from $1$ to $2$.

= Widest augmenting path
{parent=Augmenting path}
{title2=$\max_{P:s\leadsto t}\min_{e\in P}c_{\mathrm{res}}(e)$}

An <augmenting path> maximizing the possible increase in flow, namely its smallest <residual network> edge capacity. If the remaining maximum flow is $F>0$ and the original undirected network has $m$ edges, a path has bottleneck at least $F/m$. Otherwise the vertices reachable through arcs of capacity at least $F/m$ define a cut of capacity strictly less than $F$, contradicting the <max-flow min-cut theorem>. Only one directed arc per original crossing edge contributes to that cut.

= Geometric convergence of widest-path augmentation
{parent=Widest augmenting path}
{title2=$F_k\leq(1-1/m)^k f^*$}

Starting from zero flow, a <widest augmenting path> increases the current flow by at least $F_k/m$, where $F_k$ is its remaining optimality gap. Therefore $F_{k+1}\leq(1-1/m)F_k$. Integer capacities keep this gap integer, so a gap below one is zero. The method takes $O(m\log f^*)$ augmentations for $f^*\geq2$, or uniformly $O(m\log(1+f^*))$; zero maximum flow requires no augmentation.

= Ford-Fulkerson algorithm
{c}
{parent=Augmenting path}
{wiki}

The Ford-Fulkerson algorithm repeatedly augments a feasible flow along an <augmenting path>. When no such path remains, the source-reachable vertices in the <residual network> define a cut of capacity equal to the flow value, proving optimality by the <max-flow min-cut theorem>.

= Integrality of the Ford-Fulkerson algorithm
{c}
{parent=Ford-Fulkerson algorithm}

Starting from the zero flow with integer capacities, every residual capacity and every augmenting bottleneck is an integer. Each augmentation therefore preserves integer edge flows and raises the flow value by at least one. Since the value is bounded by the total capacity leaving the source, the algorithm terminates after finitely many augmentations with an integral maximum flow.

= Parametric maximum flow with one source capacity
{parent=Max-flow min-cut theorem}

If one edge leaving the source has capacity $x$, every cut capacity is affine in $x$ with coefficient zero or one. In the 2024 Cambridge Part IB example, cuts of capacities $x+5$ and $14$ are both sharp, giving
$$
\delta^*(x)=\min\{x+5,14\}.
$$

= Eulerian graph
{parent=Graph theory}
{c}
{wiki}

An <Eulerian graph> has an <Euler circuit>: a closed <trail> traversing every <edge> exactly once. Here the <graph> is a finite <undirected graph>, possibly a <multigraph> with <self-loops>. Parallel <edges> retain distinct identities, and each <self-loop> contributes two to its <vertex degree>. Repeated <vertices> are allowed. This models inspecting every road segment once, even when a junction must be revisited.

For a nonempty set of <edges>, the <Euler circuit criterion> requires a <connected graph> only on the <edge support of a graph>: <isolated vertices> impose no travel requirement. A single <vertex> with a <self-loop> and two <vertices> joined by two parallel <edges> are both <Eulerian graphs>. Neither requires three <vertices>. With no <edges> and at least one <vertex>, we allow the zero-length <trail> at that <vertex> as an <Euler circuit>; the <graph> with no <vertices> has no such <walk>.

The motivating <Seven Bridges of Königsberg> problem asks for an <Eulerian trail>, which need not return to its starting <vertex>. In <Leonhard Euler>'s <Solutio problematis ad geometriam situs pertinentis by Leonhard Euler>, §2 poses the bridge-crossing question and §§4–6 encode land regions by letters. The river's shape drops out: only the connections matter. The paper was written in 1735 and published in 1741, according to the Euler Archive; the journal volume carries the nominal year 1736.<Euler Archive, E53: Solutio problematis ad geometriam situs pertinentis>{ref}

= Edge support of a graph
{parent=Eulerian graph}
{title2=$G_+$}

For a finite undirected <multigraph> $G=(V,E)$, its <edge support of a graph> is the <subgraph> $G_+=(V_+,E)$ with $V_+=\{v\in V:\deg(v)>0\}$. It removes exactly the <isolated vertices>, retaining every <edge>, including <self-loops> and parallel <edges>. When $E\ne\varnothing$, connectivity of this <subgraph> is the relevant condition for an <Eulerian trail> or <Euler circuit>.

= Seven Bridges of Königsberg
{c}
{parent=Eulerian graph}
{wiki}

Model the four land regions by <vertices> $A,B,C,D$ and the seven bridges by distinct <edges>: two $AB$, two $AC$, and one each of $AD,BD,CD$. Their <vertex degrees> are $(5,3,3,3)$. <Leonhard Euler>'s paper records these connections in §6 and the obstruction in §9; §20 states the zero-or-two odd-region rule. Four odd <vertex degrees> exclude an <Eulerian trail>, even if returning to the starting <vertex> is optional.<Solutio problematis ad geometriam situs pertinentis by Leonhard Euler>[§§6, 9, 20]{ref}

= Eulerian trail
{c}
{parent=Eulerian graph}

= Eulerian path
{c}
{synonym}

An <Eulerian trail>, also called an <Eulerian path>, is a <trail in a graph> traversing every <edge> exactly once. The name <Eulerian path> does not require a <graph path>: <vertices> may repeat. A <Hamiltonian path> instead visits every <vertex> exactly once, and may omit <edges>. An <Eulerian trail> may omit <isolated vertices>, so these are different coverage requirements.

For a finite undirected <multigraph> with at least one <edge>, allowing <self-loops>, an <Eulerian trail> exists exactly when the <edge support of a graph> is a <connected graph> and there are zero or two <vertices> of odd <vertex degree>. In the two-odd-<vertex> case they must be the two distinct endpoints; in the zero-odd-<vertex> case every <Eulerian trail> is an <Euler circuit>. Count a <self-loop> twice in the <vertex degree> but once in the <trail>, and count each parallel <edge> separately.<Math 530: Graph Theory (Combinatorial Mathematics I), Spring 2023/Math 530 Spring 2023, Lecture 6: Multigraphs and digraphs>[pp. 1–3, Theorem 1.1.2 and the note about isolated vertices]{ref}

The <parity> obstruction is local: each intermediate visit pairs an arriving <edge> with a departing <edge>. Only the distinct endpoints can have an unpaired incidence. The <handshaking lemma>, $\sum_{v\in V}\deg(v)=2|E|$, also shows that the number of <vertices> with odd <vertex degree> is even. Sufficiency in the two-endpoint case reduces to the <Euler circuit criterion>: add one extra <edge> between the odd <vertices>, construct an <Euler circuit>, rotate its record to start with the extra <edge>, then delete that <edge>. The remaining <trail> has the required endpoints.

For the <Seven Bridges of Königsberg> <multigraph>, the <vertex degrees> at $A,B,C,D$ are $(5,3,3,3)$, so no <Eulerian trail> exists. Remove the unique <edge> $AD$: the <vertex degrees> become $(4,3,3,2)$, and a six-<edge> <Eulerian trail> runs from $B$ to $C$. Label the parallel <edges> $p_1,p_2$ between $A,B$ and $q_1,q_2$ between $A,C$; label $BD$ by $r$ and $CD$ by $s$. A complete record is $B,p_1,A,q_1,C,s,D,r,B,p_2,A,q_2,C$. It revisits $A,B,C$ while using every remaining <edge> once.

\Image[eulerian-degree-parity-multigraphs.png]
{title=Four odd <vertex degrees> forbid an <Eulerian trail>; two allow distinct endpoints; zero allow an <Euler circuit>}

= Euler circuit
{parent=Eulerian graph}
{c}

= Euler tour
{c}
{synonym}

An <Euler circuit>, also called an <Euler tour>, is an <Eulerian trail> whose final <vertex> equals its initial <vertex>. Its length is $|E|$. It can revisit other <vertices>, unlike a <Hamiltonian cycle>, which visits each <vertex> once before returning to the start. For a finite undirected <multigraph> with at least one <edge>, an <Euler circuit> exists exactly when the <edge support of a graph> is a <connected graph> and every <vertex degree> is even.

For example, let $p,q$ be distinct parallel <edges> joining $A,B$, let $\ell$ be a <self-loop> at $A$, and add an <isolated vertex> $Z$. Then $(\deg(A),\deg(B),\deg(Z))=(4,2,0)$, and $A,\ell,A,p,B,q,A$ is an <Euler tour>. The <self-loop> supplies one traversal and two incidences; $Z$ need not be visited. Replacing $p,q$ by a single <edge> changes both <vertex degrees> to odd numbers and removes the possibility of an <Euler circuit>. Thus collapsing parallel <edges> can change the answer.

Two <triangles in a graph> sharing one <vertex> $A$ give another instructive <Euler circuit>: $A,B,C,A,D,E,A$. The shared <vertex> has <vertex degree> four, the others have <vertex degree> two, and $A$ is revisited between the two closed <trails>. This is precisely the splice used by <Hierholzer's algorithm>.

= Euler circuit criterion
{parent=Eulerian graph}
{c}

Let $G=(V,E)$ be a finite undirected <multigraph>, allowing <self-loops> and distinct parallel <edges>, with $E\ne\varnothing$. Then
$$
G\text{ has an Euler circuit}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad G_+\text{ is connected and }\deg(v)\equiv0\pmod2\text{ for every }v\in V,
$$
where $G_+$ is the <edge support of a graph>, and <vertex degrees> count each <self-loop> twice. There is no lower bound of three on the number of <vertices>. If $E=\varnothing$, the zero-length convention gives an <Euler circuit> exactly when $V\ne\varnothing$.<Math 530: Graph Theory (Combinatorial Mathematics I), Spring 2023/Math 530 Spring 2023, Lecture 6: Multigraphs and digraphs>[p. 1, Theorem 1.1.2(a) and isolated-vertex note; p. 2, trivial trail convention]{ref}

The two requirements are independent: two separate <vertices>, each carrying a <self-loop>, have even <vertex degrees> but disconnected <edge support of a graph>, so no <Euler circuit> can cover both <edges>.

Necessity follows because a single <trail> cannot cover <edges> in different <connected components of a graph>, while a closed <trail> pairs all arriving and departing incidences, including those at its starting <vertex>. For sufficiency, extend a <trail> using unused <edges> until no extension is possible. Even <vertex degree> <parity> prevents it from stopping anywhere except its starting <vertex>: a different stopping <vertex> would have an odd number of used incidences and hence at least one unused incidence. The result is a closed <trail>.

If unused <edges> remain, connectivity of the <edge support of a graph> supplies a <vertex> on the current closed <trail> with an unused incident <edge>. Removing a closed <trail> leaves even unused <vertex degrees>, so the same argument constructs another closed <trail> from that <vertex>. Splice it into the existing record. The two <trails> use disjoint <edge> identities, so the splice repeats no <edge>. Each splice uses more <edges>; finiteness ensures termination with an <Euler circuit>. This is the constructive idea of <Hierholzer's algorithm>.

\Image[hierholzer-closed-trail-splicing.png]
{title=<Hierholzer's algorithm> inserts one closed <trail> into another at their shared <vertex>, using each of six <edges> exactly once}

In the drawing, arrows indicate the chosen traversal direction along undirected <edges>. The first closed <trail> uses $AB,BC,CA$ and the second uses $AD,DE,EA$. Insert the second at the final occurrence of $A$ in $A,B,C,A$, giving $A,B,C,A,D,E,A$. A reader can check consecutive endpoints, closure, and the six distinct <edges> directly. With <multigraphs>, perform the same check on <edge> identities, since endpoint pairs alone cannot distinguish parallel <edges>.

= Hierholzer's algorithm
{c}
{parent=Euler circuit criterion}

<Hierholzer's algorithm> constructs an <Euler circuit> by growing closed <trails> along unused <edges> and splicing them at shared <vertices>, as in the <Euler circuit criterion>. Each <edge> must have a distinct record. An implementation using <adjacency lists>, a <stack>, and a used flag for each <edge> runs in $O(|V|+|E|)$ time, including the connectivity and <vertex degree> checks. Store both incidences of a <self-loop>, while marking its single <edge> record used only once.

= Line graph
{parent=Graph theory}
{wiki}

The line graph $L(G)$ has one vertex for each edge of $G$, with adjacency when two original edges share an endpoint.

= Line graph of a regular graph is Eulerian
{parent=Line graph}

If $G$ is connected and $r$-regular, then $L(G)$ is connected and every one of its vertices has degree $2r-2$. The <Euler circuit criterion> therefore applies.

= Graph drawing
{parent=Graph theory}

A <graph drawing> represents <vertices> by distinct points in the <Euclidean plane> and <edges> by curves joining their endpoints. A crossing of <edges> is not an extra <vertex> unless we explicitly change the <graph> to make it one. Curves may bend: the <graph> records connections, not coordinates.

= Planar graph
{parent=Graph theory}
{wiki}

A <planar graph> admits at least one <graph drawing> in which <edges> meet only at common endpoints. This is a property of the abstract <graph>, not a requirement on every <graph drawing>. A <plane graph> specifies one such drawing.<Planar Graphs; Bipartite Matching by Albert R. Meyer (6.042J lecture 5f)>[Planar Graphs; Bipartite Matching, slides lec 5f.3 and 5f.12]{ref}

For example, the <complete graph> $K_4$ joins every pair of four <vertices>, giving six <edges>. Put its <vertices> at the corners of a square and its diagonals cross. Instead put $A,B,C$ at the corners of a <triangle> and $D$ inside it: the same six <edges> $AB,AC,BC,AD,BD,CD$ now meet only at their endpoints. The first <graph drawing> obscures planarity; the second proves it. In particular, its <crossing number> is zero.

\Image[complete-four-vertex-graph-crossing-and-plane-drawings.png]
{id=complete-four-vertex-graph-crossing-and-plane-drawings}
{title=The same complete graph on four vertices drawn with a crossing and with four faces}

The distinction matters whenever connections must fit on one surface without intersecting. A failed <graph drawing> may only need rearrangement, whereas <nonplanarity by edge counts> proves that every rearrangement fails.

= Kuratowski's theorem
{parent=Planar graph}
{c}
{wiki}

A finite <simple graph> is a <planar graph> if and only if it contains no <graph subdivision> of the <complete graph> $K_5$ or the <complete bipartite graph> $K_{3,3}$ as a <subgraph>; equivalently, neither is a <topological minor>. The easy direction follows by tracing each replacement <graph path> as one drawn <edge>. The converse asserts that every failure of planarity contains one of these two patterns.<Graph Theory by Reinhard Diestel/Planar Graphs (author-hosted chapter preview)>[§4.4, Theorem 4.4.6(iii), printed p. 111]{ref}

<Graph Theory by Reinhard Diestel> (Planar Graphs preview, §4.4, Theorem 4.4.6, printed p. 111) dates <Kuratowski's theorem> to 1930 and the equivalent <graph minor> characterization to 1937. These dates are that textbook's historical attribution.

= Plane graph
{parent=Planar graph}

A <plane graph> is a <planar graph> with a specified <graph drawing> whose <edges> meet only at common endpoints, also called an embedding in the <Euclidean plane>. Its <faces of a plane graph> and <facial boundary walks> refer to that embedding. This geometric use of embedding differs from a <graph embedding>, which maps one abstract <graph> into another.

= Face of a plane graph
{parent=Plane graph}

= Faces of a plane graph
{synonym}

A <face of a plane graph> is a <connected component> of the <Euclidean plane> after removing the drawn <vertices> and <edges>. Count the <outer face> too. This is the plane counterpart of a <face of a planar map> on the <sphere>.<Planar Graphs; Bipartite Matching by Albert R. Meyer (6.042J lecture 5f)>[Planar Graphs; Bipartite Matching, slide lec 5f.6, including the outer face]{ref}

= Outer face
{parent=Face of a plane graph}

The <outer face> is the unique unbounded <face of a plane graph> for a finite <plane graph>. It counts once even though it surrounds the whole <graph drawing>. A <tree> has only this <face of a plane graph>.

= Facial boundary walk
{parent=Face of a plane graph}

For a finite <connected graph> embedded as a <plane graph>, follow the <edges> around a <face of a plane graph> to obtain a <closed walk>. Count traversals, not distinct <edges>: a <bridge in a graph> occurs twice along one <facial boundary walk>. Each <edge> contributes two sides in total.<Mathematics for Computer Science by Eric Lehman and others>[Mathematics for Computer Science, §12.4, Lemma 12.4.1, printed p. 485]{ref}

For the three-<vertex> <graph path> $A-B-C$, the only <facial boundary walk> is $A,B,C,B,A$, of length four. Both <edges> are <bridges in a graph>; neither is a <graph cycle>. Thus a <facial boundary walk> need not be a <graph cycle>.

= Honeycomb lattice
{parent=Planar graph}

The honeycomb lattice is the periodic degree-three <planar graph> consisting of the vertices and edges of a regular hexagonal tiling. It has two vertex classes; every edge joins opposite classes. Its <planar dual graph> is the <triangular lattice>.

= Martini lattice
{parent=Honeycomb lattice}

The martini lattice is obtained from the <honeycomb lattice> by replacing every vertex in one of its two vertex classes by a triangle, attaching its three former incident bonds to the triangle's three vertices. It is a periodic degree-three <planar graph>, with triangular faces and nine-sided faces. Each decorated vertex and its incident bonds is a three-terminal cell with three internal triangle bonds and three terminal spokes. Its independent <bond percolation> threshold is $1/\sqrt2$, as follows from <three-terminal cell partition duality>.

= Four color theorem
{parent=Planar graph}
{wiki}

= Four colour theorem
{synonym}

Every finite <planar graph> has <chromatic number> at most four. This stronger theorem is distinct from the elementary <five colour theorem>; the latter's <Kempe chain> proof does not prove this assertion.

= Five color theorem
{parent=Planar graph}
{wiki}

= Five colour theorem
{synonym}

Every finite <planar graph> admits a proper vertex coloring with at most five colors. <Euler formula for a connected planar graph> supplies a vertex of degree at most five. Inductively color its deletion. The only obstruction is five differently colored neighbours in cyclic order. If opposite neighbours 1 and 3 are in different two-color components, interchange one component. If they are connected, planarity prevents opposite neighbours 2 and 4 from being connected in their two-color subgraph, and that interchange frees a color instead. This is a <Kempe chain> proof.

= Planar map
{parent=Planar graph}
{wiki}

A planar map is a <planar graph> together with a fixed embedding in the <sphere>, considered up to orientation-preserving homeomorphism. A rooted planar map has a distinguished oriented edge, which removes most embedding symmetries.

= Face of a planar map
{parent=Planar map}

A face of a planar map is a connected component of the complement of its embedded graph. Its degree is the number of edge incidences around its boundary, counted with multiplicity, and the sum of all face degrees is twice the number of edges.

= Rooted planar map
{parent=Planar map}

A rooted planar map is a <planar map> with a distinguished oriented edge called its root edge. Its tail is the root vertex, and the face on a chosen side of the edge may be designated as the root face.

= Planar quadrangulation
{parent=Planar map}

A planar quadrangulation is a <planar map> in which every face has degree four, with incidences counted with multiplicity. A pointed quadrangulation additionally has a distinguished vertex.

A <squaregraph> has separate conditions on bounded faces and interior vertex degrees; its outer face need not be quadrilateral.

= Trivial bijection between planar maps and quadrangulations
{parent=Planar quadrangulation}

The trivial bijection sends a rooted <planar map> with $n$ edges to a rooted <planar quadrangulation> with $n$ faces. Put one new vertex in each face and, in each corner, connect that face vertex to the incident original vertex. Each original edge then lies inside one quadrangular face; deleting the original edges gives the quadrangulation. The face bipartition recovers the original vertices and hence the inverse map.

= Planar dual graph
{parent=Planar graph}

A planar dual graph has one vertex in each face of a fixed planar embedding and one dual edge crossing each primal edge.

This is the planar-embedding case of a <dual graph>.

= Planar cluster-count identity
{parent=Planar dual graph}
{title2=$k(\bar\omega^*)=o(\omega)-|V|+k(\omega)+1$}

For a fixed finite plane graph, the complementary dual configuration opens a dual edge exactly when its primal edge is closed. Its components correspond to faces of the spanning open primal graph. The componentwise <Euler formula for a connected planar graph> gives the displayed identity.
