The outer face and isolated primal vertices are included; the identity also handles disconnected graphs, bridges and loops.

= Dual bond percolation
{parent=Planar dual graph}

In planar <bond percolation>, declare a dual edge open exactly when the primal edge it crosses is closed. Independent primal parameter $p$ then gives independent dual parameter $1-p$.

= Three-terminal cell partition duality
{parent=Dual bond percolation}

A planar finite network with three boundary terminals induces one of five connection partitions: all three connected, all three separate, or one connected pair and an isolated third terminal. Put three dual terminals on the intervening boundary arcs. Passing to the <planar dual graph> interchanges the all-connected and all-separate partitions and permutes the three pair partitions. If the network is symmetric under all terminal permutations, equality $P_3=P_0$ of the first two probabilities makes its whole partition law dual-invariant. Independent cells on a self-dual triangular terminal arrangement then give identical primal and dual coarse connection laws. Infinite connectivity is preserved because cells are uniformly finite. For a <martini lattice> cell with six independent bonds of common parameter $p$, $P_3=3p^5-2p^6$ and $P_3-P_0=(2p^2-1)(p^4-3p^3+2p^2+1)$. The unique zero in $[0,1]$ is $1/\sqrt2$.

= Dual contour bound for a finite planar cluster
{parent=Dual bond percolation}

In <bond percolation> on the square lattice, the outer boundary of a finite <percolation cluster>, after filling its holes, consists of closed bonds. Their crossing edges in the <planar dual graph> form a connected enclosing contour, and hence belong to a single <dual bond percolation> cluster $K$. If $|K|=m$, both its coordinate widths are at most $m-1$, so the enclosed primal cluster contains at most $m^2$ vertices. A contour enclosing the origin also places every vertex of $K$ within distance $m+1$ of the origin. An exponential volume tail for the subcritical <dual bond percolation> clusters thus gives a bound $Ce^{-c\sqrt n}$ for a finite primal cluster of size at least $n$.

= Planar duality for rectangle crossings
{parent=Planar dual graph}

For a rectangle in a planar lattice, exactly one of an open primal left-to-right crossing and a closed dual top-to-bottom crossing occurs.

= Exact self-dual rectangle crossing probability
{parent=Planar duality for rectangle crossings}
{title2=$\mathbb P_{1/2}(H_n)=1/2$}

For the lattice rectangle with <graph vertices> $\{0,\ldots,n\}\times\{0,\ldots,n-1\}$, let $H_n$ be an open left-to-right crossing. Its complementary event is a closed dual top-to-bottom crossing. The dual crossing rectangle has width $n-1$ and height $n$, so rotation and translation identify it with the original rectangle. Boundary <edges> along the starting and ending sides do not affect the crossing event. At $p=1/2$ the primal and dual <edge> states have identical laws, hence
$$
\mathbb P_{1/2}(H_n)=1-\mathbb P_{1/2}(H_n)=\frac12.
$$
The one-unit adjustment in the side lengths makes the symmetry exact rather than an informal assertion about a finite <graph vertex> square.

= Euler formula for a connected planar graph
{parent=Planar graph}
{c}
{title2=$n-m+f=2$}

For a finite <connected graph> with at least one <vertex>, embedded as a <plane graph>, let $n$ count <vertices>, $m$ count <edges>, and $f$ count <faces of a plane graph>, including the <outer face>. Then
$$
n-m+f=2.
$$
The hypotheses refer to an actual embedding; regions in a crossing-filled <graph drawing> do not supply its $f$.<Planar Graphs; Bipartite Matching by Albert R. Meyer (6.042J lecture 5f)>[Planar Graphs; Bipartite Matching, slides lec 5f.14–5f.15]{ref}

In the <plane graph> $K_4$ pictured above, three bounded <faces of a plane graph> and the <outer face> give $4-6+4=2$. A square <cycle graph> gives $4-4+2=2$, while the <graph path> $A-B-C$ gives $3-2+1=2$. Changing a crossing into a new <vertex> changes the <graph>: the square-and-diagonals drawing then has five <vertices>, eight <edges>, and five <faces of a plane graph>, giving $5-8+5=2$.

A proof outline deletes <edges> on <graph cycles>. Each deletion preserves the <connected graph> and merges two <faces of a plane graph>, leaving $n-m+f$ unchanged. Eventually a <tree> remains, with $m=n-1$ and $f=1$. Adding the point at infinity identifies the plane picture with a <sphere>; the value two agrees with its <Euler characteristic> and the <cell-count formula for Euler characteristic>.

= Small faces of a spherical polyhedral graph
{parent=Euler formula for a connected planar graph}
{title2=$F_3+F_4+F_5\ge4$}

For a connected cellular spherical <planar graph> with vertex degrees at least three and face sizes at least three, degree/face double counting and the <Euler formula for a connected planar graph> give $\sum_n(6-n)F_n=12+\sum_m(2m-6)V_m\ge12$. The left side is at most $3(F_3+F_4+F_5)$, proving the displayed bound. In particular a convex polyhedron has at least four faces of size at most five.

= Planar graph edge bound
{parent=Euler formula for a connected planar graph}
{title2=$m\leq3n-6$}

A finite <simple graph> that is a <connected graph> and a <planar graph>, with $n\geq3$ <vertices> and $m$ <edges>, satisfies $m\leq3n-6$. Choose a <plane graph>: each <facial boundary walk> has length at least three, so $3f\leq2m$. Substitute $f=2-n+m$ from the <Euler formula for a connected planar graph>.<Mathematics for Computer Science by Eric Lehman and others>[Mathematics for Computer Science, §12.4, Lemma 12.4.2 and Theorem 12.4.3, printed pp. 485–486]{ref}

The <complete graph> $K_4$ attains equality: $6=3\cdot4-6$. The bound also holds for a disconnected finite <simple graph> that is a <planar graph> with $n\geq3$: place its <connected components of a graph> in disjoint regions and join them with new <bridges in a graph>, then apply the connected bound.

= Bipartite planar graph edge bound
{parent=Planar graph edge bound}
{title2=$m\leq2n-4$}

If a finite <simple graph> is a <connected graph>, a <bipartite graph>, and a <planar graph>, with $n\geq3$ <vertices>, then $m\leq2n-4$. Every <facial boundary walk> has even length and length at least three, hence at least four. Thus $4f\leq2m$; substitute the <Euler formula for a connected planar graph>.<Mathematics for Computer Science by Eric Lehman and others>[Mathematics for Computer Science, §12.5, Lemma 12.5.2 and Theorem 12.5.3, printed pp. 486–487]{ref}

The square <cycle graph> attains equality: $4=2\cdot4-4$. The three-<vertex> <graph path> also attains equality: $2=2\cdot3-4$. Its repeated <bridges in a graph> illustrate why counting <facial boundary walk> length correctly matters. Neither bound applies unchanged to a <multigraph>: two <vertices> may support arbitrarily many parallel <edges> in a <plane graph>.

= Nonplanarity by edge counts
{parent=Planar graph edge bound}

The <complete graph> $K_5$ has $n=5$ and $m=\binom52=10$, exceeding the <planar graph edge bound> $3\cdot5-6=9$. Therefore it is not a <planar graph>. The <complete bipartite graph> $K_{3,3}$ has $n=6$ and $m=3\cdot3=9$. The general bound allows twelve <edges>, but the <bipartite planar graph edge bound> allows only eight, proving nonplanarity.

\Image[plane-graph-face-counts-and-edge-obstructions.png]
{id=plane-graph-face-counts-and-edge-obstructions}
{title=Actual plane-graph face counts compared with impossible face counts for the two complete-graph obstructions}

These are necessary bounds, not a complete planarity test. For an original counterexample, subdivide every <edge> of $K_{3,3}$ once. The resulting <simple graph> is a <connected graph> with $n=15$, $m=18$, and is <bipartite>: put all original <vertices> in one part and all nine new <vertices> in the other. Both inequalities hold, $18\leq39$ and $18\leq26$. Nevertheless, a crossing-free <graph drawing> would let us trace each two-<edge> <graph path> as one curve, producing an impossible embedding of $K_{3,3}$. The <graph subdivision> preserves this obstruction. <Kuratowski's theorem> supplies a stronger characterization than these counts.

= Planar girth edge bound
{parent=Euler formula for a connected planar graph}

If every face of a connected planar graph has size at least $g>2$, double-counting edge-face incidences and using Euler's formula gives
$$
e\leq\frac{g(n-2)}{g-2}.
$$

= Triangle-pentagon planar edge bound
{parent=Euler formula for a connected planar graph}

Let a connected bridgeless planar graph have $n\ge4$ vertices, $e$ edges, no four-cycle, and $t$ triangular faces. If no edge borders two triangular faces, then
$$
2e\ge3t+5(f-t),
\qquad e\ge3t,
$$
so $f\le8e/15$. The <Euler formula for a connected planar graph> then gives
$$
e\le\frac{15(n-2)}7.
$$

= Icosidodecahedral graph
{parent=Triangle-pentagon planar edge bound}

The icosidodecahedral graph is the skeleton of the <icosidodecahedron>. It has 30 vertices, 60 edges, 20 triangular faces, and 12 pentagonal faces. Every edge separates a triangle from a pentagon, so it attains equality in the <triangle-pentagon planar edge bound>.

= Crossing number
{parent=Graph theory}
{wiki}

The crossing number of a graph is the minimum number of edge-crossing pairs among its plane drawings. For a fixed drawing, deleting at most one edge per crossing pair leaves a planar graph.

= Crossing lemma
{parent=Crossing number}
{wiki}

If a simple graph has $n$ vertices and $e\geq4n$ edges, every plane drawing has at least
$$
\frac{e^3}{64n^2}
$$
crossings. Apply the linear planar bound to the random induced subgraph obtained by retaining vertices independently with probability $4n/e$.

= Crossing lemma for multigraphs
{parent=Crossing lemma}
{title2=$e\geq4\mu v\Longrightarrow\operatorname{cr}(G)\geq e^3/(64\mu v^2)$}

Here a loopless <multigraph> has $v$ <vertices>, $e$ <edges>, and at most $\mu$ parallel <edges> per pair. In a minimum-crossing drawing, crossings between incident <edges> can be removed by exchanging their initial segments, so every crossing involves four distinct <vertices>. For each parallel class choose an <edge> with probability $1/\mu$ per <edge>, with the remaining probability choosing none. Retain <vertices> independently with probability $p$. The sampled <simple graph> has expected <edge> count $p^2e/\mu$, expected <vertex> count $pv$, and expected crossing count $p^4\operatorname{cr}(G)/\mu^2$. Deleting one <edge> per crossing and using the <planar graph edge bound> gives $p^4\operatorname{cr}(G)/\mu^2\geq p^2e/\mu-3pv$. Choose $p=4\mu v/e$.

= Graph neighbourhood
{title2=$N(S)$}
{parent=Graph theory}

= Neighbourhoods of a vertex
{synonym}

The neighbourhood $N(S)$ of a vertex set $S$ consists of all vertices adjacent to at least one vertex of $S$.

= Neighbour of a vertex
{parent=Graph neighbourhood}

= Graph neighbour
{synonym}

= Neighbours of a vertex
{synonym}

= Neighbour
{synonym}

A neighbour of a <vertex> $v$ in a <graph> is a <vertex> joined to $v$ by an <edge>. These <vertices> form the <graph neighbourhood> $N(v)$.

= Closed graph neighbourhood
{title2=$N[S]$}
{parent=Graph neighbourhood}

The closed neighbourhood of a vertex set is $N[S]=S\cup N(S)$.

= External vertex boundary
{title2=$\partial_vS$}
{parent=Graph neighbourhood}

= Vertex boundary
{synonym}

The external vertex boundary of a vertex set $S$ is
$$
\partial_vS=N(S)\setminus S=N[S]\setminus S.
$$
For comparisons at fixed $|S|$, minimizing $|\partial_vS|$ is equivalent to minimizing $|N[S]|$.

= Common neighbour
{parent=Graph neighbourhood}

A common neighbour of vertices $u$ and $v$ is adjacent to both of them.

= Degree
{disambiguate=graph theory}
{title2=$\deg(v)$}
{parent=Graph theory}
{wiki}

= Degree of a vertex
{synonym}

= Vertex degree
{synonym}

= Degrees of vertices
{synonym}

The degree of a vertex is the number of edges incident with it, equivalently the cardinality of its neighbourhood in a simple graph.

= Degree sequence
{parent=Degree (graph theory)}
{title2=$\mathbf d=(d_1,\ldots,d_n)$}

The degree sequence of a labelled <graph> records the <degree of a vertex> at each label. The <handshaking lemma> gives $\sum_i d_i=2|E|$. A nonincreasing list forgets the assignment of degrees to labels unless a particular ordering is specified.

= Outdegree
{parent=Degree (graph theory)}
{title2=$d^+(v)$}

The number of edges of a <directed graph> directed away from a given vertex. Assigning directions to undirected edges is an <orientation of a graph>.

= Average degree of a graph
{parent=Degree (graph theory)}
{title2=$\overline d(G)=2e(G)/|V(G)|$}

The average <vertex degree> of a <graph> is the sum of its vertex degrees divided by its order. <Double counting> edge endpoints gives $\overline d(G)=2e(G)/|V(G)|$. It lies between the <minimum degree of a graph> and the <maximum degree of a graph>.

= Regular graph
{parent=Degree (graph theory)}
{wiki}

A graph is $d$-regular when every vertex has degree $d$.

= Nested matching criterion for a regular subgraph
{parent=Regular graph}

Let $p$ be <prime> and set $s=2p-2$. If a <bipartite graph> has edge-disjoint <perfect matchings> $M_0,\ldots,M_{\sigma-1}$ on nested nonempty <vertex> sets, and $|V(M_{i+s})|>s|V(M_i)|/(s+1)$, then the union of $s+1$ consecutive <matchings in a graph> has maximum degree at most $2p-1$ and more than $(p-1)|V(M_i)|$ <edges>. The <prime-regular subgraph from Boolean polynomial constraints> gives a $p$-regular subgraph. Its <perfect matching> decomposition gives a $k$-regular subgraph for every $k\leq p$. If none exists, every $s$ steps shrink the <vertex> set by factor at most $s/(s+1)$, bounding $\sigma$ by a constant times $\log n$.

= Short-cycle expectation in a uniform regular graph
{parent=Regular graph}
{title2=$\mathbb EC_\ell\longrightarrow(r-1)^\ell/(2\ell)$}

For fixed $r,\ell\geq3$ and $rn$ even, a prescribed <graph cycle> in the <configuration model> has two ordered distinct half-edges at each of its vertices. Therefore its expected count is $(n)_\ell[r(r-1)]^\ell/[2\ell\prod_{j=0}^{\ell-1}(rn-2j-1)]$. Conditioning on the chosen cycle leaves degrees $r-2$ at its vertices and $r$ elsewhere; forbid further edges along that cycle. The <bounded-degree pairing avoidance estimate> changes by $o(1)$, so the simplicity probability conditional on the cycle divided by the unconditional probability tends to one. The displayed expectation therefore holds for the uniform <simple graph>, not just the unconditioned pairing model.

= Petersen graph
{c}
{parent=Regular graph}
{wiki}

The <graph> whose vertices are two-element subsets of a five-element set, with two vertices adjacent when the subsets are disjoint. It has ten vertices, degree three, zero common neighbours for adjacent pairs and one for nonadjacent pairs. Its <adjacency matrix of a graph> satisfies $A^2+A-2I=J$ and has <eigenvalues> $3,1,-2$ of multiplicities $1,5,4$.

= Constant eigenvector of a regular graph
{parent=Regular graph}

If $A$ is the adjacency matrix of a $d$-regular graph and $\mathbf e=(1,\ldots,1)^T$, every row sum of $A$ is $d$, so
$$
A\mathbf e=d\mathbf e.
$$
If the graph is connected, the $d$-eigenspace is exactly $\operatorname{span}\{\mathbf e\}$.

= Connected regular graph with two adjacency eigenvalues
{parent=Regular graph}

A connected regular graph has exactly two distinct adjacency eigenvalues if and only if it is a <complete graph> $K_n$ with $n\geq2$. Its eigenvalues are $n-1$ on the constant vectors and $-1$ on their orthogonal complement.

= Degree-biased vertex distribution
{parent=Degree (graph theory)}

In a finite graph with at least one edge, the degree-biased vertex distribution assigns probability proportional to the <degree of a vertex>. It is the distribution of an endpoint on a specified side of a uniformly random edge.

= Universal vertex
{parent=Degree (graph theory)}
{wiki}

A universal vertex is adjacent to every other vertex of its graph.

= Minimum degree of a graph
{title2=$\delta(G)$}
{parent=Degree (graph theory)}

= Minimum degree
{synonym}

The minimum degree $\delta(G)$ is the least degree of a vertex of $G$.

The minimum degree is $\delta(G)=\min_{v\in V(G)}\deg(v)$, the least <degree of a vertex> in the graph.

= Maximum degree of a graph
{title2=$\Delta(G)$}
{parent=Degree (graph theory)}

The maximum degree $\Delta(G)$ is the greatest degree of a vertex of $G$.

It is the maximum of the <degree of a vertex> over all vertices.

= Locally finite graph
{parent=Degree (graph theory)}
{wiki}

A graph is locally finite when every vertex has finite degree.

= Graph exhaustion
{parent=Locally finite graph}
{title2=$V_1\subseteq V_2\subseteq\cdots,\quad\bigcup_nV_n=V$}

An exhaustion of a countable <graph> is an increasing <sequence> of finite connected <graph vertex> sets whose union is the whole <graph vertex> set. In a <locally finite graph>, every prescribed finite <graph path> together with all <graph neighbours> of its visited <graph vertices> lies in a sufficiently large exhaustion set. This permits coupling finite-volume and infinite-volume <simple random walks> up to a finite stopping time.

= Cut
{disambiguate=graph theory}
{parent=Graph theory}
{wiki}

= Cut of a graph
{synonym}

A cut is a partition $V=A\sqcup B$ of the vertex set. Its size is the number of edges with one endpoint in each part.

= Edge cutset
{parent=Cut (graph theory)}

An edge cutset separating two vertices is a set of edges whose removal leaves no <path in a graph> between those vertices. For a vertex set containing exactly one of the two vertices, its <edge boundary> is such a cutset.

= Maximum cut
{parent=Cut (graph theory)}
{wiki}

A maximum cut has greatest size among all cuts of a finite graph.

= Random cut lower bound
{parent=Cut (graph theory)}

Every finite graph with $m$ edges has a <cut of a graph> of size at least $m/2$, and hence a spanning <bipartite graph> with at least $m/2$ edges. Put each vertex independently on either side with equal probability. Every edge crosses with probability $1/2$, so <linearity of expectation> gives expected cut size $m/2$.

= Balanced random cut lower bound
{parent=Random cut lower bound}

If a graph has even order $n$ and $m$ edges, it has a cut with equally sized parts and at least
$$
\frac{mn}{2(n-1)}
$$
crossing edges. Choose one part uniformly among the $n/2$-vertex subsets. A fixed edge is separated with probability
$$
2\frac{n/2}{n}\frac{n/2}{n-1}=\frac{n}{2(n-1)},
$$
and apply <linearity of expectation>.

= Unfriendly partition
{parent=Cut (graph theory)}
{wiki}

= Unfriendly partition of a graph
{synonym}

An unfriendly partition $V=A\sqcup B$ puts at least as many neighbours of every vertex in the opposite part as in its own part.

= Unfriendly partition theorem for a finite graph
{parent=Unfriendly partition}

Every finite graph has an unfriendly partition. In a maximum cut, moving any vertex to the other side cannot increase the number of crossing edges, which is exactly the required neighbour inequality.

= Unfriendly partition theorem for a countable locally finite graph
{parent=Unfriendly partition}

Take unfriendly partitions of an exhaustion by finite induced subgraphs and use a diagonal subsequence to stabilize the colour of each vertex. Local finiteness makes every neighbourhood stabilize after finitely many steps, so each unfriendly inequality passes to the limit.

= Random unfriendly partition of a countable infinite-degree graph
{parent=Unfriendly partition}

Independently colour the vertices of a countable graph red or blue with equal probabilities. If every vertex has infinite degree, then each fixed vertex has infinitely many neighbours of each colour almost surely. A countable union of the exceptional null events is null, so such a colouring exists.

= Bipartite graph
{parent=Graph theory}
{wiki}

= Bipartite
{synonym}

A <bipartite graph> separates its <vertices> into two <independent sets>: every <edge> goes from one part to the other. Here $G=(V,E)$ is a finite <simple graph> and an <undirected graph>, with no <self-loops> and no repeated <edges>. The two parts $X,Y\subseteq V$ satisfy
$$
X\cap Y=\varnothing,\qquad X\cup Y=V,\qquad \{u,v\}\in E\Longrightarrow (u\in X,\ v\in Y)\ \text{or}\ (u\in Y,\ v\in X).
$$
Thus neither part contains an <edge>. The parts need not have equal sizes, and some pairs across them may have no <edge>; requiring every cross pair gives a <complete bipartite graph>. The <independent set> and cross-<edge> definitions are equivalent.<Graph Theory by Reinhard Diestel/The Basics (author-hosted chapter preview)>[Section 1.1, printed p. 3, independent vertices; Section 1.6, printed p. 17, bipartite and complete multipartite graphs]{ref}

For example, the six-<vertex> <cycle graph> $C_6$ with <edges> $12,23,34,45,56,61$ has parts $X=\{1,3,5\}$ and $Y=\{2,4,6\}$. Drawing the same <graph> as a hexagon or in two columns changes neither its <edges> nor its <bipartite> property. An extra <isolated vertex> $z$ can go in either part.

\Image[bipartite-graph-independent-parts.png]
{id=bipartite-graph-independent-parts}
{title=The same six-cycle and isolated vertex drawn as a cycle and as two independent parts}

We allow either part to be empty. Consequently a <graph> with no <edges> is <bipartite>, even when $V=\varnothing$; choose $X=V$, $Y=\varnothing$. Here "two colours" means two colours are available, with no requirement to use both. This convention explicitly includes the empty case, whereas definitions requiring two nonempty classes need an exception. For several <connected components of a graph>, choose the two parts separately in each <graph component> and take their <unions>. In a nonempty <connected graph> that is <bipartite>, fixing one <vertex>'s colour forces all others along <graph paths>, so its two-colour assignment is unique up to swapping the colours. Each <graph component>, including an <isolated vertex>, can be swapped independently.

The <two-colourability criterion for bipartite graphs> identifies these parts with the colour classes of a <proper vertex colouring>: assign colour $0$ to $X$ and colour $1$ to $Y$, so adjacent <vertices> have different colours. More surprisingly, the <odd-cycle characterization of bipartite graphs> turns a global requirement into one precise obstruction:
$$
G\text{ is bipartite}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad G\text{ contains no odd cycle}.
$$
This statement is for the finite <undirected graph> and <simple graph> model above; a <graph cycle> has distinct <vertices> except for the repeated starting <vertex>. Along a <graph cycle> in a <bipartite graph>, colours alternate, so returning to the starting colour takes an <even number> of <edges>. Therefore any <odd cycle>, whether or not other <edges> join its <vertices>, prevents a <bipartite> split.<Graph Theory by Reinhard Diestel/The Basics (author-hosted chapter preview)>[Section 1.6, Proposition 1.6.1 and proof, printed pp. 17–18]{ref}

<Breadth-first search> supplies the converse constructively. Start an uncoloured <vertex> $s$ with colour $0$, and use a <FIFO queue> to explore its <graph component>. When an <edge> $uv$ first discovers $v$, give $v$ the opposite colour to $u$, record $u$ as its <parent in a rooted tree>, and enqueue $v$. If both endpoints of any scanned <edge> already have the same colour, stop with a conflict. Restart from an uncoloured <vertex> until every <graph component> has been examined. If no conflict occurs, all <edges> cross the two colour classes, which are the required <independent sets>.<Algorithms, 4th edition by Robert Sedgewick and others/BipartiteX.java>{ref}

A conflict also explains the failure. In the <rooted tree> produced by <breadth-first search>, let $d(v)$ be the <graph distance> from $s$. The colour of $v$ is its distance's <integer parity>. An <edge> $uv$ in an <undirected graph> satisfies $|d(u)-d(v)|\leq1$, so equal colours force $d(u)=d(v)$. Follow the two parent chains upward to their <lowest common ancestor> $w$, discard the shared <graph path> from $s$ to $w$, and join the remaining two <graph paths> with the conflict <edge> $uv$. The result is a genuine <graph cycle> with
$$
\bigl(d(u)-d(w)\bigr)+\bigl(d(v)-d(w)\bigr)+1=2\bigl(d(u)-d(w)\bigr)+1
$$
<edges>, an <odd integer>. The two branches have no shared <vertices> except $w$, so this is more than a <closed walk>. This <odd cycle> is a certificate that anyone can check directly, using only its listed <vertices> and <edges>.

\Image[bipartite-graph-odd-cycle-certificate.png]
{id=bipartite-graph-odd-cycle-certificate}
{title=A BFS colour conflict closes a five-cycle; the shared root path is excluded from the certificate}

In the example, <breadth-first search> from $s$ gives $d(w)=1$, $d(a)=d(b)=2$, and $d(u)=d(v)=3$. The conflict <edge> $uv$ closes the <odd cycle> $w,a,u,v,b,w$, with five <edges>. The common <edge> $sw$ is excluded. Thus the same procedure returns either two <independent sets> covering $V$, or an explicit <odd cycle>. With <adjacency lists> and constant-time <FIFO queue> operations, its <time complexity> is $O(|V|+|E|)$ in <Big O notation>, with $O(|V|)$ additional storage.<Algorithms, 4th edition by Robert Sedgewick and others/BipartiteX.java>{ref}

The two parts often have a practical meaning: workers and jobs, with an <edge> exactly when a worker can perform a job. A <matching in a graph> chooses compatible worker–job pairs with no reused endpoint; merely being <bipartite> does not guarantee enough compatible pairs. <Hall's marriage theorem> characterizes when every worker can be matched, and the <assignment problem> adds costs to seek an optimal assignment. These connections make the <bipartite> structure useful after recognition, as the starting point for selecting <edges> under constraints.

= Crown graph
{parent=Bipartite graph}
{wiki}
{title2=$K_{n,n}\setminus\{a_ib_i:1\leq i\leq n\}$}

A crown graph is a <complete bipartite graph> with a perfect matching removed. For $n\geq2$ it has <chromatic number> two, but <greedy coloring> in the order $a_1,b_1,a_2,b_2,\ldots,a_n,b_n$ uses $n$ colors: the matched pair gets the same new color, and every subsequent pair sees all earlier colors.

= Biadjacency matrix
{parent=Bipartite graph}
{title2=$M_{xy}=\mathbf1_{\{xy\in E\}}$}

For a <bipartite graph> with ordered parts $X,Y$, its biadjacency matrix has rows indexed by $X$ and columns indexed by $Y$, with entry one for an <edge> and zero otherwise. It has size $|X|$ by $|Y|$, rather than the square full <bipartite adjacency matrix> on $X\sqcup Y$, which is the block <matrix> $\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&M\\M^{\mathsf T}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)$. Its <singular values> describe the <normalized adjacency operator of a bipartite graph> after division by $\sqrt{|X||Y|}$.

= Normalized adjacency operator of a bipartite graph
{parent=Bipartite graph}
{title2=$(\theta_Gv)(x)=\mathbb E_yG(x,y)v(y)$}

Equip the function spaces of the two nonempty parts of a <bipartite graph> with uniform <inner products>. Its normalized adjacency operator is $(\theta_Gv)(x)=\mathbb E_yG(x,y)v(y)$. In orthonormal coordinate <bases>, its matrix is $G/\sqrt{|X||Y|}$, not the unscaled adjacency matrix. For a <biregular graph> of <edge density of a bipartite graph> $\gamma$, constant functions give a largest <singular value> $\gamma$, and the remaining <singular values> belong to the centered kernel $G-\gamma$.

= Biregular graph
{parent=Bipartite graph}

= Semiregular bipartite graph
{synonym}

A biregular graph is a <bipartite graph> whose vertices have constant degree on each part separately. The two degree constants can differ. If its parts are $X,Y$ and its <edge density of a bipartite graph> is $\gamma$, the two degrees are $\gamma|Y|$ and $\gamma|X|$. Thus its adjacency function has constant row and column averages $\gamma$.

= Independent-set bound for biregular graphs
{parent=Biregular graph}
{title2=$i(G)\leq(2^r+2^s-1)^{m/s}$}

For a <biregular graph> with $m$ left vertices of degree $r$ and $n$ right vertices of degree $s$, let $X$ be the indicator vector of a uniformly selected <independent set>. Use base-two <information entropy>. <Shearer inequality> gives $H(X_U)\leq r^{-1}\sum_{w\in W}H(X_{N(w)})$. Conditional on $X_U$, each right vertex with an empty selected neighbourhood is an independent fair binary choice, and the other right vertices are forbidden. Writing $q_w=\mathbb P(X_{N(w)}=0)$ gives $H(X)\leq r^{-1}\sum_w[H(X_{N(w)})+rq_w]$. For the $2^s$ neighbourhood patterns, assign weight $2^r$ to the zero pattern and weight one to all others. <Jensen's inequality> implies $H(Y)+rq_w\leq\log_2(2^r+2^s-1)$. Since $n/r=m/s$, the bound follows from $H(X)=\log_2i(G)$. If $s$ divides $m$, the disjoint union of $m/s$ copies of the <complete bipartite graph> $K_{s,r}$ attains equality: each component has $2^s+2^r-1$ <independent sets>.

= Quasirandom bipartite graph
{parent=Biregular graph}

For a <biregular graph> of <edge density of a bipartite graph> $\gamma$, small rectangular discrepancy, a <box norm> fourth power close to $\gamma^4$, and a small second <singular value> of the <normalized adjacency operator of a bipartite graph> are equivalent notions of quasirandomness, with quantitative bounds independent of the part sizes. Centering the adjacency function removes the constant singular component.

= Spectral discrepancy bound for a biregular graph
{parent=Quasirandom bipartite graph}
{title2=$|e(A,B)-\delta|A||B||\leq s\sqrt{|A||B|}$}

Let $H=M-\delta J$ be the centered <biadjacency matrix> of an equal-part <biregular graph>, and put $s=\|H\|_{\mathrm{op}}$. For subsets $A,B$ of the two parts, their edge discrepancy is $\mathbf1_A^{\mathsf T}H\mathbf1_B$. The <Cauchy-Schwarz inequality> proves the displayed bound. If the part size is $n$, centering the two <indicator vectors> improves it to $s n\sqrt{\alpha(1-\alpha)\beta(1-\beta)}$. The <centered four-cycle identity for a biregular graph> shows that $t_4(G)\leq\delta^4(1+c^4)$ implies $s\leq c\delta n$ for $c\geq0$.

= Centered four-cycle identity for a biregular graph
{parent=Quasirandom bipartite graph}
{title2=$t_4(G)=\delta^4+n^{-4}\operatorname{tr}((HH^{\mathsf T})^2)$}

For an equal-part <biregular graph> with part size $n$ and degree $\delta n$, let $M$ be its <biadjacency matrix> and $H=M-\delta J$, where $J$ is the all-ones <matrix>. The constant row and column sums make $H$ annihilate constants on both sides. Since $MM^{\mathsf T}=\delta^2nJ+HH^{\mathsf T}$ and the two summands have zero products, their squared <matrix trace> splits as displayed. The <bipartite four-cycle density> counts the squared <matrix trace> of $MM^{\mathsf T}$ divided by $n^4$.

= Half graph
{parent=Bipartite graph}
{wiki}

A half graph has two <vertex> classes $X=\{x_1,\ldots,x_n\}$ and $Y=\{y_1,\ldots,y_n\}$, with $x_i$ adjacent to $y_j$ exactly when $i\leq j$. Its nested <vertex neighbourhoods> give a useful obstruction to partitions in which every pair is a <regular pair of vertex sets>.

= Boundary rank obstruction in a half graph
{parent=Half graph}

Partition both sides of a <half graph> into classes of size at least $100$. A class of size $s$ has at most $2\lceil s/10\rceil$ elements with fewer than $s/10$ class elements on one side in the underlying order. If there are $k_X$ and $k_Y$ classes, the union of these boundary elements has size at most $2n/5+2(k_X+k_Y)\leq11n/25$. Some index $m$ is therefore away from both boundaries. The class containing $m$ on each side has at least one tenth of its points strictly above $m$ and strictly below $m$. Choosing lower $X$ points with upper $Y$ points, or upper $X$ points with lower $Y$ points, yields <edge density of a bipartite graph> values one and zero. Both cannot be within $1/10$ of the same class-pair <edge density of a bipartite graph>, so that pair is not a $1/10$-<regular pair of vertex sets>.

= Odd-cycle characterization of bipartite graphs
{parent=Bipartite graph}

A graph is <bipartite> if and only if it contains no <odd cycle>. One direction follows because every cycle alternates between the two vertex classes. For the converse, in each <connected component of a graph> choose a root and partition the vertices according to the parity of their <graph distance> from it. An edge joining equal parities would close an odd cycle, so every edge crosses the partition.

= Complete bipartite graph
{parent=Bipartite graph}
{wiki}

The complete bipartite graph $K_{r,s}$ has vertex classes of sizes $r$ and $s$ and contains all $rs$ edges between the classes.

= Common neighbourhood from bipartite density
{parent=Complete bipartite graph}
{title2=$|N(S)|\ge N(\beta/2)^s$}

If a <bipartite graph> with class sizes $m,N$ has at least $\beta mN$ <edges>, double count pairs consisting of an $s$-set in the first class and a common neighbour in the second. Convexity of binomial coefficients gives some $S$ with $|N(S)|\ge N\binom{\lfloor\beta m\rfloor}s/\binom ms$. When $s\le\beta m/2$, the quotient is at least $(\beta/2)^s$. This turns density into a large complete bipartite subgraph.

= Star
{disambiguate=graph theory}
{parent=Complete bipartite graph}
{title2=$K_{1,m}$}
{wiki}

= Star graph
{synonym}

= Graph star
{synonym}

A star graph is the <complete bipartite graph> with one centre and $m$ leaves. Any pair of its <edges> meet at the centre.

= Two-colourability criterion for bipartite graphs
{parent=Bipartite graph}

A finite graph is <bipartite graph>[bipartite] exactly when it has a proper colouring with two colours. Equivalently,
$$
P_G(2)>0
$$
for its <chromatic polynomial>.

= Graph homomorphism
{parent=Graph theory}
{wiki}

A graph homomorphism from $H$ to $G$ is a <function> $f:V(H)\to V(G)$ that sends every edge $uv$ of $H$ to an edge $f(u)f(v)$ of $G$.

= Graph embedding
{parent=Graph homomorphism}
{title2=$f:V(H)\hookrightarrow V(G)$}

A graph embedding in the subgraph sense is an <injective> <graph homomorphism>. It preserves edges, while it may send a nonedge to an edge. An induced embedding additionally preserves nonedges. For <directed graphs>, every prescribed arrow must be preserved.

= Homomorphism density
{title2=$t(H,G)$}
{parent=Graph homomorphism}
{wiki}

For finite graphs $H,G$, the homomorphism density $t(H,G)$ is the probability that a uniformly random map $V(H)\to V(G)$ is a <graph homomorphism>. For bipartite graphs with prescribed vertex classes, the random map is chosen separately and uniformly into the corresponding classes.

= Sidorenko conjecture
{c}
{parent=Homomorphism density}
{wiki}

The Sidorenko conjecture states that every <bipartite graph> $H$ satisfies
$$
t(H,G)\geq t(K_2,G)^{|E(H)|}
$$
for every bipartite host graph $G$. Thus a random map is at least as likely to preserve every edge as the heuristic that treats the edge constraints independently predicts.

= Sidorenko inequality for trees
{c}
{parent=Sidorenko conjecture}

Every <tree> $T$ satisfies the <Sidorenko conjecture>. If a bipartite host graph has edge density $\alpha$, then a uniformly random bipartition-respecting map from a $k$-vertex tree into the host is a <graph homomorphism> with probability at least $\alpha^{k-1}$.

= Directed graph
{parent=Graph theory}
{wiki}

A directed graph consists of <vertices of a graph>[vertices] joined by oriented edges.

= Kernel-perfect directed graph
{parent=Directed graph}

A <digraph kernel> is an <independent set> $K$ of <vertices> such that each vertex outside $K$ has a <directed edge> from itself to a vertex in $K$. A <directed graph> is kernel-perfect if every <induced subgraph> has a <digraph kernel>. Preference orientations of the <line graph> of a <bipartite graph> are kernel-perfect: a <stable matching in a bipartite graph> is independent in the line graph, and stability supplies the required outgoing edge at every unmatched edge.

= Kernel list-coloring lemma
{parent=Kernel-perfect directed graph}
{title2=$|L(v)|\ge d^+(v)+1$}

In a <kernel-perfect directed graph>, lists of size at least $d^+(v)+1$ admit a proper <graph coloring>. Choose a color $a$ and a <digraph kernel> in the subgraph on vertices whose lists contain $a$. Color that kernel $a$, delete it, and remove $a$ from the remaining lists. Every vertex losing a color also loses an outgoing neighbour; hence the inequality persists. Induction on the <vertex> count finishes the proof.

= Kernel of a directed graph
{parent=Directed graph}

= Digraph kernel
{synonym}

A digraph kernel is an <independent set> $K$ such that each <vertex> outside $K$ has a <directed edge> to a vertex in $K$. A directed cycle of even length has a kernel, obtained by taking alternate vertices; a directed triangle has none. The <kernel-perfect directed graph> property requires existence of such a set in every <induced subgraph>.

= Plünnecke graph
{c}
{parent=Directed graph}

A finite directed graph with layers $V_0,\ldots,V_n$ and edges only to the next layer is a Plünnecke graph when every edge $u\to v$ permits both commutation matchings. The successors of $v$ inject into the successors of $u$ through edges to the original successors; the predecessors of $u$ inject into those of $v$ through edges from the original predecessors. These conditions formalize interchanging adjacent summands in an additive graph. They persist on channels consisting of paths between prescribed endpoint sets.

= Weighted cut lemma for commutative graphs
{parent=Plünnecke graph}
{title2=$w(v)=C^{-i}\ (v\in V_i)$}

A minimum-weight set meeting every bottom-to-top path can be chosen entirely in the endpoint layers. The local step is a three-layer channel whose middle layer is a minimum cut. Replacing middle subsets by their successors or predecessors gives expansion inequalities. Sorting middle vertices by incoming degree and top vertices by their largest predecessor degree gives one edge-count inequality; reversing the graph gives the opposite inequality. Equality forces the bottom layer to have the same weight as the middle cut. Replacing the highest intermediate portion of a minimum cut by this bottom layer repeatedly proves the assertion.

= Graph magnification ratio
{parent=Plünnecke graph}
{title2=$D_k(G)=\min_{\varnothing\ne Z\subseteq V_0}|\operatorname{Im}^{(k)}Z|/|Z|$}

This is the smallest ratio of the size of a $k$-step image to the size of its nonempty starting set in the bottom layer. For a <Plünnecke graph>, the roots $D_k^{1/k}$ are nonincreasing. The minimum, rather than just the image of the whole layer, is essential.

= Coherent cyclic order of a digraph
{parent=Directed graph}

= Coherent cyclic order
{synonym}

A cyclic ordering of a <directed graph> is considered unchanged by rotations or swaps of consecutive nonadjacent vertices. A representative is coherent when every backward edge $v_j\to v_i$ has a forward <directed path> from $v_i$ to $v_j$. Equivalently every edge is on a directed circuit that winds exactly once around the order. The number of backward edges on a circuit is invariant under the allowed changes. Every nontrivial <strongly connected directed graph> admits such an order.

= Cyclic stability of a digraph order
{parent=Coherent cyclic order of a digraph}
{title2=$\alpha(\mathcal C)$}

= Cyclic stability
{synonym}

The cyclic stability is the largest size of an <independent set> appearing consecutively in a representative of a <coherent cyclic order of a digraph>. It is at most the ordinary <independence number>. A set with no forward path between its distinct vertices can be moved into a consecutive block by swapping nonadjacent vertices. Choose a representative minimizing the span of the set; a vertex in a gap can be moved left or right past the block, contradicting minimality unless there are no gaps.

= Tournament
{disambiguate=graph theory}
{parent=Directed graph}
{title2=$u\to v\ \text{or}\ v\to u$}
{wiki}

= Tournament
{synonym}

A tournament is an orientation of a <complete graph>: between each pair of distinct <vertices> there is exactly one <directed edge>.

= Median order of a tournament
{parent=Tournament (graph theory)}

= Median order
{synonym}

A median order maximizes the number of forward <directed edges> in a linear ordering of the <vertices> of a <tournament>. Every interval inherits a median order. Moving the first vertex of an interval to its end proves that it has at least as many outgoing as incoming edges in that interval; otherwise the move would increase the number of forward edges. Reversing all edges and the order gives the dual assertion for the last vertex.

= Half-sparse median-order embedding
{parent=Median order of a tournament}
{title2=$|f(T)\cap I|<|I|/2+1$}

= Half-sparse embedding
{synonym}

A <graph embedding> into an ordered <tournament> is half-sparse when the displayed inequality holds for every proper final interval $I$. Reserving two extra tournament vertices allows extension at any outward-oriented leaf: the <median order of a tournament> supplies at least half of the later vertices as outneighbours of the parent's image, while half-sparsity leaves at least one of them unused. Adding one image while extending every old final interval by two preserves the inequality.

= Regular tournament
{parent=Tournament (graph theory)}
{title2=$d^+(v)=d^-(v)=(N-1)/2$}

A regular <tournament> has equal <outdegree> and indegree at every vertex. On $2k+1$ vertices, label by residues modulo $2k+1$ and direct $i\to j$ when $j-i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}$. This gives an explicit regular tournament. It contains no outward-oriented star with more than $k$ leaves.

= Directed cycle
{parent=Directed graph}

= Directed circuit
{synonym}

A closed directed path whose vertices are distinct except for the common start and endpoint is a directed cycle. In a <residual network>, augmenting around a directed cycle preserves <flow balance>. A negative total cost on such a cycle gives an improving displacement for a <minimum-cost flow>.

= Spanning circuit family
{parent=Directed cycle}

A spanning circuit family is a collection of <directed cycles> whose union contains every vertex of the <directed graph>. Different circuits may overlap. This is distinct from a <cycle cover of a directed graph>, which requires vertex-disjoint cycles.

= Circuit-cover bound by independence number
{parent=Spanning circuit family}
{title2=$\#\text{circuits}\le\alpha(D)$}

For a nontrivial <strongly connected directed graph>, a <coherent cyclic order of a digraph> and the <Dilworth theorem> produce a <spanning circuit family> with at most its <independence number> circuits. Put a largest consecutive stable block first and duplicate its vertices as sinks. Use forward edges and edges into the sink copies to form an acyclic auxiliary graph. Its reachability order has width equal to the block size. Expand a chain partition to paths, then exchange intersecting path suffixes until the permutation of source-to-sink labels has as many cycles as possible. Paths in the same permutation cycle are disjoint, so identifying copied endpoints produces simple directed circuits. A windmill of directed triangles sharing one vertex shows that the bound is sharp.

= Rooted directed graph
{parent=Directed graph}
{title2=$(G,E,r_G)$}

A rooted directed graph has a distinguished root. Root-connectedness means each vertex can be reached by a finite directed path from that root. This is directed reachability, rather than connectedness of the underlying undirected graph.

= Bisimulation
{parent=Rooted directed graph}
{title2=$Z\subseteq G\times H$}
{wiki}

A bisimulation relates the roots of two <rooted directed graphs> and matches every successor move in either graph with a successor move in the other leading to related vertices. Multiple successors may be related to the same vertex. This local matching is weaker than graph isomorphism.

= Bisimulation game
{parent=Bisimulation}
{title2=$G_{\mathrm{bisim}}(G,H)$}

The Spoiler takes one successor step from the current vertex of either <rooted directed graph>; the Duplicator must take a matching successor step in the other. The Duplicator wins by never failing at a finite stage, including when the Spoiler cannot move. A winning strategy gives a <bisimulation> by collecting endpoint pairs of all finite plays consistent with it.

= Bisimilarity
{parent=Bisimulation}

= Bisimilar
{synonym}

Two <rooted directed graphs> are bisimilar if some <bisimulation> relates their roots. A root with one terminal successor is bisimilar to a root with two terminal successors, although the graphs have different sizes.

= Skeleton of a directed graph
{parent=Directed graph}

The skeleton of a <directed graph> is the <undirected graph> obtained by replacing every directed edge with an undirected edge and forgetting its orientation. In causal structure learning, the <PC algorithm> first recovers this adjacency structure before learning orientations.

= Cycle cover of a directed graph
{parent=Directed graph}

= Cycle covers of a directed graph
{synonym}

A cycle cover of a finite directed graph is a collection of vertex-disjoint directed cycles containing every vertex exactly once. Cycle covers of the weighted directed graph with adjacency matrix $A$ are the terms in the <permanent of a matrix> $\operatorname{perm}A$.

This is a <vertex cycle cover> with the additional requirements of directed cycles and vertex disjointness.

= Directed edge
{parent=Directed graph}

A directed edge $u\to v$ has an initial vertex $u$ and a terminal vertex $v$.

= Directed walk
{parent=Directed graph}

A directed walk is a sequence of vertices in which each consecutive pair is joined by a <directed edge> in the direction traversed.

= Closed directed walk
{parent=Directed walk}

A directed walk is closed when its first and last vertices agree.

= Directed path
{parent=Directed graph}

A directed path follows every edge in its specified direction and visits no vertex more than once.

= Strong connectivity
{parent=Directed graph}

= Strongly connected directed graph
{synonym}

A directed graph is strongly connected when every vertex is reachable from every other vertex by a <directed path>.

= Adjacency matrix of a directed graph
{title2=$A$}
{parent=Directed graph}

The adjacency matrix of a finite directed graph has entry $A_{ij}$ equal to the number of directed edges from vertex $i$ to vertex $j$. The entry $(A^n)_{ij}$ counts length-$n$ directed walks from $i$ to $j$, so $\operatorname{tr}(A^n)$ counts pointed closed directed walks of length $n$.

= Adjacency matrix
{title2=$A(G)$}
{parent=Graph theory}
{wiki}

= Adjacency matrix of a graph
{synonym}

The adjacency matrix of a finite simple graph has entry $A_{uv}=1$ when vertices $u,v$ are adjacent and zero otherwise.

For a directed or weighted graph, entries instead record directed adjacency or edge weights.

= Walk count from powers of an adjacency matrix
{parent=Adjacency matrix}

For every nonnegative integer $k$, the entry $(A^k)_{uv}$ equals the number of length-$k$ walks from $u$ to $v$. This follows by induction, since matrix multiplication appends one adjacent vertex to each walk.

= Distance
{disambiguate=graph theory}
{title2=$d(u,v)$}
{parent=Graph theory}
{wiki}

= Graph distance
{synonym}

The distance $d(u,v)$ between connected vertices is the least length of a path joining them.

= Path metric
{title2=$\rho$}
{parent=Distance (graph theory)}
{wiki}

Given positive edge lengths $\ell(e)$, the path metric is the infimum of $\sum_{e\in\gamma}\ell(e)$ over paths $\gamma$ joining two vertices.

= Graph diameter
{title2=$d(G)$}
{parent=Distance (graph theory)}

The diameter of a connected finite graph is the maximum distance between two vertices.

Thus diameter is an extremal statistic of <graph distance>.

= Linear independence of adjacency powers up to the diameter
{parent=Graph diameter}

If a connected graph has adjacency matrix $A$ and diameter $d$, then
$$
I,A,\ldots,A^d
$$
are linearly independent. For the largest index $k$ with a nonzero coefficient, choose vertices at distance $k$; the corresponding entries of all lower powers vanish, while $(A^k)_{uv}>0$ by the <walk count from powers of an adjacency matrix>.

= Spectral graph theory
{parent=Graph theory}
{wiki}

<Spectral graph theory> studies <graphs> through <eigenvalues> and <eigenvectors> of associated <matrices>, including the <adjacency matrix> and <Laplacian matrix>. A <graph eigenvalue> usually refers to the adjacency-matrix spectrum unless another matrix is specified.

= Expander mixing lemma
{parent=Spectral graph theory}
{wiki}
{title2=$|e(S,T)-d|S||T|/n|\leq d\rho\sqrt{|S|(1-|S|/n)|T|(1-|T|/n)}$}

For a $d$-regular undirected <graph> whose normalized adjacency <eigenvalues> other than the constant <eigenvalue> have absolute value at most $\rho$, center the indicator vectors of $S,T$. Their constant parts give the expected term $d|S||T|/n$, and the <operator norm> on the mean-zero subspace bounds the remaining <inner product>. Here $e(S,T)=\mathbf1_S^TA\mathbf1_T$ counts oriented incidences, so an <edge> within $S\cap T$ is counted twice.

= Graph eigenvalue
{parent=Spectral graph theory}

The eigenvalues of a finite graph are the eigenvalues of its adjacency matrix.

= Bipartite adjacency matrix
{parent=Graph theory}

With the two vertex classes listed consecutively, a bipartite graph has adjacency matrix $\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&B\\B^T&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)$.

= Perfect matching from a nonzero determinant
{parent=Bipartite adjacency matrix}

= Nonzero determinant criterion for a perfect matching
{synonym}

If a square bipartite adjacency matrix $B$ has nonzero determinant, some product in its Leibniz expansion is nonzero. Its permutation selects one edge at each vertex and therefore gives a perfect matching.

= Matching
{disambiguate=graph theory}
{parent=Graph theory}
{wiki}

= Matching in a graph
{synonym}

= Matchings in a graph
{synonym}

A matching is a set of edges with no shared endpoints. It saturates a vertex set when every vertex in that set is incident to one of its edges.

= Stable matching in a bipartite graph
{parent=Matching (graph theory)}

= Stable matching
{synonym}

Give each <vertex> a strict ordering of its <neighbours of a vertex>, all preferred to being unmatched. A <matching in a graph> is stable if no unused <edge> has both endpoints preferring each other to their current partners, with an unmatched endpoint willing to accept any neighbour. The <Gale-Shapley algorithm> produces a stable matching even when the two parts have unequal sizes and some pairs are unacceptable.

= Mutual-worst edge in stable matchings
{parent=Stable matching in a bipartite graph}

If the endpoints of an <edge> rank one another last and one <stable matching in a bipartite graph> contains that edge, every stable matching contains it. In the symmetric difference of two stable matchings, preference comparisons propagate along alternating paths: if a vertex prefers its new partner, that partner must prefer its old partner to avoid blocking the old matching. An alternating path cannot end unmatched on the preferred side, so the component is an alternating cycle, on which the two parts prefer opposite matchings. A mutual-worst edge absent from the new matching would make both its endpoints prefer the new matching, contradicting this propagation.

= Gale-Shapley algorithm
{c}
{parent=Stable matching in a bipartite graph}

An unmatched vertex in the proposing part proposes to its best untried neighbour. The receiving vertex keeps its preferred proposal, rejecting any other. There are at most as many proposals as <edges>, so this terminates. A rejected proposer can never become preferable to the receiver's final partner, since the receiver's held partner only improves. An edge never proposed along cannot block either: its proposer finishes with a better partner or has exhausted all choices. Thus the final <matching in a graph> is stable.

= Near-perfect matching
{parent=Matching (graph theory)}

In a <graph> with $2n$ <vertices>, a near-perfect <matching in a graph> has $n-1$ <edges> and exactly two unmatched <vertices>.

= Matching generating function
{parent=Matching (graph theory)}
{title2=$Z_G(\lambda)=\sum_k m_k\lambda^k$}

The coefficient $m_k$ counts <matchings in a graph> with $k$ <edges>. For a <bipartite graph> with $n$ <vertices> on each side, $m_n$ counts <perfect matchings> and equals the <permanent of a matrix> of its bipartite adjacency <matrix>. Positive activity $\lambda$ makes $Z_G$ the normalizing constant of the <weighted matching Markov chain>.

= Annealing ratios for a matching generating function
{parent=Matching generating function}
{title2=$Z_G(b)/Z_G(a)=\mathbb E_{\pi_a}(b/a)^{|M|}$}

For $0<a\leq b$, expand the <expectation> under the <weighted matching Markov chain> stationary weights to obtain the displayed identity. If the largest <matching in a graph> has $n$ <edges> and $b/a\leq1+1/n$, the observable lies in $[1,e]$. Its sample mean has controlled error by the <Hoeffding inequality>. A product of $J$ ratios, each estimated to relative accuracy $O(\varepsilon/J)$, approximates the full ratio to relative accuracy $O(\varepsilon)$.

= Augmenting path in a matching
{parent=Matching (graph theory)}

An augmenting path alternates between edges outside and inside a <matching in a graph> and joins two unmatched endpoints. Flipping its edges increases the matching size by one. In an assignment equality graph it moves toward a <perfect matching> while preserving zero <reduced costs> on matched edges.

= Short augmenting paths in a dense bipartite graph
{parent=Augmenting path in a matching}
{title2=$\delta(G)>n/2\Longrightarrow\text{an augmenting path of length at most three}$}

The two sides have $n$ <vertices> each. Given a nonperfect <matching in a graph>, choose unmatched <vertices> $u,v$ on opposite sides. An unmatched <neighbour> of either supplies a one-edge <augmenting path in a matching>. Otherwise their <neighbours> are matched; the matched left <vertices> corresponding to $N(u)$ and the set $N(v)$ have total size greater than $n$, so they intersect. This supplies the alternating <path in a graph> $u,b,a,v$ with $ab$ in the <matching in a graph>. Repeated augmentation produces a <perfect matching>.

Choose such an augmentation deterministically. Its reverse is specified by at most four <vertices>, giving at most $2n^4$ preimages for a <matching in a graph> of the next size. Thus the <matching generating function> coefficients satisfy $m_k\leq2n^4m_{k+1}$.

= Perfect matching
{parent=Matching (graph theory)}
{wiki}

A <perfect matching> is a <matching in a graph> that covers every <vertex> exactly once. Its <edges> have no shared endpoints, and every <vertex> is an endpoint of one selected <edge>. It is useful when a collection must be divided into disjoint pairs with no element left over.

In a <bipartite graph> with equally many <vertices> on its two sides, a <perfect matching> can assign every person one allowed job and every job one person. The example under <permanent of a matrix> has three people and three jobs, with exactly three valid assignments. The blue <edges> below show those three <perfect matchings>; a crossing in the drawing is not an extra <vertex>.

\Image[permanent-perfect-matching-assignments.png]
{title=Three perfect matchings pair every person vertex with one job vertex along allowed edges}

Every selected <edge> covers two <vertices>, so a <graph> with an <odd integer> number of <vertices> has no <perfect matching>. An <even integer> number is necessary but does not guarantee existence: an <isolated vertex> cannot be covered. A <perfect matching> also need not be unique. For example, the <complete bipartite graph> $K_{2,2}$ has two <perfect matchings>, corresponding to the two ways of assigning two people to two jobs.

Counting all <perfect matchings> is a different question from finding one. For a zero-one <biadjacency matrix> of a balanced <bipartite graph>, the count is its <permanent of a matrix>. The <nonzero determinant criterion for a perfect matching> supplies an existence test, but cancellation means that a zero <determinant> does not rule one out.

= Flexible vertex in a balanced bipartite graph
{parent=Perfect matching}

A vertex on one side of a balanced <bipartite graph> is flexible if every incident edge lies in a <perfect matching>. The strict <Hall marriage theorem> inequalities for nonempty proper subsets imply flexibility of every vertex: after forcing an edge, removal of its ends loses at most one neighbour from each remaining set. If a perfect matching exists without the strict condition, choose a minimal nonempty tight set. Its induced balanced graph has the strict condition internally, and each internal matching extends using the original matching outside that set. Thus at least one flexible vertex always exists.

= Minimum-weight perfect matching
{parent=Perfect matching}
{title2=$\min_M\sum_{e\in M}c_e$}

A minimum-weight <perfect matching> pairs every vertex exactly once while minimizing total edge weight. General weighted <perfect matching> has polynomial-time algorithms. In the <Christofides algorithm>, it corrects precisely the odd-degree vertices of a <minimum spanning tree>. Alternating edges of a cyclic order on an even number of vertices give two <perfect matchings>; the cheaper one costs at most half that cycle's cost.

= One-factorization
{parent=Perfect matching}

= One-factorisation
{synonym}

A one-factorization partitions every edge of a graph into <perfect matchings>. Each matching uses every vertex exactly once. A <total of synthemes> is precisely a one-factorization of the complete graph on six points.

= Maximal matching
{parent=Matching (graph theory)}

A matching is maximal when no edge can be added to it, and maximum when no matching has more edges. Every maximum matching is maximal, and the unmatched vertices of a maximal matching form an independent set.

Maximality is a property of a <matching in a graph>, distinct from maximum cardinality.

= Matching number
{title2=$\nu(G)$}
{parent=Matching (graph theory)}

= Maximum matching number
{synonym}

The matching number $\nu(G)$ is the largest number of edges in a matching of $G$.

The matching number $\nu(G)$ is the maximum cardinality of a <matching in a graph>.

= Hall's marriage theorem
{parent=Matching (graph theory)}
{c}
{wiki}

= Hall marriage theorem
{c}
{synonym}

A bipartite graph has a matching saturating $X$ exactly when $|N(S)|\ge|S|$ for every $S\subseteq X$.

= Random bipartite matching from empty-rectangle exclusion
{parent=Hall's marriage theorem}
{title2=$P(\text{no perfect matching})\leq 3n^{2\lceil\sqrt n\rceil}(1-p)^{n/2}\longrightarrow0$}

In the equal-part random <bipartite graph> with fixed edge probability $0<p<1$, rule out empty rectangles of side $\lceil\sqrt n\rceil$ and vertices with at least $n-\lceil\sqrt n\rceil$ nonneighbors. A union bound gives the displayed asymptotic estimate. Small vertex sets satisfy Hall's condition by the degree bound; intermediate sets do so because their omitted-neighbor rectangle would contain a forbidden square; large sets meet every vertex in the other part. Thus <Hall's marriage theorem> gives a perfect matching asymptotically almost surely. This does not mean probability one at any finite size.

= Hall matching of maximal separated sets
{parent=Hall's marriage theorem}
{c}

Two maximum-<cardinality> $\varepsilon$-separated subsets $A,B$ of a <compact metric space> have a bijection that moves each point by less than $\varepsilon$. Join $a$ to $b$ when their distance is below $\varepsilon$. If $S\subseteq A$ had fewer than $|S|$ neighbours, replacing these neighbours in $B$ by $S$ would create a larger separated set. The <Hall marriage theorem> therefore supplies a <perfect matching>. This allows empirical averages on a compact <isometry group> to be made approximately invariant without assuming a prior <Haar measure> construction.

= Degree-weighted Hall condition
{parent=Hall's marriage theorem}
{c}

In a finite bipartite graph, suppose every left vertex has positive degree and each edge joins vertices with $d(x)\geq d(y)$ from left to right. Then $|N(A)|\geq\sum_{x\in A}\sum_{y\sim x}1/d(y)\geq|A|$, so a matching covers the left side.

= Measurable Hall theorem
{parent=Hall's marriage theorem}

= Hall theorem for Lebesgue measurable sets
{c}
{synonym}

For finitely many <Lebesgue measurable sets> $A_i\subseteq[0,1]$ and nonnegative demands $b_i$, pairwise disjoint measurable <subsets> $B_i\subseteq A_i$ with $\lambda(B_i)=b_i$ exist exactly when $\lambda(\bigcup_{i\in I}A_i)\geq\sum_{i\in I}b_i$ for every index <subset> $I$. Necessity is additivity and monotonicity of <Lebesgue measure>. For sufficiency, partition the <set union> into membership cells $E_J$, send flow from a source through demand vertices $i$ to cells with $i\in J$, then to a sink. Source capacities are $b_i$, cell capacities are $\lambda(E_J)$, and intermediate capacities are $D=\sum_i b_i$. Every <cut of a flow network> has capacity at least $D$ by the assumed inequalities. The <max-flow min-cut theorem> provides the allocations, and <divisibility of Lebesgue measure> turns each cell allocation into disjoint pieces. If $D=0$, all $B_i$ can simply be empty.

= Hall induction through a tight set
{parent=Hall's marriage theorem}
{c}

For Hall's theorem, either every proper nonempty set has one excess neighbour and one deletes an arbitrary edge's endpoints, or a tight set $S$ with $|N(S)|=|S|$ splits the problem into the induced graph on $S\cup N(S)$ and its complement.

= Regular bipartite graph has a perfect matching
{parent=Hall's marriage theorem}

In a $k$-regular bipartite graph, edge counting gives equal vertex-class sizes and $k|S|\leq k|N(S)|$. Hall's theorem therefore supplies a perfect matching.

= Regular-graph matching bound from unmatched vertices
{parent=Matching (graph theory)}

If a $k$-regular graph on $n$ vertices has a maximum matching of size $m$, its unmatched vertices are independent. Counting their incident edges gives $k(n-2m)\leq2(k-1)m$, and hence $m\geq kn/(4k-2)$.

= Disjoint union of triangles as a sharp matching example
{parent=Regular-graph matching bound from unmatched vertices}

The disjoint union of $r$ triangles is $2$-regular on $3r$ vertices and has matching number $r$, attaining the regular-graph lower bound $n/3$.

= Ramsey theorem
{c}
{parent=Graph theory}

Every sufficiently large graph contains either a prescribed clique or a prescribed independent set.

= Diagonal Ramsey number
{parent=Ramsey theorem}

The diagonal Ramsey number $R(t)$ is the least $n$ such that every red-blue edge-colouring of $K_n$ contains a monochromatic $K_t$.

= Binomial upper bound for a Ramsey number
{parent=Diagonal Ramsey number}

The off-diagonal recursion
$$
R(s,t)\leq R(s-1,t)+R(s,t-1)
$$
with $R(1,t)=R(s,1)=1$ gives
$$
R(s,t)\leq\binom{s+t-2}{s-1}.
$$
In particular, $R(t)\leq\binom{2t-2}{t-1}<2^{2t}$.

= Monochromatic-triangle counting formula
{parent=Diagonal Ramsey number}

In a red-blue <edge colouring> of $K_n$, let $d_v$ be the red degree of vertex $v$ and let $M$ be the number of <monochromatic> triangles. Every nonmonochromatic triangle has exactly two vertices at which its two incident edges have different colors, so
$$
M=\binom n3-\frac12\sum_v d_v(n-1-d_v).
$$
Since $d_v(n-1-d_v)\leq\lfloor(n-1)^2/4\rfloor$, this identity gives a lower bound for $M$.

= Graph Ramsey number
{parent=Ramsey theorem}

For a finite graph $G$, its two-colour Ramsey number $R(G)$ is the least $n$ such that every red-blue colouring of $K_n$ contains a monochromatic copy of $G$. It exists because a monochromatic clique on $|V(G)|$ vertices contains a copy of $G$.

= Triangle-free Ramsey lower bound by alteration
{parent=Graph Ramsey number}
{title2=$R(3,t)=\Omega((t/\log t)^{3/2})$}

Sampling $G(n,p)$ and deleting one vertex per triangle and per independent $t$-set gives $R(3,t)>n-\binom n3p^3-\binom nt(1-p)^{\binom t2}$. Choosing $n$ of order $(t/\log t)^{3/2}$ and $p$ of order $\log t/t$ makes the forbidden configurations inexpensive enough to prove the corresponding lower bound.

= Off-diagonal graph Ramsey number
{title2=$r(G,H)$}
{parent=Graph Ramsey number}

The Ramsey number $r(G,H)$ is the least $n$ such that every red-blue colouring of $K_n$ contains a red copy of $G$ or a blue copy of $H$.

= Clique-path Ramsey number
{title2=$r(K_s,P_t)$}
{parent=Off-diagonal graph Ramsey number}

If $P_t$ denotes a path of length $t$, then
$$
r(K_s,P_t)=(s-1)t+1
$$
for $s\geq2$ and $t\geq1$.

= Ramsey number of a star
{parent=Graph Ramsey number}
{c}

For the star $K_{1,t}$,
$$
R(K_{1,t})=
\begin{cases}
2t,&t\text{ odd},\\
2t-1,&t\text{ even}.
\end{cases}
$$
The parity distinction follows from the handshake lemma applied to a hypothetical $(t-1)$-regular colour class on $2t-1$ vertices.

= Paw graph
{parent=Graph Ramsey number}
{wiki}

The paw graph is a triangle with one pendant edge.

= Ramsey number of the paw graph
{parent=Paw graph}
{c}

The paw graph $H$ has $R(H)=7$. A colouring of $K_6$ with two disjoint red triangles and all cross-edges blue avoids a monochromatic paw. In $K_7$, a monochromatic triangle exists; avoiding a paw forces all its edges to the other four vertices into the opposite colour, which then forces those four internal edges into the triangle's colour and creates a paw.

= Uniform hypergraph Ramsey number
{title2=$R^{(r)}(k)$}
{parent=Ramsey theorem}

The diagonal $r$-uniform hypergraph Ramsey number $R^{(r)}(k)$ is the least $N$ such that every red-blue colouring of the $r$-element subsets of an $N$-element set has a monochromatic $k$-element set.

= Erdős-Hajnal bound for the three-edge hypergraph on four vertices
{c}
{parent=Uniform hypergraph Ramsey number}

Let $K_4^{(3)-}$ be the three-uniform hypergraph with three of the four possible edges on four vertices. Every $K_4^{(3)-}$-free three-uniform hypergraph on $N$ vertices has an independent set of size at least
$$
c\frac{\log N}{\log\log N}.
$$
Equivalently, $r(K_4^{(3)-},K_k^{(3)})\leq k^{Ck}$.

= Canonical Ramsey theorem
{c}
{parent=Ramsey theorem}

For every colouring of the $r$-element subsets of an infinite set, there is an infinite increasing sequence $X$ and a coordinate set $I\subseteq\{1,\ldots,r\}$ such that two increasing $r$-tuples from $X$ have the same colour exactly when they agree in every coordinate indexed by $I$.

= Partition regular equation
{parent=Ramsey theorem}

A homogeneous linear equation is partition regular when every finite colouring of the positive integers admits a solution whose coordinates all have one colour.

The equation has <partition regularity> when every finite colouring admits such a monochromatic solution.

= Dirac's theorem
{c}
{parent=Graph theory}
{wiki}

= Dirac theorem
{c}
{synonym}

Every graph on $n\ge3$ vertices with minimum degree at least $n/2$ has a Hamilton cycle.

= Longest-path rotation
{parent=Graph theory}

If an endpoint of a longest path is adjacent to an internal vertex, replacing the incident path edge by that chord produces another longest path with the same vertex set and a new endpoint. Every neighbour of every endpoint obtained this way must remain on the original path.

This rotation technique is used in arguments surrounding <Pósa's theorem> and other <Hamiltonian cycle> criteria.

= Neighbourhood bound for a square-free bipartite graph
{parent=Longest-path rotation}

Let $A$ be $k$ vertices of a bipartite graph, each of degree at least $k$. If the graph has no $4$-cycle, then $|N(A)|\geq2k-1$. Indeed, any pair in $A$ has at most one common neighbour, so
$$
\sum_{y\in N(A)}\binom{d_A(y)}2\leq\binom k2.
$$
If $|N(A)|\leq2k-2$, Cauchy--Schwarz and $\sum_y d_A(y)\geq k^2$ make the left side strictly larger than $\binom k2$, a contradiction.

= Strongly regular graph
{parent=Graph theory}
{wiki}

A strongly regular graph has constant degree and fixed common-neighbour counts for adjacent and nonadjacent vertex pairs.

= Triangle-free graphs with three common neighbours
{parent=Strongly regular graph}
{title2=$n=1+d(d+2)/3,\quad d\in\{1,3,21\}$}

For a finite simple graph with at least two vertices, no triangles, and exactly three common neighbours for every nonadjacent pair, the graph is connected. For adjacent vertices $u,v$, the bipartite graph between $N(u)\setminus\{v\}$ and $N(v)\setminus\{u\}$ has degree two at every vertex, forcing $d(u)=d(v)$. Thus the graph is regular. Counting second neighbours gives $3(n-d-1)=d(d-1)$. The nontrivial adjacency eigenvalues are $(-3\pm\sqrt{4d-3})/2$, and the larger-root multiplicity is $[n-1+d^2/\sqrt{4d-3}]/2$. Integrality forces $t=\sqrt{4d-3}$ to be an odd integer dividing $9$, yielding the stated degrees. The complete bipartite graph $K_{3,3}$ realizes degree three. The one-vertex graph is a vacuous exception with degree zero.

= Adjacency-matrix relation for a strongly regular graph
{parent=Strongly regular graph}

If a strongly regular graph on $n$ vertices has degree $d$, with $r$ common neighbours for adjacent vertices and $s$ for nonadjacent vertices, then its adjacency matrix satisfies
$$
A^2=(d-s)I+(r-s)A+sJ.
$$

= Three-eigenvalue characterization of a connected strongly regular graph
{parent=Strongly regular graph}

A connected regular graph is strongly regular exactly when its adjacency matrix has three distinct eigenvalues, apart from the complete graphs, which have two. The forward direction follows from the <adjacency-matrix relation for a strongly regular graph>; conversely, the quadratic polynomial vanishing on the two nonconstant eigenspaces is a scalar multiple of the <all-ones matrix>.

= Moser spindle
{c}
{parent=Graph theory}
{wiki}

The Moser spindle is a seven-vertex unit-distance graph with chromatic number four.

= Random graph
{parent=Graph theory}
{wiki}

A random graph is a graph sampled from a probability distribution on graphs.

= Configuration model
{parent=Random graph}
{wiki}

= Pairing model
{synonym}

For a prescribed <degree sequence> with even sum $M=\sum_i d_i$, attach $d_i$ labelled <half-edges> to <vertex> $i$ and choose a uniform <perfect matching> of the $M$ half-edges. Each pair becomes an <edge>, giving a <multigraph> that may have <self-loops> or parallel edges. The number of pairings is $M!/[2^{M/2}(M/2)!]$. Every <simple graph> with that degree sequence has exactly $\prod_i d_i!$ pairing representations, so conditioning on simplicity produces the uniform simple-graph model.

= Bounded-degree pairing avoidance estimate
{parent=Configuration model}
{title2=$\Pr(\text{simple and avoiding }G_0)=\exp(-\lambda/2-\lambda^2/4-\mu)+o(1)$}

For bounded <vertex degrees>, total degree $M\to\infty$, and a forbidden graph $G_0$ of bounded <maximum degree>, put $\lambda=M^{-1}\sum_i d_i(d_i-1)$ and $\mu=M^{-1}\sum_{ij\in E(G_0)}d_i d_j$. Count prescribed loop pairs, double-edge pairs, and forbidden-edge pairs in the <configuration model>. Their means are respectively $\lambda/2+o(1)$, $\lambda^2/4+o(1)$, and $\mu+o(1)$. A fixed collection of $t$ disjoint prescribed pairs occurs with probability $[(M-1)(M-3)\cdots(M-2t+1)]^{-1}$. Bounded degrees make intersecting bad configurations contribute $O(M^{-1})$ to each fixed <factorial moment>. Thus the total bad-pattern count has factorial moments $(\lambda/2+\lambda^2/4+\mu)^j+o(1)$. The <Bonferroni inequalities> and uniformly bounded means give the displayed zero-pattern probability, without requiring the parameters to converge.

= Half-edge of a graph
{parent=Configuration model}

= Half-edge
{synonym}

A half-edge records an incidence of an <edge> at a <vertex>. Two half-edges form each edge, including two incidences at the same vertex for a <self-loop>. In the <configuration model> these incidences are individually labelled before random pairing.

= Preferential attachment
{parent=Random graph}
{wiki}

In preferential attachment, new <edges> favour existing <vertices> in proportion to a prescribed attractiveness, often their current <degree of a vertex>. A complete model must specify initial conditions, <self-loop> conventions and the normalization at each attachment. These details matter for the <vertex degree> distribution of the earliest <vertices>.

= Linearized chord diagram model
{parent=Preferential attachment}
{title2=$G_n^{(1)}$}

= LCD model
{c}
{synonym}

Start with one <self-loop>, and at step $t$ add one <vertex> and one <edge>. An old <vertex> $i$ is chosen with <probability> $d_i(t-1)/(2t-1)$, and the new <vertex> is chosen with <probability> $1/(2t-1)$, producing a <self-loop>. <Self-loops> contribute two to the <degree of a vertex>. There are $n$ <edges> and total <vertex degree> $2n$ at time $n$. This exact normalization is distinct from treating the total <vertex degree> as the number of <edges>.

= First-vertex degree in the LCD model
{parent=Linearized chord diagram model}
{title2=$\Pr(d_1(n)\ge y\sqrt n)\to e^{-y^2/4}$}

In a <uniform linearized chord diagram>, $\Pr(\sqrt nR_1\ge x)=(1-x^2/n)^n\to e^{-x^2}$. Conditional on the right endpoints, $d_1(n)=2+\sum_{i=2}^n\mathbf1_{\{L_i\le R_1\}}$, with <independent> success <probabilities> $R_1/R_i$. A <Chernoff bound> gives $R_i=(1+o(1))\sqrt{i/n}$ uniformly for $i\ge n^{1/10}$. The early indicators are $o(\sqrt n)$, and the remaining conditional <mean> is $(2+o(1))nR_1$. Their conditional <variance> is $O_{\mathbb P}(\sqrt n)$. Hence $d_1(n)/\sqrt n-2\sqrt nR_1\to0$ in <probability>, proving the displayed tail limit. A <self-loop>'s contribution is included in the constant two.

= Uniform linearized chord diagram
{parent=Linearized chord diagram model}

Pair $2n$ linearly ordered endpoints uniformly. Each pair is a chord; its larger endpoint ends a <vertex> block, with all endpoints after the previous right endpoint merged into that <vertex>. Orient the chord from its right-endpoint block to its left-endpoint block. Removing the last endpoint and its mate leaves a uniform smaller pairing. Reinserting the mate into one of $2n-1$ equally likely gaps chooses an existing block proportionally to its endpoint count, or the final gap creates a <self-loop>. This proves equivalence with the <linearized chord diagram model>.

An equivalent continuous representation draws $n$ <independent> pairs of <independent> uniform points on $[0,1]$, orders the pairs by their maxima $R_1<\cdots<R_n$, and calls their minima $L_i$. The $R_i^2$ are <uniform order statistics>; conditional on all the $R_i$, the $L_i$ are <independent> uniform points on $[0,R_i]$.

= Random k-out graph
{parent=Random graph}
{title2=$G_{k\text{-out}}$}

Each <vertex> independently chooses $k$ distinct other <vertices> uniformly, and an undirected <edge> exists when either endpoint chooses the other. This has dependent undirected <edges> and is not a <binomial random graph>. Coupling the choices as the first $k$ entries of <independent> random permutations makes the <graphs> increasing in $k$.

= Connectivity of random k-out graphs
{parent=Random k-out graph}
{title2=$\Pr(G_{k\text{-out}}\text{ connected})\to1\quad(k\ge2)$}

For $k=2$, a disconnected <graph> has a component of size $3\le s\le n/2$. The <probability> that a fixed $s$-set has no crossing <edge> is at most $(s/n)^{2s}(1-s/n)^{2(n-s)}$. The <union bound> and $\binom ns\le(n/s)^s[n/(n-s)]^{n-s}$ bound the failure <probability> by $\sum_{s=3}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(s/n)^s=o(1)$. Split this sum at $\sqrt n$. The <random k-out graph> coupling extends the result to every fixed $k\ge2$.

= Rado graph
{c}
{parent=Random graph}
{title2=$G^{(2)}_{\mathrm{univ}}$}
{wiki}

= Countable random graph
{synonym}

The Rado graph is the unique countably infinite <graph> with the two-uniform <hypergraph extension property>. Equivalently, for any disjoint finite vertex sets $U,W$, some new vertex is adjacent to every member of $U$ and no member of $W$. The <back-and-forth method> proves uniqueness. Independent edge sampling on the <natural numbers> with any fixed $p\in(0,1)$ produces this graph <almost surely>. It contains both edges and nonedges and therefore is not a <complete graph>. Its first-order axioms constitute the <theory of the random graph>.

= Achlioptas process
{c}
{parent=Random graph}
{title2=$G_{m+1}=G_m\cup\{e_{m,\omega_m}\}$}

At each step offer a fixed number of independent uniform candidate <edges> on a fixed vertex set, and let a rule select one. The standard two-choice model offers two candidates. The rule may depend on the entire previously exposed history. Repeated already present <edges> make no change in the independent-candidate version; another usual version offers only absent <edges>. The <forced merging of large components> and <persistence of unsampled graph components> apply to both over a linear number of steps. Fixed choice is essential in the <continuity of fixed-choice percolation> argument.

= Continuity of fixed-choice percolation
{parent=Achlioptas process}

A fixed-choice <Achlioptas process> cannot have its <largest component of a graph> jump from $o(n)$ to a fixed positive fraction of $n$ within $o(n)$ steps, <with high probability>. The proof combines <forced merging of large components> and <persistence of unsampled graph components>. An assumed jump forces positive vertex mass in each band $[k,Dk)$ slightly before the jump: insufficient smaller components can feed the final large component, while enough components above $Dk$ would merge too early. Persistence carries a fixed positive amount of each band to a common time. Taking sufficiently many disjoint bands with $k=1,D,D^2,\ldots$ then counts more than $n$ <vertices>. The number of offered candidates is fixed throughout this argument. Riordan and Warnke establish this obstruction and broader continuity results in https://arxiv.org/abs/1102.5306[their original research paper].

= Persistence of unsampled graph components
{parent=Achlioptas process}
{title2=$N_{[k,Dk)}\geq\alpha n\Rightarrow N_{[k,Dk)}\geq\beta n\text{ later}$}

Fix positive $\alpha$, $D\geq2$ and $B>0$. Suppose a size band $[k,Dk)$ of an <Achlioptas process> contains at least $\alpha n$ <vertices> at time $m$. For every fixed $k$, at least $\beta n$ of those <vertices> remain in unchanged <graph components> throughout the next $T=\lceil Bn/k\rceil$ steps, <with high probability>, where one may take $\beta=\alpha e^{-16BD}/4$. This is a deliberately nonoptimal positive constant.

For independent uniform candidate <edges>, an initial <graph component> with $w<Dk$ <vertices> is untouched if none of the $2T$ candidate <edges> meets it. A uniform <edge> hits it with <probability> at most $3w/n$. For sufficiently large $n$, $(1-3w/n)^{2T}\geq e^{-16BD}$, so the <expected value> of the untouched vertex mass $Y$ is at least $\alpha e^{-16BD}n$. Replacing one row of two candidate <edges> can change $Y$ by at most $8Dk$, because only the initial <graph components> hit by an old or new endpoint can change status. The <McDiarmid inequality> therefore makes $Y\geq\alpha e^{-16BD}n/2$ except on an event of <probability> $e^{-c n/k}$, for a positive constant $c$ depending only on $\alpha,B,D$. An untouched <graph component> stays unchanged whichever offered <edge> is selected.

For candidates restricted to absent <edges>, generate each by an independent uniform proposal stream and reject already present <edges>. During any interval of $O(n)$ steps with $O(n)$ present <edges>, each proposal has rejection <probability> $O(1/n)$ conditional on the past. The number of rejections is at most $O(\log n)$ except on an event of <probability> smaller than any inverse power of $n$: if there were $r$ rejections among $2T+r$ proposals, a <union bound> over their positions bounds this by $\binom{2T+r}r(C/n)^r$. The first $2T$ proposals are independent and give the previous untouched-mass estimate. Extra proposals can touch at most $2r$ additional initial <graph components>, losing only $O(Dk\log n)=o(n)$ <vertices> for fixed $k$. This leaves the stated $\beta n$ bound.
